> Section 2 of 22 · Introduction · id `introduction` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=introduction)

<h1 id="reader-content-introduction">Introduction</h1>
<h2 id="reader-content-what-this-is">What this is</h2>
<p>The Czech Language Navigator is an overview of the key points important in the study of Czech as a second language. It is organized in a way that will be familiar to most students of language but it also contains sections that go beyond grammar.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-core-grammar-sections">Core grammar sections</h2>
<p>The first nine sections outline the core parts of speech in detail. These are followed by sections on Word Order, Pronunciation and Spelling. These sections focus on the things traditionally found in grammar books. They describe the how the different parts of speech are formed and how they are used in phrases and sentences.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-sections-on-usage">Sections on usage</h2>
<p>This is followed by a section that covers the different ways in which Czech expresses itself going beyond grammar. Things like idioms, common ways of speaking and various differences between Czech and English. These things often fall between grammar books and dictionaries and may be scattered across various textbooks. The Navigator tries to present them more systematically.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-sections-with-useful-background">Sections with useful background</h2>
<p>The next three sections provide useful background about Czech to learners and travelers. There is a section on the differences between standard and non-standard Czech. This is followed by a section of trivia related to Czech such as the history of the language that a student can use to enrich their understanding of the context in which the language is used.</p>
<p>The final section is an overview of the Czech grammatical system that is taught in Czech schools. Many Czechs will be familiar with the structure and terminology and conversations about the Czech language will be easier.</p>
<p>You can find out more information about the origins of this document in <strong>Acknowledgements and licencing</strong> at the end.</p>

<h1 id="reader-content-parts-of-speech-nouns-verbs-and-more">Parts of speech: Nouns, Verbs and More</h1>
<p>This section describes in detail the forms and functions of all the different parts of speech in Czech:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Nouns</p></li>
<li><p>Adjectives</p></li>
<li><p>Pronouns</p></li>
<li><p>Numerals</p></li>
<li><p>Verbs</p></li>

</ul>
<p>At the end, you will also find a chart on How and when Czech words change their forms and How parts of speech interact when combined. This is the longest part of the navigator because it is what most learners find challenging.</p>

> Section 4 of 22 · Parts of speech: Nouns, Verbs and More > Nouns · id `nouns` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=nouns)

<h1 id="reader-content-nouns">Nouns</h1>
<h2 id="reader-content-gender">Gender</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-what-is-gender">What is gender?</h3>
<p>All Czech nouns have what is called gender. The gender of the noun is most important when you need to decide on the form of numbers, pronouns, and adjectives describing it. The words describing or determing the noun must change their form to match the noun’s gender. It also happens with verbs in the past tense.</p>
<p>To match the noun’s gender, the other words have to either add an appropriate ending or changing an existing one. For example, <em>ten bílý dům byl</em> vs <em>ta bílá kniha byla -</em> that<sup>‑masculine</sup> white<sup>‑masculine</sup> house was<sup>‑feminine</sup> vs. that<sup>‑feminine</sup> white<sup>‑feminine</sup> book was<sup>‑feminine</sup>.</p>
<p>English only has natural gender (used for people and male and female animals) but nouns in most other European languages have gender. French, Spanish and German are some of the best known. In Czech, just like in these other languages, you for the most part have to learn which words belong to what gender by heart.</p>
<p>Czech has three genders (like German and unlike French or Spanish which only have two). They are <em>masculine</em>, <em>feminine</em>, and <em>neuter</em>. In some ways, you can think about gender as if, for Czechs every object was either a <em>man</em>, a <em>woman,</em> or a <em>child</em>. This doesn’t really have any real consequences, except, Czechs find it amusing when genders are mismatched.</p>
<p>For instance “juice, coke, wine” (<em>džus</em>, <em>kola</em>, <em>víno</em>) are all of a different gender (whereas in English they are all neuter). Thus, <em>dobrý džus, dobrá kola, dobré víno</em> mean respectively “good<sup>‑man</sup> juice, good<sup>‑woman</sup> coke, good<sup>‑child</sup> wine.” But Czechs find it funny when non-native speakers mix up the gender. <em>Ha, ha, ha,</em> dobrá džus (good<sup>‑woman</sup> juice), <em>imagine that</em>.</p>
<p>To make it easier to remember that gender mostly matters for the words that interact with the noun, in the charts in this collection, we use the gender forms for the word <em>this</em>: <strong>ten</strong>, <strong>ta</strong>, <strong>to</strong>. The sooner you start internalizing this connection, the better.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-summary-of-agreement-rules">Summary of agreement rules!</h3>

<td align="left">Masculine (TEN) nouns are further divided into <strong>animate</strong> and <strong>inanimate</strong>. This is reflected in plural agreement. In present day spoken Czech, the distinction is not always present but it is strictly required in the written form. In fact, it would even make sense to say that Czech has four genders.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-how-to-tell-the-gender-of-a-noun">How to tell the gender of a noun</h3>
<p>In Czech, you can tell the gender from the ending about 80% of the time. The general rule is: <em>-C = masculine</em>, -<em>a = feminine</em>, and <em>-o = neuter</em> (the exceptions are <em>feminines</em> ending in a soft consonant or <em>e</em>, and <em>neuters</em> ending in <em>e</em> or <em>í</em>; there are also a few <em>masculines</em> that end in <em>a</em> or rarely <em>e</em>).</p>
<p>Here are a few gender indicators to look out for.</p>
<p id="reader-content-there-are-some-which-are-almost-exception-proof"><strong>There are some which are almost exception proof:</strong></p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-4">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">b, g, k, h, ch, d, r, p, n, m, l</td>
<td align="left">=</td>
<td align="left">ten</td>
<td align="left">rohlík (roll); oběd (lunch );</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">a</td>
<td align="left">=</td>
<td align="left">ta</td>
<td align="left">Praha, voda, kola, houska</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">o</td>
<td align="left">=</td>
<td align="left">to</td>
<td align="left">město (city);</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">í</td>
<td align="left">=</td>
<td align="left">to</td>
<td align="left">nádraží (train station)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>There are only a handful of <em>ten</em> nouns with <em>a</em> for an ending: <em>chleba</em> (bread), <em>táta</em> (<em>dad</em>), and all -ist words like <em>komunista</em>, <em>cyklista</em>, <em>turista</em>. There is one <em>ta</em> noun with <em>í</em> in <em>paní</em> (Mrs.).</p>
<p id="reader-content-the-rest-are-shared-by-two-genders-about-5050"><strong>The rest are shared by two genders about 50/50:</strong></p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-5">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">e</td>
<td align="left">=</td>
<td align="left">ta/to</td>
<td align="left">restaurace, rýže</td>
<td align="left">kuře, moře</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">s, t, v, c, j, č, ž, š, ř</td>
<td align="left">=</td>
<td align="left">ten/ta</td>
<td align="left">čaj, muž</td>
<td align="left">kolej, noc, daň</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Czech <em>ten</em> nouns are divided into so called <em>hard</em> and <em>soft</em> (according to the consonant they end in). The <em>soft</em> consonants share their endings with feminine nouns while <em>hard</em> are limited to the masculine nouns.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-case">Case</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-what-is-case">What is case?</h3>
<p>The word <em>case</em> refers to a set of endings that are used to indicate how words relate to one another. For instance, who does what to whom. In Czech, it is expressed through different endings. Nouns, pronouns, numerals and adjectives all use different case ending to express things like being an object (receiver) or a subject (initiator), or belonging to something. All prepositions also require that a noun has a case ending. Sometimes, the case ending tells you about whether you are somewhere or you are going somewhere. Sometimes a case ending helps link nouns together, e.g. if something belongs to something.</p>
<p>In the English sentence, <em>Petr has a son</em> the relationship between Petr and his son is set by the fact that the word <em>Petr</em> precedes the verb and the word <em>son</em> follows it. In Czech, this doesn't mean much, because Czech word order is more flexible. The relatioship between Petr and his some is captured by the case endings: <em>Petr_ má syna</em>. Thanks to the endings, you can also say <em>Syna má Petr</em> and the meaning is the same.</p>
<p>All verbs require the words that follow them to change form (that is add an ending). Most of the time, it is the object, but often there are other roles such as recipient or part of something.</p>
<p>In traditional grammar, cases have names such as <em>accusative</em> or <em>dative</em> that hint at the function. The names of the cases are not important, however. What really matters, is how the case is used in the language. What’s more, Czechs will mostly not be familiar with these names, they know the cases by their number. You can find out more in the final section on <em>Grammar as Czechs Know It</em>.</p>
<p>The following sections will describe the functions of the various cases and all the different rules for changing the forms of nouns, and other words.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-what-are-the-functions-of-czech-cases">What are the functions of Czech cases?</h3>
<p>Czech has six cases that describe the role of the word in a sentence. In English, they are expressed either through word order or prepositions. For example:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-6">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">A <em>friend</em> wrote. He is a <em>friend</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong>subject</strong></td>
<td align="left">Kamarád napsal. Je kamarád.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">A friend wrote a <em>letter</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong>object</strong></td>
<td align="left">Kamarád napsal <em>dopis</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">A friend <em>of our family</em> wrote a letter.</td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>of</em> sth/sb</strong></td>
<td align="left">Kamarád <em>naší rodiny</em> napsal dopis.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">A friend of our family wrote a letter <em>to Jana</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>to</em> something /somebody</strong></td>
<td align="left">Kamarád naší rodiny napsal dopis <em>Janě</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">A friend of our family wrote a letter to Jana <em>in a car</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong>in/about/... sth/sb</strong></td>
<td align="left">Kamarád naší rodiny napsal dopis Janě <em>v autě</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">A friend of our family wrote a letter to Jana in a car <em>with a fountain pen</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong>with/by/through sth/sb</strong></td>
<td align="left">Kamarád naší rodiny napsal dopis Janě v autě <em>plnícím perem</em>.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>or</strong></p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-7">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">A <em>friend</em> is here. He is a <em>friend</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong>subject</strong></td>
<td align="left">Kamarád je tady. Je kamarád.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">I see a <em>friend</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong>object</strong></td>
<td align="left">Vidím kamaráda.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">This is a story of a <em>friend</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>of</em> sth/sb</strong></td>
<td align="left">To je příběh <em>kamaráda</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">I gave a book to a <em>friend.</em></td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>to</em> sth/sb</strong></td>
<td align="left">Dal jsem knihu <em>kamarádovi</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">This is a story about a <em>friend</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong>in/about/... sth/sb</strong></td>
<td align="left">To je příběh o <em>kamarádovi.</em></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">I will come with a <em>friend</em></td>
<td align="center"><strong>with/by/through sth/sb</strong></td>
<td align="left">Přijdu s <em>kamarádem</em>.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>or</strong></p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-8">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">A <em>book</em> is here. It is a <em>book</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong>subject</strong></td>
<td align="left"><em>Kniha</em> je tady. Je to <em>kniha</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">I have a <em>book</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong>object</strong></td>
<td align="left">Mám <em>knihu</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">A page of the <em>book</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>of</em> sth/sb</strong></td>
<td align="left">Strana <em>knihy</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">He admitted to the <em>book</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>to</em> something /somebody</strong></td>
<td align="left">Přiznal se k té <em>knize</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">He wrote it in the <em>book</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong>in/about/... sth/sb</strong></td>
<td align="left">Napsal to v té <em>knize</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">He hit him with the <em>book</em>.</td>
<td align="center"><strong>with/by/through sth/sb</strong></td>
<td align="left">Uhodil ho tou <em>knihou</em>.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The underlined words or endings have equivalent functions in both languages. All the cases, except the subject, can be used with prepositions. The 6<sup>th</sup> case <em>in/about ...</em> sth/sb is only used after a preposition.</p>
<p>You can ask for all the <em>italicized</em> words using question words <em>WHO?</em> and <em>WHAT?</em> <em>What</em> is it? <em>What</em> do you have? With <em>whom</em> will you come? In Czech, it is the same only there is a different form of <em>Who? What?</em> for each case. Therefore the cases are named by their Czech <em>Who? What?</em> questions, which is how Czech children learn them in school. The cases are also numbered. In English, they are known by their Latin names. You can see that summarized in the chart below. The cases are listed in the same order as in the examples above.</p>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> In most textbooks and grammar books you find 7 cases. But the seventh case is only used for addressing people, and does not express any relationship between things in the world. That’s why it is best to learn it separately. It is mostly confusing when listed with the other forms and that’s why it is left out of the charts and has its own section on addressing people.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-summary-of-czech-cases">Summary of Czech cases</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-9">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Kdo? Co?</td>
<td align="left">subject</td>
<td align="left">1st – první pád</td>
<td align="left">Nominative</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Koho? Co?</td>
<td align="left">object</td>
<td align="left">4th – čtvrtý pád</td>
<td align="left">Accusative</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Koho? Čeho?</td>
<td align="left"><em>of</em> sth/sb</td>
<td align="left">2nd – druhý pád</td>
<td align="left">Genitive</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Komu? Čemu?</td>
<td align="left"><em>to</em> something /somebody</td>
<td align="left">3rd – třetí pád</td>
<td align="left">Dative</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">(v/o) Kom? Čem?</td>
<td align="left">in/about/... sth/sb</td>
<td align="left">6 th – šestý pád</td>
<td align="left">Locative</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Kým? Čím?</td>
<td align="left">with/by/through sth/sb</td>
<td align="left">7th – sedmý pád</td>
<td align="left">Instrumental</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The traditional ordering of cases is <em>1. Nominative, 2. Genitive, 3. Dative, 4. Accusative, 5.</em> <em>Vocative</em> (see below)<em>, 6. Locative, 7. Instrumental</em>. But this is not based on neither logical progression nor frequency. The order used in this book is a compromise between logical progression and frequency with the three most frequent cases being first and the least frequent case last.</p>
<p>The following sections describe the form and function of each case in detail. They are followed by an comprehensive chart of the different case endings.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-xx-models">XX Models</h2>
<p>Different types of nouns and other words change their forms differently. Each group is named after a model word and it is also called a model (<em>vzor</em> in Czech). There are fifteen models of Czech nouns, including the more prominent sub-models. They are all listed in the chart below. Each model is given a common word to represent it. Different textbooks use different example words. Here we chose ones we hope are most useful for learners:</p>
<p><strong>TEN:</strong> <em>kamarád</em> (friend), <em>muž</em> (man), <em>čaj</em> (tea), <em>rohlík</em> (bread roll), <em>poradce</em> (advisor), <em>policista</em> (policeman)</p>
<p><strong>TA:</strong> <em>voda</em> (water), <em>rýže</em> (rice), <em>daň</em> (tax), <em>noc</em> (night)</p>
<p><strong>TO:</strong> <em>pivo</em> (beer), <em>kuře</em> (chicken), <em>nádraží</em> (station), <em>moře</em> (sea)</p>
<p>All models can have further submodels for some exceptions but we ignore these here. For example, <strong><em>rohlík</em></strong> has a common submodel <strong><em>les</em></strong> <em>(forest)</em> but we treat them as the same model. They only differ slightly in some cases.</p>
<p>Model and case are often used to mean the same thing. In fact, model describes which particular ending a word will take in a certain case. Once you know the gender, you can most often tell the model from the form in the <em>Kdo? Co?</em> case. But there are many words, where this is not possible (for example, feminine (TA) nouns ending in a consonant).</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-kamarád-and-muž-soft-and-hard-animate-masculines"><em>kamarád</em> and <em>muž</em> – soft and hard animate masculines</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-10">

<tr>

<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-d3f5e0559067b5ea55f3-r1c3" scope="col" align="left">muž</th>
</tr>
</thead>

<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-d3f5e0559067b5ea55f3-r2c1" scope="row" align="left"><strong>Kdo? Co?</strong> N</th>

<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-d3f5e0559067b5ea55f3-r2c3" align="left">muž-Ø</td>
</tr>

</tr>

</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-d3f5e0559067b5ea55f3-r7c1" scope="row" align="left"><strong>Kým? Čím?</strong> I</th>

</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-rohlík-and-čaj-soft-and-hard-inanimate-masculines"><em>rohlík</em> and <em>čaj</em> – soft and hard inanimate masculines</h3>
<h3 id="reader-content-voda-and-rýže-feminines-ending-in-a-vowel"><em>voda</em> and <em>rýže</em> – feminines ending in a vowel</h3>
<h3 id="reader-content-noc-and-daň---two-feminines-ending-in-a-consonant"><em>noc</em> and <em>daň</em> - two feminines ending in a consonant</h3>
<h3 id="reader-content-pivo">pivo</h3>
<h3 id="reader-content-nádraží-and-účetní-nouns-of-all-genders-that-look-like-adjectives"><em>nádraží</em> and <em>účetní</em> – nouns of all genders that look like adjectives</h3>
<h2 id="reader-content-kdo-co---nominative---1.-pád---1st-case">Kdo? Co? - Nominative - 1. pád - 1<sup>st</sup> case</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-function">Function</h3>
<ul>
<li><p>Words in this case most often function as the subject of a sentence.</p></li>
<li><p>This form is found in the dictionary</p></li>
<li><p>This is the form used when naming or pointing at things (your name is…, this is…)</p></li>
<li><p>It is the form used in comparisons using <em>than</em> (<em>než</em>)</p></li>
</ul>
<h3 id="reader-content-form">Form</h3>
<p>This is the basic form of all words. It is found in the dictionary. Gender can be best determined based on Kdo? Co? form in instances where it is possible. See gender.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>It is never found after a preposition.</p></li>
<li><p>In a neutral sentence, it is most often found before the verb but often comes after the verb when you want to stress the subject.</p></li>
</ul>

<p class="heading" id="reader-content-exceptions-in-the-plural">Exceptions in the plural</p>
<p>Nominative plural <em>TEN animate</em><strong>:</strong></p>
<p><em>Kamarád</em> nouns ending in <strong>-an</strong> may end in <strong>-i</strong> as well as <strong>-é</strong>.</p>

<tbody>

<h2 id="reader-content-koho-co---accusative---4.-pád---4th-case">Koho? Co? - Accusative - 4. pád - 4<sup>th</sup> case</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-function-1">Function</h3>
<ul>
<li><p><em>Koho? Co?</em> is the most important case of the Czech language but only the second most frequent</p></li>
<li><p>The Latin name suggests accusing. It describes the relationship of influence between two objects, people, or objects and people.</p></li>
<li><p>Its main function is the object of the sentence.</p></li>
<li><p>It is used with some prepositions (like <em>na</em>) to indicate directionality.</p></li>
</ul>
<h3 id="reader-content-form-1">Form</h3>

<p>(<em>Chleba</em> is an exception and doesn’t follow the model of <em>policista</em> and doesn’t change with this case.)</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-most-common-verbs">Most common verbs</h3>
<p>This case is used as the object with most verbs that take an object. Here are the most common ones:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>mít</strong> (have), <strong>chtít</strong> (want), <strong>znát</strong> (know), <strong>vidět</strong> (see), <strong>vzít</strong> (take), <strong>dostat</strong> (receive), <strong>koupit</strong> (buy)</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Reflexive verbs with <strong>se</strong> such as <strong>bát se</strong> (to be afraid, to fear) cannot be followed by this case because <strong>se</strong> already is already in the accusative. The exception is <strong>učit se</strong> (to study/learn) which can have an object in the accusative in modern Czech although historically it required a dative.</p>
<p>Reflexive verbs with <strong>si</strong> such as <strong>brát si</strong> (take), <strong>umýt si</strong> (wash [one’s hands]), <strong>koupit si</strong> (buy) can require an accusative.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-most-common-prepositions">Most common prepositions</h3>
<p>This case is used after some prepositions for locations. The meaning is then typically that of movement or direction. It is frequently used in contrast with the Locative (Kom? Čem?) which signifies static location.</p>
<p><strong>na</strong> – <em>na diskoték<strong>u</strong></em> (to the disco) vs. <em>na diskotéce</em> (at the disco)</p>
<p><strong>před</strong> (in front of)<strong>, za</strong> (behind)<strong>, nad</strong> (above)<strong>, pod</strong> (beneath) – always meaning in the direction</p>
<p><strong>o</strong> – typically used with Locative but takes Accusative in the meaning of “bet something”, i.e. <em>vsadit se o stovku</em> (bet a thousand)</p>
<p><strong>v –</strong> typically used with Locative but takes Accusative in the meaning of “believe in”, i.e. <em>věřit v boha</em> (believe in God), Days of the week also take Accusative after <em>v</em>.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-koho-čeho---genitive---2.-pád---2nd-case">Koho? Čeho? - Genitive - 2. pád - 2<sup>nd</sup> case</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-function-2">Function</h3>
<ul>
<li><p>The core meaning of this case is <strong>belonging</strong>. It is equivalent to the English <strong><em>of</em></strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>It is used with many prepositions: <em>z, ze</em> (from), <em>bez</em> (without), <em>do</em> ( to), <em>od</em> (from), <em>blízko</em> (close to), <em>vedle</em> ( next to), <em>během</em> ( during), <em>kromě</em> ( except, besides), …</p></li>
<li><p>The partitive genitive is used after units of weights and measures and after other words denoting quantity.</p></li>
<li><p>deset deka sýr<strong>a</strong>, litr mlék<strong>a</strong>, kilo pomeranč<strong>ů</strong>, půl hodin<strong>y</strong>, málo cukr<strong>u</strong>, hodně káv<strong>y</strong>, trochu mlék<strong>a</strong>,… Kolik šunk<strong>y</strong> chcete?</p></li>
<li><p>In the plural, this case is used for counting with numbers of <strong>5 and above</strong> (see section on Numerals).</p></li>
<li><p>Because of its use in counting, this is the most frequent case.</p></li>
</ul>
<h3 id="reader-content-form-2">Form</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-17">

















<thead>
<tr>
<th align="center"></th>
<th colspan="6" align="center">Ten (masculine)</th>
<th colspan="4" align="center">Ta ( feminine)</th>
<th colspan="4" align="center">To (neuter)</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="center"></td>
<td colspan="2" align="center">Animate</td>
<td colspan="2" align="center">Inanimate</td>
<td colspan="2" align="center"></td>
<td colspan="4" align="center"></td>
<td colspan="4" align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center">kamarád</td>
<td align="center">muž</td>
<td align="center">rohlík</td>
<td align="center">čaj</td>
<td align="center">poradce</td>
<td align="center">policista</td>
<td align="center">voda</td>
<td align="center">rýže</td>
<td align="center">daň</td>
<td align="center">noc</td>
<td align="center">pivo</td>
<td align="center">kuře</td>
<td align="center">nádraží</td>
<td align="center">moře</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center">Sg</td>
<td align="center">- a</td>
<td align="center">- e</td>
<td align="center">-u/a</td>
<td align="center">- e</td>
<td align="center"><strong>^</strong></td>
<td align="center">- y</td>
<td align="center">- y</td>
<td align="center"><strong>^</strong></td>
<td align="center">- ě</td>
<td align="center">- i</td>
<td align="center">- a</td>
<td align="center">-ete</td>
<td align="center"><strong>^</strong></td>
<td align="center"><strong>^</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center">Pl</td>
<td align="center">- ů</td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center">Ø</td>
<td align="center">- í</td>
<td align="center">- í</td>
<td align="center">- í</td>
<td align="center"><strong>Ø</strong></td>
<td align="center">- at</td>
<td align="center"><strong>^</strong></td>
<td align="center">- í</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p class="heading" id="reader-content-singular">Singular</p>
<p>The model of <em>rohlík</em> has two alternative endings. You have to memorize which words use which ending.</p>
<p><em>Rohlík</em> nouns end in <strong>-a</strong> mostly in months.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>leden – ledn<strong>a</strong>, únor – únor<strong>a</strong>, březen – březn<strong>a</strong>, duben – dubn<strong>a</strong>, květen – květn<strong>a</strong>, červen – červn<strong>a</strong> (The only regular month is listopad – listopad<strong>u</strong>) and in place names ending in <strong>‑ýn/‑ín</strong> or <strong>–ov</strong>: Londýn – Londýn<strong>a</strong>, Berlín – Berlín<strong>a</strong>, Krumlov – Krumlov<strong>a</strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><em>Rohlík</em> and <em>čaj</em> nouns ending in <strong>-ek, -ec, -en</strong> drop “e” before the ending</p>
<blockquote>
<p>hrn<strong>e</strong>k – hrnk<strong>u</strong>, prosin<strong>e</strong>c – prosinc<strong>e</strong>, břez<strong>e</strong>n - březn<strong>a</strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p class="heading" id="reader-content-plural">Plural</p>
<p><em>Rýže</em> nouns ending in <strong>-ice</strong>, <strong>-ile</strong> lose the final <strong>-e</strong> (<strong>Ø</strong>) as in</p>
<blockquote>
<p>ulic<strong>e</strong> – ulic, košil<strong>e</strong> – košil, Strakonic<strong>e</strong> – Strakonic, stanic<strong>e</strong> - stanic</p>
</blockquote>
<p><em>Voda</em> and <em>pivo</em> nouns drop their vowel endings ( <strong>Ø</strong> ) and therefore end in consonant clusters difficult to pronounce. To aid pronunciation <strong>-e-</strong> is inserted.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>kamarádka – kamarád<strong>e</strong>k, tužka – tuž<strong>e</strong>k, barva – bar<strong>e</strong>v, divadlo – divad<strong>e</strong>l</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Some nouns shorten their stem vowel.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>jm<strong>é</strong>no – jm<strong>e</strong>n, chv<strong>í</strong>le – chv<strong>i</strong>l, pen<strong>í</strong>ze - pen<strong>ě</strong>z</p>
</blockquote>
<h3 id="reader-content-most-common-verbs-1">Most common verbs</h3>
<p>This case is used as the object of several important verbs.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>zeptat/ptát se <em>někoho</em></strong> <strong>na něco</strong> (ask somebody about something)</p>
<p><strong>bát se <em>něčeho</em></strong> (to be afraid of something)</p>
</blockquote>
<h3 id="reader-content-most-common-prepositions-1">Most common prepositions</h3>
<p>This case is used with many directional prepositions.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>do</strong> (to, into)</p>
<p><strong>z</strong> (from inside)</p>
<p><strong>od</strong> (from edge of)</p>
<p><strong>u</strong> (by, at, near), <strong>blízko</strong> (near)</p>
</blockquote>
<h3 id="reader-content-typical-categories-of-words">Typical categories of words</h3>
<p>This case is most likely to be used with:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Foods when counting (pl)</p></li>
<li><p>Foods when measuring</p></li>
<li><p>Place names (from/to)</p></li>
</ul>
<h2 id="reader-content-komu-čemu---dative---3.-pád---3rd-case">Komu? Čemu? - Dative - 3. pád - 3<sup>rd</sup> case</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-function-3">Function</h3>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Indirect object</strong> (John gave the book <em>to Mary</em>)</p></li>
<li><p>With prepositions <em>k</em> (to, toward) and <em>naproti</em> (opposite).</p></li>
<li><p>The endings in singular are identical with those of <em>(o) Kom? Čem?</em> (Locative) <em>Case</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>This is a relatively less frequent case but often needed for giving.</p></li>
</ul>
<h3 id="reader-content-form-3">Form</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-18">

















<thead>
<tr>
<th align="center"></th>
<th colspan="6" align="center">Ten (masculine)</th>
<th colspan="4" align="center">Ta ( feminine)</th>
<th colspan="4" align="center">To (neuter)</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="center"></td>
<td colspan="2" align="center">Animate</td>
<td colspan="2" align="center">Inanimate</td>
<td colspan="2" align="center"></td>
<td colspan="4" align="center"></td>
<td colspan="4" align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center">kamarád</td>
<td align="center">muž</td>
<td align="center">rohlík</td>
<td align="center">čaj</td>
<td align="center">poradce</td>
<td align="center">policista</td>
<td align="center">voda</td>
<td align="center">rýže</td>
<td align="center">daň</td>
<td align="center">noc</td>
<td align="center">pivo</td>
<td align="center">kuře</td>
<td align="center">nádraží</td>
<td align="center">moře</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center">Sg</td>
<td align="center">-ovi, u</td>
<td align="center">-ovi, i</td>
<td align="center">-u</td>
<td align="center">-i</td>
<td align="center">-i</td>
<td align="center">-ovi</td>
<td align="center">-ě</td>
<td colspan="3" align="center">-i</td>
<td align="center">-u</td>
<td align="center">-eti</td>
<td align="center"><strong>^</strong></td>
<td align="center">-i</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center">Pl</td>
<td align="center">-ům</td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center">-ám</td>
<td colspan="3" align="center">-ím</td>
<td align="center">-ům</td>
<td align="center">-atům</td>
<td colspan="2" align="center">-ím</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>When the dative is applied to two or more consecutive <em>TEN</em> nouns, the last one uses a different ending. This is very common with names. The last noun ends in <strong><em>-ovi</em></strong> and all preceding nouns end in <strong>‑u<em>.</em></strong> For example:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>k</strong> Pavl<strong>ovi</strong>, pan<strong>u</strong> doktor<strong>ovi</strong>, pan<strong>u</strong> doktor<strong>u</strong> Havl<strong>ovi</strong>, pan<strong>u</strong> doktor<strong>u</strong> Pavl<strong>u</strong> Havl<strong>ovi</strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><em>TA</em> nouns change endings:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-19">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>banka</p></td>
<td><p><strong>></strong></p></td>
<td><p><strong>k</strong> bance</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>k <strong>></strong> c</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>sprcha</p></td>
<td><p><strong>></strong></p></td>
<td><p><strong>proti</strong> sprše</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>ch <strong>></strong> š</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>opera</p></td>
<td><p><strong>></strong></p></td>
<td><p><strong>k</strong> opeře</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>r <strong>></strong> ř</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>Praha</p>
<p>Olga</p></td>
<td><p><strong>></strong></p></td>
<td><p><strong>k</strong> Praze</p>
<p><strong>k</strong> Olze</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>h <strong>></strong> z</p>
<p>g <strong>></strong> z</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-most-common-verbs-2">Most common verbs</h3>
<p>This case is used as the second or indirect object of verbs. The first object is typically accusative.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>dát něco <em>někomu</em></strong> (give something [acc.] to somebody])</p>
<p><strong>volat, telefonovat <em>někomu</em></strong> (call, phone somebody)</p>
<p><strong>věřit <em>někomu</em></strong> (believe somebody)</p>
<p><strong>ublížit <em>někomu</em></strong> (hurt somebody)</p>
</blockquote>
<h3 id="reader-content-most-common-prepositions-2">Most common prepositions</h3>
<p>This case is used especially with one important preposition.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>k</strong> (toward) – <em>Půjdu k Petrovi.</em> I will go to Peter.</p>
<p><strong>proti</strong> (against) – <em>Jsem proti válce.</em> I’m against war.</p>
</blockquote>
<h2 id="reader-content-o-kom-čem---locative---6.-pád---6th-case">(o) Kom? Čem? - Locative - 6. pád - 6<sup>th</sup> case</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-function-4">Function</h3>
<p>This case is <strong>only used with prepositions</strong> which indicate locality and static nature. It never stands alone.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-form-4">Form</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-20">

















<thead>
<tr>
<th align="center"></th>
<th colspan="6" align="center">Ten (masculine)</th>
<th colspan="4" align="center">Ta ( feminine)</th>
<th colspan="4" align="center">To (neuter)</th>
</tr>
</thead>

<tr>
<td align="center"></td>
<td colspan="2" align="center">Animate</td>

<td colspan="4" align="center"></td>
</tr>

<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center">kamarád</td>

<td align="center">poradce</td>

<td align="center">voda</td>

<td align="center">daň</td>

<td align="center">pivo</td>

</tr>

<td align="center">Sg</td>
<td align="center">-ovi<br>
-u</td>

-i</td>
<td align="center">-u<br>
-ě</td>
<td align="center">- i</td>
<td align="center">- i</td>
<td align="center">-ovi</td>
<td align="center">-ě</td>
<td colspan="3" align="center">-i</td>
<td align="center">-u<br>
-ě</td>
<td align="center">-eti</td>
<td align="center"><strong>^</strong></td>
<td align="center">- i</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center">Pl</td>
<td align="center">-ech</td>
<td colspan="4" align="center">- ích</td>
<td align="center">-ech</td>
<td align="center">-ách</td>
<td colspan="3" align="center">-ích</td>
<td align="center">-ech</td>
<td align="center">-atech</td>
<td colspan="2" align="center">-ích</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Notice that the endings in singular are identical to those of the <em>Komu? Čemu?</em>(Dative) case.</p>
<p><em>TEN</em> nouns in the singular of <em>Kamarád</em> may have two endings in singular: <strong>-ovi, -u.</strong></p>
<p><strong>-ovi</strong> is usually used with names, but only for the last word in a sequence and <strong>-u</strong> for all the preceding words.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>o</strong> Pavl<strong>ovi</strong>, pan<strong>u</strong> doktor<strong>ovi</strong>, pan<strong>u</strong> doktor<strong>u</strong> Havl<strong>ovi</strong>, pan<strong>u</strong> doktor<strong>u</strong> Pavl<strong>u</strong> Havl<strong>ovi</strong></p>
</blockquote>
<h3 id="reader-content-singular-exceptions">Singular exceptions</h3>
<p>In the singular the ending <strong>-e</strong> is realized in the following ways:</p>
<p>after <strong>-l-, -s-,-z-</strong> in the singular of <em>rohlík , voda , pivo.</em></p>
<blockquote>
<p>dopis – v dopise, obraz – na obraze, škola – ve škole, divadlo – v divadle</p>
</blockquote>
<p>after <strong>–k-, -ch-, -r-, -h-, -g-</strong> in the singular of <em>voda</em></p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-21">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>banka</p></td>
<td><p><strong>></strong></p></td>
<td><p><strong>v</strong> bance</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>k <strong>></strong> c</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>sprcha</p></td>
<td><p><strong>></strong></p></td>
<td><p><strong>ve</strong> sprše</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>ch <strong>></strong> š</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>opera</p></td>
<td><p><strong>></strong></p></td>
<td><p><strong>v</strong> opeře</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>r <strong>></strong> ř</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>Praha</p>
<p>Olga</p></td>
<td><p><strong>></strong></p></td>
<td><p><strong>v</strong> Praze</p>
<p><strong>o</strong> Olze</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>h <strong>></strong> z</p>
<p>g <strong>></strong> z</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>(Note also papír – na papíře)</p>
<p>In the singular the ending <strong>-ě</strong> is realized in the following ways:</p>
<p>after <strong>-b-, -p-, -v-, -f-, -m-</strong> in the singular of <em>rohlík, voda, pivo</em></p>
<blockquote>
<p>ryba - v rybě, káva – v kávě, Harfa – na Harfě, zima – v zimě</p>
</blockquote>
<p>after <strong>-d-, -t-, -n-</strong> in the singular of <em>rohlík, voda, pivo</em></p>
<blockquote>
<p>oběd – na obědě, auto – v autě, kino – v kině</p>
</blockquote>
<p>In the singular the ending <strong>-u</strong> of <em>rohlík, pivo</em> is preferred especially after <strong>-k-, -h-, -g-, -ch-</strong>.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Polsko – v Polsku, roh – na rohu, Peking – v Pekingu, ucho – v uchu</p>
</blockquote>
<p>In the singular <em>pivo</em> and <em>rohlík</em> have an alternative ending for same words which have to be memorized.</p>
<p><em>TEN</em> nouns in the plural of <em>kamarád, rohlík, pivo</em> ending in <strong>-k-, -h-, -g-, -ch-</strong> end in <strong>-ích.</strong></p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-22">










<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">vlak</td>
<td align="left"><strong>></strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>ve</strong> vlacích</td>
<td>k <strong>></strong> c</td>
<td align="left">dialog</td>
<td align="left"><strong>></strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>v</strong> dialozích</td>
<td>g <strong>></strong> z</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Čech</td>
<td align="left"><strong>></strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>o</strong> Češích</td>
<td>ch <strong>></strong> š</td>
<td align="left">roh</td>
<td align="left"><strong>></strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>v</strong> rozích</td>
<td>h <strong>></strong> z</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-typical-categories-of-words-1">Typical categories of words</h3>
<p>This case is most likely to be used with:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Place names (when being <strong>in</strong> them)</p></li>
<li><p>Processes and time periods</p></li>
<li><p>Personal names (when talking <strong>about</strong> them)</p></li>
</ul>
<h2 id="reader-content-kým-čím---instrumental---7.-pád---7th-case">Kým? Čím? - Instrumental - 7. pád - 7<sup>th</sup> case</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-function-5">Function</h3>
<ul>
<li><p>Used as an equivalent of <strong><em>with, by, through, via</em></strong> i.e. to describe instruments and means (such as going by car, or digging with a shovel).</p></li>
<li><p>Often used <strong>with prepositions</strong> such as <strong><em>s</em></strong> (with).</p></li>
<li><p>Also used to express permanent or acquired property similarly to Spanish ser and estar.</p></li>
<li><p>This is the least frequent of the Czech cases but it is still very important.</p></li>
</ul>
<h3 id="reader-content-form-5">Form</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-23">

















<thead>
<tr>
<th align="center"></th>
<th colspan="6" align="center"><p>Ten (masculine)</p></th>
<th colspan="4" align="center"><p>Ta ( feminine)</p></th>
<th colspan="4" align="center"><p>To (neuter)</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="center"></td>

<td colspan="2" align="center"><p>Inanimate</p></td>
<td colspan="2" align="center"></td>

<td colspan="4" align="center"></td>
</tr>

<td align="center"><p>nádraží</p></td>
<td align="center"><p>moře</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center"><p>Sg</p></td>
<td colspan="5" align="center"><p>- em</p></td>

<td align="center"><p>-ou</p></td>

<h3 id="reader-content-most-common-prepositions-3">Most common prepositions</h3>
<p>This case is used with several important prepositions and has the meaning of location.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>s</strong> (with) – <em>omeleta se sýrem</em> (omelette with cheese)</p>
<p><strong>před</strong> (in front of), <strong>za</strong> (behind)<strong>, pod</strong> (underneath)<strong>, nad</strong> (above, over)</p>
</blockquote>
<h3 id="reader-content-most-common-verbs-3">Most common verbs</h3>
<p>This case is mostly used with prepositions. There are only a few verbs that take it as their second object.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>zabít někoho <em>něčím</em></strong> (kill somebody [acc.] with something)</p>
<p><strong>překvapit někoho <em>něčím</em></strong> (surprise somebody [acc.] with something)</p>
<p><strong>rozbít něco <em>něčím</em></strong> (break something [acc.] with something)</p>
</blockquote>
<h3 id="reader-content-special-use-after-být-as-acquired-property">Special use after <em>být</em> as acquired property</h3>
<p>The instrumental is also used after the verb <em>být</em> (to be) especially in the future to express becoming something. It is also used as the object of the verb <em>stát se</em> (to become).</p>
<blockquote>
<p>budu učitel<strong>em</strong> (I will be a teacher)</p>
<p>stanu se učitel<strong>em</strong> (I will becom a teacher)</p>
</blockquote>
<h3 id="reader-content-typical-categories-of-words-2">Typical categories of words</h3>
<p>This case is most likely to be used with:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Means of transportation (travel by)</p></li>
<li><p>Condiments, side dishes (food with)</p></li>
<li><p>Personal names (being with)</p></li>
</ul>
<h2 id="reader-content-special-forms-for-talking-to-people-rules-for-changing-peoples-names-when-addressing-them-another-case">Special forms for talking to people: Rules for changing people's names when addressing them – another case?</h2>
<p><em>The first thing to remember:</em> in Czech, when you address someone you have to change the ending of their name. It really does make a lot of difference in establishing communication, even if you are speaking with the person in English. Expect that Czechs will change your name, too, when speaking to you in Czech.</p>
<p><em>The second thing to remember:</em> in polite conversation with someone older than you, or someone you don't know well always use their academic title rather than their name. Thus saying something like <em>Mr. Doctor</em>.</p>
<p>Traditionally, this special form is considered a case called <em>vocative</em>. Czechs know it as the 5<sup>th</sup> case (pátý pád). They all learn the rule as <em>Pátým pádem oslovujeme</em> (we use the fifth case to address).</p>

<h3 id="reader-content-how-to-address-people-with-their-full-names">How to address people with their full names</h3>
<p>Same rules as for first names apply for surnames. You always prefix the surname with</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-26">




<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>Mr. (pan)</p></th>
<th><p>Mrs (paní).</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><table id="reader-content-czech-table-27">



<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Novák</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Novotný</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Malá</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></td>
<td><table id="reader-content-czech-table-28">



<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">pane Nováku</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">pane Novotný</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">paní Malá</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Female surnames always end in <strong><em>á</em></strong>.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-how-to-address-people-with-academic-titles">How to address people with academic titles</h3>
<p>This is very important. You only use the polite Mr. or Mrs. with the title without the name.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-29">






<thead>
<tr>
<th align="left">Men:</th>
<th></th>
<th align="left">Women:</th>
<th></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Mgr. Malý</td>
<td>pane magistře</td>
<td align="left">Mgr. Reslová</td>
<td>paní magistro</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">dr. Novák</td>
<td>pane doktore</td>
<td align="left">PhDr. Nováková</td>
<td>paní doktorko</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">ing. Pinc</td>
<td>pane inženýre</td>
<td align="left">ing. Pincová</td>
<td>paní inženýrko</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">doc. Šebák</td>
<td>pane docente</td>
<td align="left">doc. Šebáková</td>
<td>paní docentko</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">prof. Machovec</td>
<td>pane profesore</td>
<td align="left">prof. Machovcová</td>
<td>paní profesorko</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Note that the female titles append <em>ka</em> to them, which changes to <em>ko</em> when addressing them.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-informal-endearing-ways-to-address-someone">Informal, Endearing ways to address someone</h3>
<p>Czechs sometimes use a special form of the first name to address people. This consists of the shortening of the name (unless the name is already short) and adding the ending <strong>-i</strong> for male or female names or occasionally <strong>-uš</strong> or <strong>-u</strong> for some female names. This is very informal and used only with the closest of friends in the context of endearment. Also, it does not work with surnames. In loving contexts, the <strong>-i</strong> is often lengthened to <strong>-í.</strong></p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-30">






<thead>
<tr>
<th align="left">Male names:</th>
<th></th>
<th align="left">Female names:</th>
<th></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Martin</td>
<td>Marti</td>
<td align="left">Jana</td>
<td>Jani</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Petr</td>
<td>Petři</td>
<td align="left">Petra</td>
<td>Petři, Petru, Petruš</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Karel</td>
<td>Karli</td>
<td align="left">Veronika</td>
<td>Veru</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Honza</td>
<td>Honzi</td>
<td align="left">Jarmila</td>
<td>Jaru, Jaruš</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Standa</td>
<td>Standi</td>
<td align="left"></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> Not all names lend themselves to this form as well as others. Long male names ending in hard consonants such as Michal, Patrik or female names ending in consonants such as Karin. However, you will find that families may find other nicknames for people with these names to which this form can be added. Often, this form is only suitable to use within a family or a close group of friends.</p>

<h2 id="reader-content-softening-palatalization">Softening – Palatalization</h2>
<p>Some endings in Czech nouns soften the preceding consonant. This is sometimes called palatalization because the roof of the tongue moves to or towards the palate to produce the new consonant. The endings are: <em>Kdo? Co? TEN animate nouns plural</em> (kamarád)<em>,</em> and <em>Komu? Čemu?/Kom? Čem? TA nouns in singular</em> (voda)</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-ten-animate-nouns---kamarád-doktor-odborník-...">TEN animate nouns - kamarád, doktor, odborník ...</h3>

<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">k <strong>></strong> c</td>
<td align="left">odborník <strong>></strong> odborníci</td>
<td align="left">(expert)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">ch <strong>></strong> š</td>
<td align="left">Čech <strong>></strong> Češi</td>
<td align="left">(Czech)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">r <strong>></strong> ř</td>
<td align="left">doktor <strong>></strong> doktoři</td>
<td align="left">(doctor)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">h <strong>></strong> z</td>
<td align="left">pstruh <strong>></strong> pstruzi</td>
<td align="left">(trout)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<td align="left">(opera)</td>

<p>Some of these models are fairly common (such as <em>muzeum/album</em>) while others only apply to a few nouns. <em>Les</em> is the only lesser model used almost exclusively for nouns of Czech origin.</p>
<p>* This model is only used for male names ending in –í of which Jiří is the only Czech example. However, it is also used for all foreign male names ending in –y such as Henry, Woody.</p>
<p>** This model is only used for female names of foreign origin ending in a consonant or –y. All forms are the same.</p>
<p><strong>†</strong> Nouns belonging to this model contain many exceptions. Some can take the endings on regular masculine inanimates ending in a hard consonant (e.g. status, status, statusu, statusu, statusu, statusem) and sometimes the declension is mixed. This varies much from speaker to speaker. <em>Autobus</em> doesn’t belong to the model. If the noun ending in <em>–us</em> is a name such as <em>Spartakus</em>, <em>Ikarus</em>, etc., then regular <em>kamarád</em> endings replace the <em>–us</em>.</p>
<p><strong>‡</strong> <em>Album</em> only differs from <em>museum</em> in the plural. The difference is whether the <em>–um</em> is preceded by a vowel or a consonant.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-most-useful-noun-endings">Most useful noun endings</h2>
<p>Learning all the endings of Czech nouns may seem daunting. However, most of them are only used quite rarely. You will need to learn them eventually but, in the meantime, you can get very far with just six including when no ending is added, which is also important. They are: -y/i, -a, -u, - Ø, -ů, -e/-ě and are listed roughly in order of frequency and importance.</p>
<p>As you can see, each of these endings can have multiple function and can be used different with different types of nouns. But if you ever need to guess how to change a noun, you will probably get much closer to the correct one with the top 3 on this list than any other. You will often sound better with the wrong ending than with no ending at all.</p>

> Section 5 of 22 · Parts of speech: Nouns, Verbs and More > Adjectives · id `adjectives` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=adjectives)

<h1 id="reader-content-adjectives">Adjectives</h1>
<p>There are two types of adjectives in Czech. One type ends in a <em>hard consonant</em> <strong>+ ý, á, é</strong>; and the other in a <em>soft consonant</em> <strong>+ í</strong> in the Kdo? Co? case.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-hard-adjectives">Hard Adjectives</h2>
<p>Adjectives where the last consonant is: <strong><em>-h-, -ch-, -k-, -r-, -d-, -t-, -n-</em></strong> (see <em>Pronunciation</em> for more details).</p>

<p><em>*</em> this ending softens the preceding consonant (see <em>Palatalization</em>)</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-soft-adjectives">Soft Adjectives</h2>
<p>Adjectives where the last consonant is: <strong>-ž-, -č-, -š-, -ř-, -c-, -j-,-ď-, -ť-, -ň-</strong> (see <em>Pronunciation</em> for more details).</p>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> Remember that <em>d, t, n</em> when followed by <em>i, í,</em> or <em>ě</em> are pronounced <em>ď, ť, ň</em>.</p>

<h2 id="reader-content-softening-palatalization-1">Softening – Palatalization</h2>
<p>Czech adjectives in <em>Kdo? Co? TEN animate plural</em> soften the last consonant. The rules for softening the consonant are similar to nouns in the same case.</p>

<tr>

<td align="left">(deaf)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>r <strong>></strong> ř</td>
<td align="left">starý <strong>></strong> staří</td>
<td align="left">(old)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>h <strong>></strong> z</td>
<td align="left"><em>drahý <strong>></strong> drazí</em></td>
<td align="left">(expensive)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>d <strong>></strong> ď</td>
<td align="left">mladý <strong>></strong> mladí</td>
<td align="left">(young)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>n <strong>></strong> ň</td>
<td align="left">unavený <strong>></strong> unavení</td>
<td align="left">(tired)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>t <strong>></strong> ť</td>
<td align="left"><em>zlatý <strong>></strong> zlatí</em></td>
<td align="left">(golden)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<h2 id="reader-content-how-to-form-adjectives">How to Form Adjectives</h2>
<ul>
<li><p>Adjectives are words that describe properties of other objects.</p></li>
<li><p>The question word for adjectives is <em>jaký?</em> vs. <em>co?</em> for nouns.</p></li>
<li><p>Adjectives can be either genuine (<em>nový dům</em> = new house) or derived (<em>zeleninová pizza</em> = vegetable pizza)</p></li>
</ul>
<h3 id="reader-content-adjectives-not-formed-from-other-parts-of-speech">Adjectives not formed from other parts of speech</h3>
<p>Most adjectives describe properties of other objects by themselves, and are not derived from other words:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>malý</strong> (small), <strong>velký</strong> (large), <strong>celý</strong> (whole), žlutý (yellow), <strong>bílý</strong> (white), <strong>nový</strong> (new), <strong>tvrdý</strong> (hard), <strong>veselý</strong> (cheerful), <strong>zdravý</strong> (healthy)…</p>
</blockquote>
<h3 id="reader-content-adjectives-derived-from-nouns">Adjectives derived from nouns</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-50">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Of what substance/ origin:</td>
<td>-ový</td>
<td colspan="2">oliva - <em>olivový</em> (olej), zelenina - <em>zeleninový</em> (salát), salám - <em>salámová</em> (pizza)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td>-í</td>

<td></td>
<td></td>
<td colspan="2"></td>

</tr>

<h3 id="reader-content-compound-adjectives">Compound Adjectives</h3>
<p><em>modr<strong>o</strong>oký</em> (blue-eyed), <em>krátk<strong>o</strong>zraký</em> (near-sighted), <em>čern<strong>o</strong>bílý</em> (black and white) <em><br>
světl<strong>e</strong>modrý</em> (light blue)</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-possessive-adjectives">Possessive adjectives</h2>
<p>In English, possession is expressed by –’<em>s</em>. For instance, <em>John’s father, Jana’s book</em>. In Czech, endings <strong>-ův</strong> or <strong>-in</strong> are used as in <em>Johnův otec</em> - <em>Janina kniha</em>. They take different forms in different cases. See below. The only reason they are called adjectives is because they are placed in the same position as adjectives and agree in case with the following noun. Otherwise, their endings are similar to those of nouns. They are only really used with nouns that describe animate objects and proper names. Never with nouns like <em>stůl, auto, mléko</em> (the Koho? Čeho? form is used instead).</p>

<td>TA</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>

</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-53">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">!</td>
<td align="left">In English, the possessive relationship is sometimes expressed by <em>of</em>. In that case, in Czech the <em>Koho? Čeho?</em> case is used. For instance, <em>kamarád Petra</em>.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

> Section 6 of 22 · Parts of speech: Nouns, Verbs and More > Pronouns · id `pronouns` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=pronouns)

<h1 id="reader-content-pronouns">Pronouns</h1>
<h2 id="reader-content-personal-pronouns">Personal Pronouns</h2>
<p>Personal pronouns have different forms after prepositions, and at the beginning of sentences. If they are used independently, an unstressed form is used. Prepositions, however, require a stressed form. Já, ty, on have a special form used for when the noun is at the beginning of the sentence or when it is stressed.</p>

<p>The remaining pronouns are identical to the forms of pronouns without preposition.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-possessive-pronouns">Possessive Pronouns</h2>
<p>Possessive pronouns express the relationship of possession, such as <em>my, your, …, their friend, friends</em>. In Czech, each possessive pronoun has several forms marking both the case and gender of the following noun. Note, also, that Czech possessive pronouns do not have a stressed form like the English <em>mine, yours, …</em>.</p>

<h2 id="reader-content-svůj-a-tricky-possessive-pronoun">SVŮJ a tricky possessive pronoun</h2>
<p>This pronoun is a rough equivalent of the English <em>my own, ..., their own</em>. It is only used when the possessor is also the subject of the sentence. This allows us to differentiate two meanings of <em>He went to visit his brother</em>.: <em>Šel navštívit <strong>jeho</strong> bratra.</em> (somebody else's) or <em>Šel navštívit <strong>svého</strong> bratra.</em> (his own)</p>

<p><strong><em>Tento, tenhle</em></strong> or <strong><em>tamten</em></strong> are used when we are pointing at one object or person among several others. <strong><em>Ten, ta, to</em></strong> are used if there is no doubt which one we mean. They often correspond to the English <strong><em>the</em></strong>.</p>

<h2 id="reader-content-forms-of-všechno-every-all">Forms of VŠECHNO (every, all)</h2>

<h2 id="reader-content-interrogative-relative-indefinite-and-negative-pronouns-and-adverbs">Interrogative (relative), Indefinite and Negative Pronouns and Adverbs</h2>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-64">









<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p><strong>?</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Interrogative</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>ně-</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Indefinite</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>ni-</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Negative</strong></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p>co</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>what</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>něco</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>something</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>nic</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>nothing</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p>kdo</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>who</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>někdo</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>somebody</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>nikdo</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>nobody</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p>kde</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>where</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>někde</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>somewhere</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>nikde</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>nowhere</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p>kdy</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>when</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>někdy</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>sometime</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>nikdy</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>never</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p>jaký</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>what kind</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>nějaký</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>some kind of</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>žádný</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>none</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p>jak</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>how</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>nějak</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>somehow</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>nijak</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>in no way</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p>který</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>which</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>některý</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>some</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>žádný</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>none</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>!</p></td>
<td colspan="6" align="left"><ul>
<li><p><em>co, kdo</em> change in the same manner as the case question words.</p></li>
<li><p><em>jaký</em>, <em>který</em> behave as normal adjectives.</p></li>
<li><p><em>kdy, kde, jak</em> are adverbs and not pronouns, they were included because they behave in a similar manner</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-examples-of-usage">Examples of usage:</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-65">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Vidím něco.</td>
<td align="left">I see something.</td>
<td align="left">Nevím nic.</td>
<td align="left">I don’t know anything.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Někdo jde.</td>
<td align="left">Somebody is coming.</td>
<td align="left">Nikdo to neví.</td>
<td align="left">Nobody knows it.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Někde mám peníze.</td>
<td align="left">I have money somewhere.</td>
<td align="left">Nikde nemám domov.</td>
<td align="left">I don’t have a home anywhere.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Někdy si zpívám.</td>
<td align="left">Sometimes I sing.</td>
<td align="left">Nikdy nemám čas.</td>
<td align="left">I never have time.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je tu nějaký muž.</td>
<td align="left">There is some (a) man here.</td>
<td align="left">Není tu žádný muž.</td>
<td align="left">There is no man here.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Nějak to uděláme.</td>
<td align="left">We will do it somehow.</td>
<td align="left">Nijak se to nelepší.</td>
<td align="left">It is not getting better in any way.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Někteří lidé jsou bohatí.</td>
<td align="left">Some people are rich.</td>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-other-prefixes">Other prefixes</h3>
<p>There are also other prefixes that can be used with these words. For example:</p>
<p><em>lec-/leda(s)</em> (just any, many) – <em>ledakdo</em> (just anybody)<em>, leckde</em> (just anywhere)</p>
<p><em>kde-</em> (pretty much any) – <em>kdekdo</em>, <em>kdeco</em> (cannot be used with <em>kde</em>) (colloquial)</p>
<p><em>málo-</em> (few) of <em>málokdo</em> (few people), <em>málokdy</em> (not often).</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-suffixes">Suffixes</h3>
<p>All of these words can also be used with suffixes which modify their meaning in various ways. <em>Jaký</em> and <em>který</em> take a regular ending before this suffix (e.g. <em>s jakýmsi</em> – with some…).</p>
<p>–<em>koli</em> (–ever) as in <em>kdokoli</em> (whoever), <em>kdekoli</em> (wherever), <em>jakýkoli</em> (whichever) etc.</p>
<p><em>–si</em> (some–) used as the archaic equivalent of <em>ně</em>– as in <em>kdosi</em> (someone), <em>cosi</em> (something), <em>kdesi</em> (somewhere), etc. <em>Kdysi</em> is now used mostly in the meaning of <em>long time ago</em>.</p>
<p><em>–pak</em> (quizzical enquiry) as in <em>kdopak</em> (who might it be), <em>copak</em>, <em>kdypak</em> (very colloquial)</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-reflexive-pronouns">Reflexive pronouns</h2>
<p>The reflexive pronouns refer back to the subject of the sentence. Their general meaning is <strong><em>myself, yourself, itself, .…, themselves</em></strong>. The difference between se/si and sebe/sobě is that between unstressed and stressed form.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-66">









<tbody>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r1c1" scope="col"></th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r1c2" scope="col">Kdo? Co?</th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r1c3" scope="col">Koho? Co?</th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r1c4" scope="col" align="left">Koho? Čeho?</th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r1c5" scope="col">Komu? Čemu?</th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r1c6" scope="col" align="left">Kom? Čem?</th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r1c7" scope="col" align="left">Kým? Čím?</th>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r2c1" scope="row">unstressed</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r2c2">-</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r2c3">se</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r2c4" align="left">-</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r2c5">si</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r2c6" align="left">-</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r2c7" align="left">-</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r3c1" scope="row">stressed</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r3c2">-</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r3c3">sebe</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r3c4" align="left">sebe</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r3c5">sobě</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r3c6" align="left">o sobě</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-b09b7bc1e90951002a81-r3c7" align="left">sebou</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-67">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>!</p></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Unlike English, however, Czech only uses one form <em>se</em> or <em>si</em> for all subjects</p></li>
<li><p><em>se</em> has the meaning of <em>Koho? Co?</em></p></li>
<li><p><em>si</em> has the meaning of <em>Komu? Čemu?</em></p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-uses-of-se">Uses of <em>SE</em></h3>
<p>NO MEANING: verbs where <em>se</em> is only a part of the verb</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-68">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">ptát se</td>
<td align="left">Koho? Čeho?</td>
<td align="left">to ask</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">smát se</td>
<td align="left">Komu? Čemu?</td>
<td align="left">to laugh</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">bát se</td>
<td align="left">Koho? Čeho?</td>
<td align="left">to be afraid</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>PASSIVE: <em>se</em> adds the meaning of general subject</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-69">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>Kouří se tady?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Does one smoke here?</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>Jde se?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Shall we go?</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>To se neříká.</p></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>It's not said.</p>
<p>One/you doesn't say that.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<ul>
<li>RECIPROCITY: signifies relationship between two or more objects</li>
</ul>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-70">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Známe se.</td>
<td></td>
<td>We know each other.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Sejdeme se.</td>
<td></td>
<td>We will meet together.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Milujeme se.</td>
<td></td>
<td>We love each other.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Potkáme se.</td>
<td></td>
<td>We will meet (run into) one another.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>!</td>
<td colspan="2">If a verb has <em>SE</em>, the following noun cannot be in <em>Koho? Co?</em> case.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-uses-of-si">Uses of <em>SI</em></h3>
<p>TO ONESELF</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-71">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Dám si.</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">I will have.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Koupím si.</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">I will buy.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>RECIPROCITY</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-72">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Píšeme si.</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">We write to each other.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Dáváme si dary.</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">We give each other presents.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>CASUAL ATTITUDE</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-73">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Vejde si do hospody.</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">So, he walks into a bar, just like that.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Píská si vesele.</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">He is whistling away happily.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-74">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>!</strong></td>
<td align="left">If a verb has <em>SI</em>, the following noun cannot be in <em>Komu? Čemu?</em> case.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">2</td>

<td align="left">12</td>
<td align="left">dvanáct</td>
<td align="left">22</td>

</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">3</td>
<td align="left">tři</td>

<h2 id="reader-content-and-negative-numbers">0 and negative numbers</h2>
<blockquote>
<p>0 nula</p>
<p>-1 mínus jedna</p>
</blockquote>
<h2 id="reader-content-decimals-and-fractions">Decimals and Fractions</h2>
<p>Decimals are written with a comma as in <strong>1,5</strong> which is pronounced <strong>celá</strong> (whole) as in <strong>jedna celá pět</strong>.</p>
<p>Decimals between 0 and 1 are always pronounced with zero as in 0,2 <strong>nula celá dva</strong>.</p>
<p>Fraction names are formed by adding the ending <strong>–ina</strong> to the number.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>pět pět<strong>ina</strong></p>
<p>deset deset<strong>ina</strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p>Fractions formed from 2 - 4 have irregular forms</p>
<blockquote>
<p>dva <strong>polovina</strong> (half)</p>
<p>tři tř<strong>etina</strong> (third)</p>
<p>čtyři čt<strong>vrtina</strong> (quarter)</p>
</blockquote>
<h2 id="reader-content-types-of-numerals">Types of numerals</h2>
<p>Numerals are much trickier in Czech than English. Here is a summary of the different types of numerals:</p>

<h2 id="reader-content-case-and-numbers">Case and numbers</h2>
<p>Yes. Regrettably, all the Czech numbers have cases. Therefore, you use a different form when you say <em>I'll have <strong>two</strong> dumplings</em> (dám si dva knedlíky) and <em>I'll have it <strong>with</strong> two dumplings</em> (dám si to se dvěma knedlíky.) Also the endings are different for <strong><em>one</em></strong>, and <strong><em>two, three, four</em></strong> and <em><strong>five</strong> and more</em>.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-cardinal-numbers-1-2-3-4">Cardinal numbers 1, 2, 3, 4</h3>

<td align="left"><em>třema, čtyřma</em> are <em>třemi, čtyřmi</em> in literary Czech; <em>dvěma,</em> however, is the correct form in both literary and colloquial Czech. It is often confusing for Czechs, too.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-cardinal-numbers-5-and-more">Cardinal numbers 5 and more</h3>
<p>Numbers from <strong>5</strong> through <strong>99</strong> use the ending <strong><em>-i</em></strong> for all cases except for <em>Koho? Co?</em> which stays the same as <em>Kdo? Co?</em></p>
<p>In compound numbers all the components have the ending. E.g. <em>s dvacet<strong>i</strong> pět<strong>i</strong>, bez dvacet<strong>i</strong> <strong>dvou</strong></em>.</p>
<p>100 (<strong>sto</strong>) has the same endings as <em>pivo</em> but doesn't change in daily spoken Czech</p>
<p>1 000 (<strong>tisíc</strong>) has the same endings as <em>čaj</em></p>
<p>1 000 000 (<strong>milión</strong>) has the same endings as <em>rohlík</em></p>
<p>1 000 000 000 (<strong>miliarda</strong>) has the same endings as <em>žena</em></p>
<h3 id="reader-content-other-numbers">Other numbers</h3>
<p><em>Ordinal</em> numbers (first, second, etc.) behave as adjectives: (<em>první, druhý, třetí, …</em>)</p>
<p>The <em>names</em> of numbers behave as feminine nouns: (<em>jednička, dvojka, trojka,</em> …)</p>
<p><em>Multiplication</em> numbers do not change their forms with case: (<em>jednou, dvakrát</em>, <em>třikrát, …</em>)</p>
<p><em>Generic</em> numbers don't often have to change their forms: (<em>jedny, dvoje, troje, …</em>) but if necessary they will adopt the forms of the plural of feminine nouns ending in <em>-a</em>.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-number-names">Number names</h3>
<p>Numbers have special forms for their names. These might be used for instance when referring to numbered trains, players or grades. In English, just the number or the word Number is used.</p>
<p>The number name is formed using the ending <strong>-ka</strong>. This ending often modifies the form of the preceding number. See table above for exceptions.</p>
<p>9, 10, 20, 30, 40 <strong>-ítka</strong> (replaces <strong>-et</strong> as in dev<strong>ět</strong> - dev<strong>ítka</strong>)</p>
<p>33 second number is pronounced first followed by <strong>a</strong> as in <strong>třiatřicítka</strong></p>
<p>450 <strong>-ka</strong> is only added to the last number as in <strong>čtyřistapadesátka</strong></p>
<p>200 – 900 the first number uses the Koho? Čeho? form as in 500 = <strong>pětistovka</strong></p>

<h2 id="reader-content-time-telling">Time telling</h2>
<p>There are two systems for telling time in Czech. Everybody understands the digital or military time which is used with a 24 hour clock but there is also another, more devious, way of telling time in Czech based on the 12 hour clock.</p>
<p>This system shows time in five-minute intervals with respect to fifteen-minute intervals on the dial. That is the top of the hour, quarter of the hour, bottom of the hour, and three quarters of the hour. So the following system is used:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-85">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left">5:55</td>
<td align="left">za pět minut šest</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">six in 5 minutes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>6:00</td>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">šest</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">six</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left">6:05</td>
<td align="left">šest a pět minut</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">six and 5 minutes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left">6:10</td>
<td align="left"><em>za pět minut</em> čtvrt na sedm</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">a quarter of the way to 7 in 5 minutes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>6:15</td>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">čtvrt na sedm</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">a quarter of the way to 7</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left">6:20</td>
<td align="left">čtvrt na sedm <em>a pět minut</em></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">a quarter of the way to 7 and 5 minutes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left">6:25</td>
<td align="left">za pět minut půl sedmé</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">half of the 7<strong>th</strong> in 5 minutes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>6:30</td>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">půl sedmé</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">half of the 7<strong>th</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left">6:35</td>
<td align="left">půl sedmé a pět minut</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">half of the 7<strong>th</strong> and 5 minutes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left">6:40</td>
<td align="left"><em>za pět minut</em> tři čtvrtě na sedm</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">three quarters of the way to 7 in 5 minutes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>6:45</td>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">tři čtvrtě na sedm</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">three quarters of the way to 7</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left">6:50</td>
<td align="left">tři čtvrtě na sedm <em>a pět minut</em></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">three quarters of the way to 7 and 5 minutes</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<ul>
<li><p>with <em>half</em>, the number of the hour is given in the ordinal form (2nd-12th) in the <em>Koho? Čeho?</em> case form (the exception is 1); the other three points of reference use <em>na</em> + the normal number in <em>Koho? Co?</em> (thus <em>jednu</em>)</p></li>
<li><p>this system is used with the 12 hour dial <strong>only -</strong> <em><del>za pět minut šestnáct</del></em></p></li>
<li><p>it is also possible to use 10-minute increments; thus: 5:50 - za deset minut šest, 6:10 - šest a deset minut, 6:20 - za deset minut půl sedmé, 6:35 - za deset minut tři čtvrtě na sedm</p></li>
</ul>

<h2 id="reader-content-dates-or-kolikátého-je">Dates or <em>Kolikátého je?</em></h2>
<p>Month is given second with both numeral followed by a period. E.g. 14<sup>th</sup> of January 1999 – 14. 1. 1999.</p>
<p>Both the ordinal number and the name of the month are in <em>Koho? Čeho?</em> case. If the numeral has two parts, such as twenty-two, both parts are changed.</p>
<p>The endings used for ordinal numbers are those of adjectives in <em>Koho? Čeho?</em> and the endings used for names of months are those of nouns.</p>
<p>The question used for asking for the date is: <em>Kolikátého je?</em></p>

<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">24<sup>th</sup></td>

<td align="left">1999</td>
</tr>

<td align="left">24.</td>
<td align="left">ledna</td>
<td align="left">1999</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">dvacát<strong>ého</strong> čtvrt<strong>ého</strong></td>
<td align="left">ledna</td>
<td align="left">devatenáct set devadesát devět</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">genitive of ordinal number</td>

<td align="left">year – number of hundreds</td>
</tr>
