<h3 id="reader-content-pronouncing-js-at-the-beginning-of-words">Pronouncing <em>j</em>s at the beginning of words</h3>
<p><strong><em>Jsem</em></strong> is correctly pronounced as '<strong><em>sem</em></strong>'. Although, there are Czechs who will try to convince you otherwise. The same holds for words like <strong><em>jmenuju se</em></strong>. Note, however, that in negation, the <strong><em>j</em></strong> comes back. Therefore, <strong>nej</strong>sem and <strong>nej</strong>menuju se.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-i-you-he-she-they---personal-pronouns-and-endings"><em>I, you, he, she … they -</em> Personal Pronouns and Endings</h2>
<p>Personal pronouns always have a corresponding ending on the verb they are paired with. The system of endings is fairly complex (see below) but it has these things in common.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-92">











<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"></td>
<td><p>pronoun</p></td>
<td><p>ending</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>pronoun</p></td>
<td colspan="2"><p>ending</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p>I</p></td>
<td><p>já</p></td>
<td><p>-u, -i, -m (-ím, -ám)</p></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>we</p></td>
<td><p>my</p></td>
<td colspan="2"><p>-me (-eme, …, -áme)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p>you</p></td>
<td><p>ty</p></td>
<td><p>-š (-eš, -neš, -ješ, -íš, -áš)</p></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>you guys</p></td>
<td><p>vy</p></td>
<td colspan="2"><p>-te (-ete, …, -áte)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" rowspan="3"><p>he</p>
<p>she</p>
<p>it</p></td>
<td rowspan="3"><p>on</p>
<p>ona</p>
<p>to/ono</p></td>
<td rowspan="3"><p>-0 (-e, -ne, -je, -í, -á)</p></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>they</p></td>
<td><p>oni</p></td>
<td colspan="2" rowspan="3"><p>-ou, -í (-í, -ejí, -ají)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>!</p></td>
<td colspan="6"><p>Mostly, the ending is the only way of expressing the person (similarly to Spanish). A personal pronoun is only used when stressing the subject of the verb (i.e. agent). Something done mostly by intonation in English. Therefore there is a big difference between I am = jsem and <strong>I</strong> am = <strong>já</strong> jsem.</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-summary-of-conjugation-patterns">Summary of Conjugation Patterns</h2>
<p>There are five categories of Czech verbs which are then further broken into subclasses (giving a total of 14 models). To simplify, we only show an overview with four classes (commonly used for learners) and treat the subclasses as exceptions. The entire system is described at the end of the Verbs section.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-93">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p><strong>INFINITIVE</strong></p></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><p>-uju</p>
<p>-uješ</p>
<p>-uje</p></td>
<td><p>-ujeme</p>
<p>-ujete</p>
<p>-ujou</p></td>
<td><p><strong>-ovat</strong></p></td>
<td><p>kupovat, pracovat, studovat, telefonovat, kontrolovat, opakovat</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><p>-ám</p>
<p>-áš</p>
<p>-á</p></td>
<td><p>-áme</p>
<p>-áte</p>
<p>-ají</p></td>
<td><p><strong>-at</strong></p>
<p><strong>-át</strong></p></td>
<td><p>dělat, obědvat, snídat</p>
<p>ptát se, znát</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><p>-ím</p>
<p>-íš</p>
<p>-í</p></td>
<td><p>-íme</p>
<p>-íte</p>
<p>-í, -ejí</p></td>
<td><p><strong>-et</strong></p>
<p><strong>-ět</strong></p>
<p><strong>-it</strong></p></td>
<td><p>večeřet, bydlet, myslet</p>
<p>rozumět, sedět, vědět – vím</p>
<p>končit, mluvit</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><p>-u</p>
<p>-eš</p>
<p>-e</p></td>
<td><p>-eme</p>
<p>-ete</p>
<p>-ou</p></td>
<td><p><strong>!</strong></p>
<p><strong>irregular</strong></p></td>
<td><p>jít - jdeš, jet - jedeš, číst - čteš, psát -píšeš, mýt se - myješ se, pít - piješ,</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>!</p></td>
<td colspan="4"><ul>
<li><p>Unlike English, French, or German, Czech usually uses only the ending for expressing the subject of the verb.</p></li>
<li><p>Personal pronouns (<em>I, you, he, she</em> .…) are used only to stress the subject.</p></li>
<li><p>Until recently, <strong>–u/-ou</strong> endings for <em>I</em> and <em>they</em> were considered inappropriate for standard or written Czech and <strong>‑i/‑í</strong> were the only acceptable forms even if they were never used in conversation. Nowadays, <strong>-u/-ou</strong> are acceptable in all contexts but occasionally you may still encounter some people who consider <strong>-u</strong>/<strong>-ou</strong> to be inappropriate in formal contexts.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-infinitive">Infinitive</h2>
<ul>
<li><p>The infinitive (the form listed in the dictionary) is mostly used with verbs like <em>chtít, muset, moct, potřebovat</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>It is the form of the verb that is found in the dictionaries.</p></li>
<li><p>The ending of the infinitive is always <strong><em>-t</em></strong>. Sometimes, especially in songs and poems, you may encounter the archaic form ending <strong><em>-ti</em></strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>The other exception is the verb <strong>can</strong> which has the alternative infinitive form <strong>mo<em>ci</em></strong> and verbs derived from it like <em>pomoci</em> (help). In spoken Czech, moct and pomoct more are common.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-94">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">muset</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">večeřet, končit, pracovat, studovat, …</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></li>
</ul>
<h3 id="reader-content-examples">Examples</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-95">








<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">musím se uči<strong>t</strong></td>
<td align="left">I have <strong>to</strong> study</td>
<td>chci mí<strong>t</strong></td>
<td align="left">I want <strong>to</strong> have</td>
<td align="left">potřebuju pí<strong>t</strong></td>
<td align="left">I need <strong>to</strong> drink</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">musím jí<strong>t</strong></td>
<td align="left">I have <strong>to</strong> go</td>
<td>chci spá<strong>t</strong></td>
<td align="left">I want <strong>to</strong> sleep</td>
<td align="left">potřebuju</td>
<td align="left">I need <strong>to</strong> have</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">musíš psá<strong>t</strong></td>
<td align="left">You have <strong>to</strong> write</td>
<td>chceš vstá<strong>t</strong>?</td>
<td align="left">do you want <strong>to</strong> get up?</td>
<td align="left">obědva<strong>t</strong></td>
<td align="left">lunch</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Other verbs used with the infinitive are: <em>mít</em> (should), <em>smět</em> (may), <em>nesmět</em> (must not), <em>začít</em> (begin), <em>přestat</em> (stop)</p>
<p>The infinitive can also be used in prohibitions and in emphatic commands as in <em>neparkovat!</em> (do not park) or <em>mlčet!</em> (be quiet); wishes and statements of condition (see Conditionals and Wishing later).</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-tense-and-aspect-saying-when-and-how-things-happen">Tense and Aspect (Saying When and How Things Happen)</h2>
<p>Traditionally, Czech is said to only have three tenses–past, present and future. All the complexity of how things happen in time is then expressed by aspect (vid). Traditional Czech is said to have two aspects–perfective and imperfective. The traditional <strong>definition</strong> is very simple: <strong>the perfective</strong> describes completed events happening once while <strong>the</strong> <strong>imperfective</strong> describes events in progress not yet completed. But in actual use things get much more complex not least because aspect is used slightly differently with different tenses. And this is one of the reasons why the this guide takes a different approach describing aspect from other grammars. It combines tense and aspect rather than keeping them separate. This approach has many advantages including the fact that it make the Czech tense system easy to contrast with English.</p>
<p>According to this new approach, instead of just three, there are actually <strong>eight tenses</strong> in Czech and each expresses a number of functions. To form these tenses appropriately it is necessary to use the verb of the appropriate aspect. There are three aspects of verbs – perfective, imperfective and repetitive. For almost all verbs the repetitive form is the same as the imperfective. Verbs of motion have all three forms.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-what-tenses-there-are-and-how-to-form-them">What tenses there are and how to form them</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-96">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Perfective</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Imperfective</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Repetitive</strong></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Past</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Form: -l</strong> form of perfective verbs.</p>
<p><strong>Meaning:</strong> Actions completed in the past (often only happening once).</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Form: -l</strong> form of imperfective verbs.</p>
<p><strong>Meaning:</strong> Actions happening for a certain time/in parallel in the past.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Form: -l</strong> form of imperfective verbs.</p>
<p><strong>Meaning:</strong> Habitual or repeated actions in the past.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Present</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Form:</strong> Conjugated form of imperfective verbs.</p>
<p><strong>Meaning:</strong> Actions happening now.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Form:</strong> Conjugated form of imperfective verbs.</p>
<p><strong>Meaning:</strong> Repeated, habitual actions in the present.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Future</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Form:</strong> Conjugated form of perfective verbs.</p>
<p><strong>Meaning:</strong> Future completed, unrepeated actions.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Form:</strong> <em>budu</em> + infinitive of imperfective verbs.</p>
<p><strong>Meaning:</strong> Future continuous actions.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Form:</strong> <em>budu</em> + infinitive of imperfective verbs.</p>
<p><strong>Meaning:</strong> Future repeated, habitual actions.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-what-do-the-tenses-express">What do the tenses express</h3>
<p>Each tense has several functions, that is, it expresses a slightly different meaning depending on context. While no Czech tense has a clear equivalent in another English tense, most functions do. Sometimes there are two functions in Czech where English only has one and vice versa. For ease of use, functions that are not as common or are of more benefit to the more advanced student are marked [ADV]. This division is not solid but might provide an insight into which functions are more important to learn first.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-97">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Tense/aspect</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Function</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Example</strong></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>PAST perfective</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Accomplished activity in the past (with <em>už</em> [already])</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Ten dopis jsem už napsal.</em><br>
(I’ve already written the letter.)</p>
<p><em>Domácí úkol už jsem udělal.</em><br>
(I’ve already done my homework.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Completed condition for future or action or state (with <em>jestli</em> or <em>pokud</em>)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Jestli to udělal, bude mít hodně peněz.</p>
<p>(If he had done it, he will have lots of money.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Interruption to an ongoing activity (expressed by past imperfective)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Díval jsem se na televizi [ipf.], když zazvonil telefon.<br>
(I was watching TV, when the phone rang.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Departure for further activity (often beginning with <em>když</em>)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Když jsem se nasnídal, šel jsem do školy.* (When I[’d] had my breakfast, I went to school.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Enumerated accomplishment in the past</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Přečetl tu knihu třikrát.</p>
<p>(He read the book twice.)</p>
<p>Několikrát vylezl na Sněžku.</p>
<p>(He climbed Sněžka several times.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>PAST imperfective</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Parallel continuous actions in the past (Narrative) (often with <em>zatímco</em> [while])</p>
<p>Ongoing actions in the past interrupted by a perfective action (often with <em>když</em> [when])</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Včera večer Petr vařil večeři a zpíval si.* (Last night Peter was cooking dinner, and singing.)</p>
<p>Díval jsem se na televizi, když zazvonil telefon [pf].<br>
(I was watching TV, when the phone rang.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Action continuing for a period of time in the past</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Včera jsem se díval na televizi od osmi do desíti.</p>
<p>(Yesterday, I was watching TV from 8 to 10.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Recent and/or remembered experience (can be enumerated, often in questions)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Četl jsi tu knihu?</em><br>
(Did you read the book?)</p>
<p>Díval jsem se na to dvakrát.<br>
(I watched it twice.)</p>
<p><em>Šel jsi do kina?</em><br>
(Did you go to the movies?)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Background condition for past state [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Jestli to dělal, cítil se šťastný.* (If he was doing it, he was feeling happy.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Sudden perception or realization [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Přišel a viděl.</em><br>
(He came and he saw.)</p>
<p>Najednou cítil, že ho někdo sleduje.</p>
<p>(Suddenly, he felt that someone is watching him.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Emphatic questions about the source/producer (optional) [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Kdo ti šil ten svetr?</em> (Who sowed the sweater for you?)</p>
<p>Kdo ti to říkal?</p>
<p>(Who told you that?)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>PAST repetitive</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Repeated action in the past</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Chodil jsem do školy každý den po tři roky.</em><br>
(For three years, I went to school every day.)</p>
<p>Domácí úkoly jsem si dělal každý den.<br>
(I did my homework every day.)</p>
<p>Už tenkrát si děti pravidelně čistily zuby.</p>
<p>(Even then, children brushed their teeth regularly.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Life experience (with <em>už</em>)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Petr to už dělal.</em><br>
(Petr has done it before.)</p>
<p><em>Do školy jsem chodil.</em><br>
(I’ve attended school.)</p>
<p>*Už jsem řídil auto.* (I’ve driven a car before.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>PRESENT imperfective</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Narrative of present action (Commentary)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Olda právě vchází do domu.</em><br>
(Olda is entering the building.)</p>
<p>*Novák střílí a dává gól.* (Novák shoots, and he scores!)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Future timetable</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Autobus odjíždí zítra v jednu hodinu odpoledne.<br>
(The bus leaves tomorrow at one pm.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Historical narrative [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Karel IV. zakládá univerzitu v roce 1348.</p>
<p>(Karel IV. founds the university in 1348.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>PRESENT repetitive</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Personal habit</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Ruce si myji vždy před jídlem.* (I always wash my hands before meals.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Present result of continuing past action</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Bydlíme v Praze už pět let.* (We’ve been living in Prague for 5 years now.)</p>
<p>*Pracuju u této firmy od roku 1990.* (I’ve been working for this company since 1990.)</p>
<p>*Touto společností létám už dlouho.* (I’ve been flying with this company for a long time.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Ability to do something</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Hrajete na piáno?* (Do/Can you play the piano?)</p>
<p><em>Petr běhá závodně.</em><br>
(Petr runs professionally.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Repetition</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Lodě odsud vyplouvají každé ráno.* (Every morning, the ships leave from here.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Likes and dislikes (with <em>rád, ráda, rádi</em>)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Rád chodím do divadla, ale nerad hraju fotbal.</em><br>
(I like going to the theater, but I don’t like playing football.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Statement of (scientific) fact [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Ptáci odlétají na podzim.</em><br>
(Birds fly away in the fall.)</p>
<p>Voda se vaří při 100 stupních.<br>
(Water boils at 100 degrees.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Prohibitions and negative imperatives [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Ať si sem nesedá.</p>
<p>(Let him not sit here.)</p>
<p>*Nezpívej tady.* (Don’t sing here.)</p>
<p>*Tady se chodí pomalu.* (One walks slowly here.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>FUTURE perfective</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Statement of plan</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Zítra ráno vstanu a uvařím si kávu.* (Tomorrow morning, I’ll get up and make some coffee.)</p>
<p><em>Úkol napíšu až pozítří.</em><br>
(I’ll write my homework the day after tomorrow.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Condition for future action/prediction of result of completion with <em>až</em> (when)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Až to udělá, bude mít volno.</em><br>
(When he does this, he’ll have time off.)</p>
<p>*Až přijde, zazpíváme mu písničku.* (When he comes, we’ll sing a song for him.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Condition for future action with <em>když, jestli</em>, or <em>pokud</em></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Když to udělá, nevyhodí ho.* (If he does it, they won’t fire him.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Instructions</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Nejdřív uvaříme rýži a potom nakrájíme zeleninu.* (First, we cook the rice and then we dice the vegetables.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Emphatic prohibitions/warnings with <em>ať</em> [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Ať sem ani nevkročí.</em><br>
(He’d better not even set foot in here.)</p>
<p><em>Jen ať mi to řekne.</em><br>
(Let him tell it to me, then.)</p>
<p><em>Ať si na to nikdo nesedne.</em><br>
(Let nobody sit on this.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Emphatic historical narrative (combined with imperfective) [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>A pak mu Pepa jednu vrazí a spadnou na zem, a …</em><br>
(And then Pepa smacks him, they fall on the ground and …)</p>
<p><em>Policisté ho sledují a pak ho zastřelí a on se válí na zemi.</em><br>
(The police follow him, then shoot him and he’s lying on the floor.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Repeated action with (often negative) emphasis [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>On si sem klidně každé ráno přijde a sedne si na židli.</em><br>
(He will come here every morning without so much as by your leave and sit on the chair.)</p>
<p><em>Nikdy nepozdraví.</em><br>
(He never says hello.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Emphatic plan for repeated future action [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Příští rok půjdu do kina alespoň jednou týdně.</p>
<p>(Next year, I will go see a movie at least once a week.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Polite acceptance / expression of willingness (with <em>rád</em>) [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Rád přijdu zítra na večeři.* (I will be happy to come to dinner tomorrow.)</p>
<p>Rád to udělá.</p>
<p>(He will be happy to do it.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>FUTURE imperfective</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Future parallel or interrupted action</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Vy budete vařit polévku a my budeme připravovat salát.* (You’ll be cooking the soup and we’ll be preparing the salad.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Plan for a block of time</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Zítra se budu učit od jedné do dvou.</p>
<p>(Tomorrow, I’ll study from one to two.)</p>
<p>*Příští rok budeme celé léto cestovat.* (Next year, we’ll spend the whole summer traveling.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Background of future action with <em>když</em> and <em>až</em>, <em>zatímco</em></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Když bude psát úkol, bude mu hrát hudba.* (While he’ll be doing the homework, the music will be playing.)</p>
<p><em>Až bude psát úkol, nebude s nikým mluvit.</em><br>
(When he’s writing his homework, he won’t speak to anybody.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>FUTURE repetitive</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Future repeated action (Condition)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Příští rok budu každý týden chodit do kina.</em><br>
(Next year, I will go to the movies every week.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"><p>Time non-specific future wish [ADV]</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Snad příští rok nebude tolik pršet.</em><br>
(Hopefully, next year, it won’t rain so much.)</p>
<p>*Příští týden budu více cvičit.* (I’ll exercise more next week.)</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-comparing-czech-tense-and-aspect-with-english">Comparing Czech Tense and Aspect with English</h3>
<h3 id="reader-content-czech-to-english">Czech to English</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-98">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>Czech</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>English</strong></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>tense – tense</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>present imperfective</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>present continuous</p>
<p>present simple</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left"><p>past perfective</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>past simple (cont.)</p>
<p>present perfect simple</p>
<p>past perfect</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>

</tr>

<h2 id="reader-content-aspect-of-verbs">Aspect of verbs</h2>
<p>This section describes some general facts about verbal aspect. The following sections describe in more detail its meaning and how it is formed. As we saw above, aspect only appears in conjunction with a tense (but there are some exceptions that make it useful to understand the principles of aspect on their own).</p>
<p>Here are some facts about aspect of verbs to give you more background:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Actions (running, reading, doing) and states (thinking, feeling) are described by verbs.</p></li>
<li><p>In Czech, each action can be described by one of three verbs: <em>perfective</em>, <em>imperfective</em> or <em>repetitive</em>. These are called aspects.</p></li>
<li><p>There are no neutral verbs in Czech. Each verb you find in the dictionary describes one of the three aspects.</p></li>
<li><p>The “aspect of a verb” determines how that verb describes an activity.</p></li>
<li><p>Each verb can only be used in one of the three tenses: past, present and future.</p></li>
<li><p>Because they describe the action as a whole which cannot all happen at the present point, perfective verbs cannot be used as a present tense.</p></li>
<li><p>The vast majority of <em>imperfective</em> verbs are also <em>repetitive</em>. Only verbs of motion have a special repetitive form.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Here are some advanced facts about aspect:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Sometimes the aspect of the action changes even the meaning of the verb (sometimes slightly and sometimes beyond all recognition). For example, <em>číst</em> [read] and <em>přečíst</em> [finish reading], or <em>žít</em> [live] and <em>přežít</em> [survive]. Another example, where the completed action is described by two different verbs in English is <em>volit</em> [vote] and <em>zvolit</em> [elect].</p></li>
<li><p>If the perfective verb in the imperfective/perfective pair has a prefix, its meaning is likely to be slightly different. Some prefixes like <em>při-</em> change the meaning more radically, while some such as <em>u-</em> change it only slightly or not at all (cf. dělat/udělat).</p></li>
<li><p>If the imperfective verb in the imperfective/perfective pair has a prefix, the meaning of both verbs is likely to be fairly similar.</p></li>
<li><p>Some verbs occur in one aspect only. Some states, feelings and sensations only have the imperfective aspect. (<em>Chtít, být, mít</em> are some of the examples.)</p></li>
<li><p>Very few verbs (none of the common ones and mostly of foreign origin) describe all three aspects. For example, both <em>graduovat</em> [graduate] and <em>definovat</em> [define] can be used in all three aspects.</p></li>
<li><p>The aspect of verbs shows up even in nouns and adjectives (quite a few – nearly all derived from verbs such as <em>the thinking of this man</em>, <em>the thinking man</em>).</p></li>
<li><p>Some verbs don’t have a true pair. Such as <em>sedět</em> [sit] seems to have its perfective pair in <em>sednout si</em> but <em>sednout si</em> has its own imperfective/repetitive pair in <em>sedat si</em>. The latter two describe the process of sitting down, while the <em>sedět</em> describes the process of being seated.</p></li>
<li><p>Some verbs form a false pair. They describe different aspects of the same action but grammatically they are separate and often have another pair. For example, <em>hledat</em> [search, look for] seems to be the imperfective aspect of <em>najít</em> [find] but it isn’t because the perfective pair of <em>hledat</em> is <em>vyhledat</em> [look up] and the imperfective pair of <em>najít</em> is <em>nacházet</em> (which is also the repetitive form).</p></li>
</ul>
<h3 id="reader-content-actions-and-verbs-meaning-of-aspect">Actions and verbs (Meaning of aspect)</h3>
<p>Verbs describe actions. Actions generally have three parts. The onset, progress, and finish part.</p>
<p><img src="media/image3-wmf.png"></p>
<p>Czech verbs pick different aspects of actions, and are therefore classified into three classes. So called aspects of verbs. They are:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-100">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>**Perfective Aspect** concerns the action as a whole, or the finish part only of the action.</p>
<p>A consequence of sthis is, that verbs of this aspect have always future meaning in their conjugated form. For instance, <em>přijdu</em> means <em>I will come</em></p></td>
<td><p><img src="media/image4-wmf.png"> |</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>**Imperfective Aspect** concerns progress of the action without specifying anything about its beginning or end.</p>
<p>Verbs of this aspect describe an ongoing process when conjugated and to use <em>budu</em> plus infinitive to indicate the future tense. In the past they describe something happening over a period of time.</p></td>
<td><p><img src="media/image5-wmf.png"> |</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>**Repetitive Aspect** takes any action many times.</p>
<p>This aspect often describes a habit. An example is: <em>Kam chodíš hrát tenis?</em> meaning <em>Where do you usually go to play tennis?</em> This aspect is often grouped with the <em>imperfective.</em></p></td>
<td><p><img src="media/image6-wmf.png"> |</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-how-to-tell-the-aspect-of-a-verb">How to tell the aspect of a verb</h3>
<p>When you learn a new verb, it is wise to learn its aspect because in general, there’s no way to tell what aspect a verb is just by looking at it. There are a few rules which could guide you in learning new words.</p>
<p>You usually cannot tell 100% if a verb is perfective or imperfective when you see it but if you see a pair of verbs you can usually tell which has what aspect. Except for verbs of motion, repetitive aspect is always the same as imperfective.</p>
<p>There are three ways different aspect verbs are differentiated:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-101">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Endings</strong></td>
<td align="left">The imperfective and perfective verbs have a different ending.</td>
<td align="left">opra<strong>vovat</strong> > oprav<strong>it</strong></td>
<td align="left">to correct</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Prefix</strong></td>
<td align="left">The perfective verb has a prefix and the imperfective does not.</td>
<td align="left">dělat > <strong>u</strong>dělat</td>
<td align="left">to do</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Different words</strong></td>
<td align="left">Different words are used for imperfective and perfective verbs.<br>
(comparatively rare)</td>
<td align="left">brát > vzít</td>
<td align="left">to take</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">!</td>
<td>Very often the verb with the prefix can be translated into English using the get + -en like in udělat = to get done, poznat = to get to know, podívat se = to take a look signifying that the action only happens once.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">!</td>
<td>With verbs describing meals (to dine etc.) and with quite a few other verbs as well, the perfective adds <em>se</em>. <em>obědvat –naobědvat se</em></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-105">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">🕭</td>
<td align="left">Very often the prefix changes the meaning of the verb. For instance, <em>jít</em> (to go) with the prefix <em>na-</em> changes to <em>najít</em> (to find).</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-how-does-aspect-translate-into-english">How does aspect translate into English?</h3>
<p>There is no straightforward way of translating the difference between imperfective, repetive and perfective from Czech into English unless aspect is combined with a tense. Very often, English needs two words to express completeness.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-106">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="3">One word with two aspects in Czech</td>
<td colspan="2">Two different words in English</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">dívat se</td>
<td>podívat se</td>
<td>to look</td>
<td>to take a look</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">znát</td>
<td>poznat</td>
<td>to know</td>
<td>to get to know</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">učit se</td>
<td>naučit se</td>
<td>to study</td>
<td>to learn</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">milovat</td>
<td>zamilovat se</td>
<td>to love</td>
<td>to fall in love</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">volat</td>
<td>zavolat</td>
<td>to call</td>
<td>to give a call</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>!</td>
<td colspan="4">Notice, that here the difference between imperfective and perfective is one of a process or action taking place over some time, and actions that take place once in an instant.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-aspect-of-nouns-and-adjectives-formed-from-verbs">Aspect of nouns and adjectives formed from verbs</h3>
<p>The difference in aspect also shows up in many nouns and adjective formed from verbs (so called deverbatives). Here are a few examples.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-107">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Perfective</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Imperfective</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Repetitive</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Adjectives</strong></td>
<td align="left">pochválený (praised once)</td>
<td align="left">chválený (being praised now)</td>
<td align="left">chválený (praised often)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">jdoucí (walking now)</td>
<td align="left">chodící (walking often)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Nouns</strong></td>
<td align="left">přispění (contribution)</td>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">přispívání (regular contribution)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left">probuzení (the whole of waking up)</td>
<td align="left">probouzení (the process of waking up)</td>
<td align="left">probouzení (regular waking up)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-pairs-and-hubs-of-verbs">Pairs and Hubs of Verbs</h3>
<h4 id="reader-content-verbs-organized-in-pairs">Verbs organized in pairs</h4>
<p>Most Czech verbs have at least one counterpart in a different aspect. Since the imperfective verb is almost always used as the repetitive as well, most verbs can be said to occur in pairs of <em>perfective</em> and <em>imperfective</em> verbs. A perfect example of such a pair is <em>vzít</em> vs. <em>brát</em>. <em>Vzít</em> describes the the act of taking as a whole while <em>brát</em> describes its process or repetition. But many pairs do not describe exactly the same meaning, because the nature of the completed action is different from its progress. See the list of top 150 verbs for some good examples.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-verbs-organized-in-hubs">Verbs organized in hubs</h4>
<p>Very often, an <em>imperfective</em> verb serves as the basis of a number of <em>perfective</em> verbs which are formed by adding a prefix. There are twenty prefixes available for this. So there may seem to be virtually twenty counterparts to any verb. But only the verbs of motion can take on all of the prefixes each. Often, one of the verbs in the hub has virtually the same meaning as the imperfective verb but and others have a different meaning. Very often they become separate verbs and find their own imperfective/repetitive verb to pair up with. These cannot really be said to be related to the original imperfective verb other than historically. The hub is in fact a collection of pairs.</p>
<p>Here is an example of a verb with a hub. The imperfective verb <em>psát</em> (write) has a true perfective pair in <em>napsat</em>. However, using prefixes, seven more verbs can be created from the original verb. All of these differ somewhat in meaning and some differ substantially (e.g. <em>podepsat</em> to sign). All of these non-true aspect pairs have found their own imperfective pair verb to be able to express the progress of the new meaning.</p>
<p><strong>Note 1:</strong> There is no verb <em>pisovat</em> nor is it possible to make <em>napisovat</em>.</p>
<p><strong>Note 2:</strong> Most of the prefixed perfectives can take on the reflexive <em>se</em> or <em>si</em> optionally. <em>Upsat se</em> can only appear with the <em>se</em>. <em>Dopisovat</em> can also occur with <em>si</em> in which case it means to <em>lead a correspondence</em>.</p>
<p><img src="media/image7-wmf.png"></p>

<h3 id="reader-content-differences-between-pairs-of-similar-prefixes">Differences between pairs of similar prefixes</h3>
<p>Describing these differences in words is cumbersome. It is best to compare meanings and try to internalize the difference for own use.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-u--vs.-do--as-prefixes-of-completion">u- vs. do- as prefixes of completion</h4>
<p>udělat (do once and completely) vs. dodělat (finish something previously unfinished)</p>
<p>ujít (complete a distance) vs. dojít (complete, finish walking, also idiom-run out)</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-od--vs.-roz--vs.-u--vs.-vy--vs.-na--vs.-do--vs.-z--as-prefixes-of-destruction">od- vs. roz- vs. u- vs. vy- vs. na- vs. do- vs. z- as prefixes of destruction</h4>
<p>odlomit (break a bit off), rozlomit (break into pieces[in half]), ulomit (break off a piece), vylomit (break out), nalomit (partially break off), dolomit (finish breaking off), zlomit (break irreparably [in half])</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-vy--vs.-roz--vs.-od--vs.-na--vs.-za--as-prefixes-of-beginning">vy- vs. roz- vs. od- vs. na- vs. za- as prefixes of beginning</h4>
<p>vyjít (set out), rozejít se (start walking), odejít (walk away from), našlápnout (step out)</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-po--vs.-popo--vs.-na--vs.-u--as-prefixes-of-degree">po- vs. popo- vs. na- vs. u- as prefixes of degree</h4>
<p>poskočit (jump a little), poposkočit (jump very little), nalomit (break a little), ulomit (break off a little piece)</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-forming-tenses">Forming Tenses</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-past-tenses">Past tenses</h3>
<p>All three past tenses use the <strong>–l</strong> form of the verb and the auxiliary (helping verb) <em>být</em>. They differ only in whether they will choose a perfective, imperfective or repetitive verb.</p>

</table>
<ul>
<li><p>The <strong>-l</strong> form (equivalent of the English <strong>–ed</strong> form )of the verb in Czech is formed by stripping off the <em>–t</em> in the infinitive and substituting <em>–l</em> (<em>děla<strong>t</strong></em> 🡺 <em>děla<strong>l</strong></em>).</p></li>
<li><p>The subject is expressed by an auxilliary (helping) verb – a form of být. The he/she form does not use the auxiliary. (dělal <strong>jsem</strong>, dělal <strong>jsi</strong>, dělal, dělali <strong>jsme</strong>, dělali <strong>jste</strong>, dělali)</p></li>
<li><p>The gender of the subject is expressed through an ending of the verb’s past form. <strong><em>–0</em></strong> for masculine; <strong><em>-a</em></strong> for feminine, <strong><em>-o</em></strong> for neuter. E.g. <em>dělal</em> – he did, <em>dělal<strong>a</strong></em> – she did, <em>dělal<strong>o</strong></em> – it did.</p></li>
</ul>
<h4 id="reader-content-how-to-order-words-in-the-past-tense">How to order words in the past tense?</h4>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-110">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">1</td>
<td align="left">2</td>
<td align="left">3</td>
<td align="left">4</td>
<td align="left">5</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Já</td>
<td align="left">jsem</td>
<td align="left">to</td>
<td align="left">koupil</td>
<td align="left">včera.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Koupil</td>
<td align="left">jsem</td>
<td align="left">si</td>
<td align="left">to</td>
<td align="left">včera.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Díval</td>
<td align="left">jsem</td>
<td align="left">se</td>
<td align="left">na televizi.</td>
<td align="left"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Koupil</td>
<td align="left">jsi</td>
<td align="left">mu</td>
<td align="left">to</td>
<td align="left">včera.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The auxiliary <em>jsem, jsi, jsme, jste</em> are always in the second place. The <em>se</em> and <em>si</em> follow, preceding other monosyllabic words like <em>to, mu, jí, mi</em> etc.</p>

<h3 id="reader-content-how-to-be-polite-in-the-past-tense">How to be polite in the past tense?</h3>
<p>In the polite form (which in the present tense corresponds to the plural) only the auxiliarry <em>jsem, jsi, jsme, jste</em> is in plural, and the verb has the singular form.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-114">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">jak <em>jste</em> se měl / měla</td>
<td align="left">jak <em>jste</em> se měl<em>i</em></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">how have you been (professor)</td>
<td align="left">how have you guys been</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-past-tense-and-verbs-with-se-and-si">Past tense and verbs with <em>se</em> and <em>si</em></h4>
<p>If reflexive verbs like <em>dívat se</em> or <em>koupit si</em> are in the past tense, the <em>se</em> or <em>si</em> combine with the auxiliary <em>jsi</em>.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-115">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">dívat se</td>
<td align="left">se + jsi <strong>></strong> <strong>ses</strong></td>
<td align="left">díval(a) ses</td>
<td align="left">watch</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">koupit si</td>
<td align="left">si + jsi <strong>></strong> <strong>sis</strong></td>
<td align="left">koupil(a) sis</td>
<td align="left">buy</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-past-tense-short-forms-with-kdo-co-and-other-question-words">Past tense short forms with Kdo, Co and other question words</h4>
<p>Optionally, question words can combine with a shortened form of the auxiliary <em>jsi</em>. <em>Kdo</em>? and <em>Co</em>? can be combined in all their forms.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-116">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Co jsi tam dělal?</td>
<td align="left">co + jsi <strong>></strong> cos</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Cos</strong> tam dělal?</td>
<td>What did you do there?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Kde jsi byl?</td>
<td align="left">kde + jsi <strong>></strong> kdes</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Kdes</strong> byl?</td>
<td>Where have you been?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Kam jsi šel?</td>
<td align="left">kam + jsi <strong>></strong> kams</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Kams</strong> šel?</td>
<td>Where did you go?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Kdys jsi přišel?</td>
<td align="left">kdy + jsi <strong>></strong> kdys</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Kdys</strong> přišel?</td>
<td>When did you come?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Koho jsi viděl?</td>
<td align="left">koho + jsi <strong>></strong> kohos</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Kohos</strong> viděl?</td>
<td>Who did you see?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Proč jsi to udělal?</td>
<td align="left">proč + jsi <strong>></strong> pročs</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Pročs</strong> to udělal?</td>
<td>Why did you do it?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Jak jsi tam jel?</td>
<td align="left">jak + jsi <strong>></strong> jaks</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Jaks</strong> tam jel?</td>
<td>How did you go there?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">To jsi neměl dělat.</td>
<td align="left">to + jsi <strong>></strong> tos</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Tos</strong> neměl dělat.</td>
<td>You shouldn’t have done that.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-habitual-past-tense-i-used-to-va-form">Habitual past tense – I used to … (–va– form)</h3>
<p>To express feelings equivalent to the English <em>used to … ,</em> Czech uses a special infix <strong>–va-</strong> which is inserted before the infinitival <strong>–t</strong> and lengthening the vowel before it thus creating two typical habitual sounding endings: <strong><em>-ávat</em></strong> or <strong><em>-ívat</em></strong>.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-117">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>-ávat</strong></p></td>
<td><p>Rodiče mi to říkávali.</p></td>
<td><p>My parents used to tell me that.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>-ívat</strong></p></td>
<td><p>To jsem si myslíval taky.</p>
<p>Tady chodívala moje žena.</p></td>
<td><p>I used to think that, too.</p>
<p>My wife used to walk here.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>!</p></td>
<td colspan="3"><p>Verbs whose root contains <strong>–ívat</strong> or <strong>–ávat</strong> (e.g., zp<strong>íval</strong> – sang, d<strong>ával</strong> – gave) add the habitual past ending to form <strong>–ívávat, -ávávat</strong> (e.g., zpí<strong>vával</strong> – used to sing, dá<strong>vával</strong> – used to give).</p>
<p>This form can also be used in the present and future (although in these tenses it is not as common).</p>
<p>Some verbs have a special form for this function which can be further emphasized by the infix, for example: <em>číst – čítat – čítávat</em> (read), <em>vidět – vídat – vídávat</em> (see), <em>slyšet – slýchat – slýchávat</em>, etc.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>!</p></td>
<td colspan="3"><p>This –va– form is often confused with the repetitive aspect but it is in fact a completely separate thing. It can be added even to repetitive verbs such as <em>jezdit</em> to make <em>jezdívat</em>.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>!</p></td>
<td colspan="3"><p>Any number of –vá– can be added to make –váva– if you want to indicate more infrequent repetition. A verb such as <em>chodívávávávávat</em> is then theoretically possible but only used in extremely emphatic and informal speech.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-future-tenses">Future tenses</h3>
<h4 id="reader-content-perfective-future-such-as-udělat">Perfective future – such as <em>udělat</em></h4>
<p>The formation of this tense is perfectly simple (or depending on your perspective really difficult). All that needs to be done is to conjugate a <em>perfective</em> verb (see conjugation rules earlier). If you find conjugation difficult, then you’ll find forming the perfective future difficult as well.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-imperfective-and-repetitive-future-budu-infinitive">Imperfective and repetitive future – <em>budu</em> + infinitive</h4>
<p>Both the imperfective and repetitive future tenses are formed the same way. Simply add the appropriate form of the future of <em>be</em> (budu) to either a repetitive or an imperfective verb.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-118">





<tbody>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r1c1" scope="col" align="left"><strong>Kdo</strong></th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r1c2" scope="col" align="left"><strong>…will be</strong></th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r1c3" scope="col" align="left"><strong>+ infinitive (ipf)</strong></th>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r2c1" scope="row" align="left"><strong>já</strong></th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r2c2" align="left">budu</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r2c3" align="left">dělat</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r3c1" scope="row" align="left"><strong>ty</strong></th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r3c2" align="left">budeš</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r3c3" align="left">dělat</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r4c1" scope="row" align="left"><strong>on/ona/ono</strong></th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r4c2" align="left">bude</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r4c3" align="left">dělat</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r5c1" scope="row" align="left"><strong>my</strong></th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r5c2" align="left">budeme</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r5c3" align="left">dělat</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r6c1" scope="row" align="left"><strong>vy</strong></th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r6c2" align="left">budete</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r6c3" align="left">dělat</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r7c1" scope="row" align="left"><strong>oni</strong></th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r7c2" align="left">budou</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7efad2db147009372b57-r7c3" align="left">dělat</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-irregular-future-tense-without-budu">Irregular future tense without <em>budu</em></h4>
<p class="heading" id="reader-content-verb-být-to-be">Verb být (to be)</p>
<p>The verb <em>být</em> (to be) uses its future form only. The auxiliary budu is not used again.</p>
<p>budu být <strong>></strong> budu, budeš …</p>
<p class="heading" id="reader-content-verbs-of-motion">Verbs of motion</p>
<p>Verbs of motion form irregular future tense. For example, it is impossible to say <em>budu jet</em> and the form <em>pojedu</em> must be used instead. There is no infinitive <em>pojet</em>, however.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>jít <strong>></strong> půjdu, půjdeš, … (to go)</p>
<p><em>jet</em> <strong>></strong> <em>pojedu, pojedeš,</em> … (to go [by vehicle])</p>
<p>letět <strong>></strong> poletím, poletíš, … (to fly)</p>
<p><em>běžet</em> <strong>></strong> <em>poběžím, poběžíš,</em> … (to run)</p>
<p>lézt <strong>></strong> polezu, polezeš, … (to crawl)</p>
</blockquote>
<p>There are around 20 more or less obvious verbs of motion. See Verbs of Motion below.</p>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> <em>po-</em> also functions as an ordinary perfectivizing verbal prefix (see below).</p>
<p class="heading" id="reader-content-marginal-verbs-of-motion">Marginal verbs of motion:</p>
<p>There are a few verbs that only behave as verbs of motion in the irregular future tense:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>růst <strong>></strong> porostu, porosteš, … (to grow)</p>
<p>kvést <strong>></strong> pokvetu, pokveteš, … (to bloom)</p>
<p>trvat <strong>></strong> potrvám, potrváš, … (to last, take time)</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> The imperfective future tense can also be formed with <em>budu</em> for these verbs.</p>
<p class="heading" id="reader-content-verbs-of-perception">Verbs of perception</p>
<p>Some other verbs (mostly related to perception) do not form the <em>budu</em> future tense. E.g. <em>vidět</em> (to see) where the future of <em>vidím</em> is <em>uvidím.</em> (<em>Budu vidět</em> is also possible but it means <em>I will be able to see</em>). The same holds for <em>slyšet</em> (to hear) and <em>cítit</em> (to feel, to sense, to smell).</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-summary-of-tenses-for-four-most-frequent-verbs">Summary of tenses for four most frequent verbs</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-119">









<tbody>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>dnes</strong> (today)</p></td>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>včera</strong> (yesterday)</p></td>

<p><strong>!</strong> In past tense singular for women <strong><em>a</em></strong> is appended (<em>byl – byla, chtěl jsem – chtěla jsem</em>)</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-sequence-of-tenses">Sequence of Tenses</h3>
<p>In Czech, when you say that <em>someone</em> <em>wrote, said, thought, mentioned, heard … that</em> the tense you use is the tense used by the speaker of the original sentence.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-120">

<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-86ee470558282c9df7e4-r1c3" scope="col" align="left">English</th>

<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-86ee470558282c9df7e4-r2c1" scope="row" align="left">Řekl, že přijede zítra.</th>

<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-86ee470558282c9df7e4-r3c2" align="left">He said that he <em>arrived</em> yesterday.</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-86ee470558282c9df7e4-r3c3" align="left">He said that he had arrived the day before.</td>

<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-86ee470558282c9df7e4-r4c2" align="left">He said that he <em>is reading</em> a book.</td>

<h2 id="reader-content-voice">Voice</h2>
<p>The category of <em>voice</em> distinguishes between the <em>active</em> and <em>passive</em>. While in English, this is a very important distinction, in Czech it is not used as often, and the few passive verbs that are used more frequently you learned as adjectives. The meaning of passive as in <em>It was decided</em>. is usually expressed by the reflexive <em>se</em> in Czech (<em>Rozhodlo se.</em>). See <em>Uses of SE</em>.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-passive">Passive</h3>
<p>The passive form is very simple in Czech. It is rarely used in its true form. It is rather transformed into an adjective (see below).</p>
<p>The key to formation of the passive is the past form from which the <strong><em>–l</em></strong> is stripped and replaced with <strong><em>–n</em></strong>. If the last consonant is preceded with an <em>–a-</em> it is lengthened to <em>–á-</em>. Words ending in <em>– nul</em> are changed to <em>–nut</em>.</p>
<p>Passive is gender specific which means that <strong><em>–a, -o</em></strong> are used to mark the gender of the subjects.</p>
<p>The syntax of the passive is similar to English. It is used after the copula and the agent is expressed by <em>instrumental (Kým?Čím?)</em> as in <em>Jěžíš byl ukřižován Židy.</em> (Jesus was crucified by the Jews.)</p>

<h3 id="reader-content-how-to-form-the-equivalents-of-the-english-ed-and-ing-forms-as-in-planned-planning">How to form the equivalents of the English –ed and –ing forms (as in <em>planned</em>, <em>planning</em>)</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-122">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>to plan_</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>planned</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>planned projects,</p>
<p>it will be well planned</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>planning</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>good planning is important</p>
<p>strategic planning</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>plánovat</p>
<p>naplánovat</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>plánovaný</p>
<p>naplánovaný</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>plánovan… (ipf.) projekty</p>
<p>bude to dobře naplánované (pf.)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>plánov<strong>á</strong>ní</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>dobré plánování je důležité</p>
<p>strategické plánování</p></td>
</tr>

</table>
<p>The <em>–ed</em> form you make and adjective out of a verb while the –<em>ing</em> form serves to make nouns.</p>
<p>You make the right form using the past tense form of the verb as below.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-123">






<tbody>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r1c1" scope="col"></th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r1c2" scope="col"><strong>-ed</strong> (he –ed)</th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r1c3" scope="col" align="left"><strong>-ed</strong> (a –ed thing)</th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r1c4" scope="col" align="left">-ing</th>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r2c1" scope="row">-ovat</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r2c2">-oval</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r2c3" align="left">-ovaný, -ovaná, -ované</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r2c4" align="left">-ování</td>
</tr>

<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r3c1" scope="row">-it</th>

<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r3c3" align="left">-ený</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r3c4" align="left">-ení</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r4c1" scope="row">-at</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r4c2">-al</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r4c3" align="left">-aný</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r4c4" align="left">-<em>á</em>ní</td>

<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-7d136c2cc069610b368d-r5c4" align="left">-nutí</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Note that most of the adjectival <em>–ed</em> forms are made of perfective verbs to describe accomplishment of the action.</p>
<p>The equivalent of the passive is the same as the noun form without the ending –<em>í</em>. The difference in gender is preserved by –<em>0</em> (m.), <em>--a</em> (f.), --<em>o</em> (n.) in singular and –<em>i</em> (ma.), <em>--y</em> (mi., f.), --<em>a</em> (n.) In present day Czech the adjective and the passive forms are used almost interchangeably.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-how-to-form-equivalent-of-ing-as-in-a-reading-man">How to form equivalent of –<em>ing</em> as in <em>a reading man</em>:</h3>
<ul>
<li><p>For this you need the 3<sup>rd</sup> person plural form of the verb to which you add an ending –<em>cí</em> as in <em>čtoucí muž</em> (a reading man), or <em>budoucí čas</em> (future tense).</p></li>
<li><p>Note, however, a difference between <em>plánovací oddělení</em> (department of planning—formed from the infinitive to indicate designation) vs. <em>plánující oddělení</em> (a planning department—formed regularly to indicate activity)</p></li>
</ul>
<h2 id="reader-content-mood">Mood</h2>
<p>There are three <em>moods</em> a Czech verb can be in. <em>Indicative –</em> simple statements; <em>Conditional</em> – statements of what <em>would be</em>; <em>Imperative</em> – direct commands. Unlike English, Czech uses very different forms of verbs to express commands.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-imperative">Imperative</h3>
<p id="reader-content-if-you-want-someone-to-do-something-you-tell-them-to-do-it."><strong>If you want someone to do something you tell them to do it.</strong></p>
<p>For example:</p>
<p>They repeat, <strong>repeat</strong> too!!!!!! Opakuj<strong>í</strong>, <strong>opakuj</strong> taky!</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Opakuj<strong>í, opakujte</strong> taky!</p>
<p>They repeat, <strong>let’s repeat</strong> too!!! Opakuj<strong>í, opakujme</strong> taky!</p>
</blockquote>
<p>In English you just name the action, in Czech you give the verb a special form and you add <strong>–te</strong> to it if you want more than one person to do something, or <strong>–me</strong> if you want to say <em>Let’s …</em> .</p>
<p id="reader-content-to-do-it-with-the-right-forms"><strong>To do it with the right forms:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Take the third person plural and strip off the ending <strong>–ou</strong> or <strong>–í</strong></p></li>
<li><p>The consonants <em>d, t, a</em> are softened into <em>ď,ť, ň</em> (Exx. <em>jedou</em> <em>je<strong>ď</strong>!</em>)</p></li>
<li><p>The ending <strong>–aj</strong> changes into <strong>–ej</strong> (Exx. <em>dělaj</em>[í] <strong>></strong> <em>dělej!</em>)</p></li>
<li><p><strong>–e-</strong> verbs are divided into two classes because it would be difficult to pronounce some of them without adding <strong>–i,</strong> (Exx. <em>začni!</em> <em>jdou</em> <strong>></strong> <em>jdi!</em>)</p></li>
<li><p>-<strong>i</strong> can also be added to some other verbs like (Exx. <em>myslíš</em> <strong>></strong> <em>mysli!</em>)</p></li>
<li><p>To command more people ( plural ) just add <strong>–te</strong> to the singular form</p></li>
<li><p>The words ending in <strong>–i</strong> change from <strong>–ite</strong> to <strong>–ete</strong> (Exx. <em>jdi</em> <strong>></strong> <em>jděte!!</em>)</p></li>
<li><p>To say <em>Let us do something!</em> you use <strong>–me</strong> in place of <strong>–te</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<h4 id="reader-content-telling-someone-to-come-along-or-close-to-you">Telling someone to come along, or close to you</h4>
<blockquote>
<p>Come here!!! Pojď sem!!!</p>
<p>Come with me!!! Pojeď se mnou!!!</p>
</blockquote>
<p>To do this you use the prefix <strong>po-</strong>. <strong><em>po-</em></strong> can be used with verbs of motion such as, <em>pojď</em>, <em>pojeď</em>, <em>poběž</em> parallel to their regular form <em>jdi</em>, <em>jeď</em>, <em>běž</em>. It implies direction to[wards] (me – the speaker) or together with (me – the speaker).</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-summary-of-the-imperative">Summary of the Imperative</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-124">






<tbody>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-92cb77a18d83d5216a43-r1c1" scope="col" align="left">infinitive</th>

<tr>

<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-92cb77a18d83d5216a43-r3c3" align="left">dělej</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-92cb77a18d83d5216a43-r3c4" align="left">dělejte</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-92cb77a18d83d5216a43-r4c1" scope="row" align="left">mluvit</th>

<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-92cb77a18d83d5216a43-r5c1" scope="row" align="left">pít</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-92cb77a18d83d5216a43-r5c2" align="left">pij<strong>ou</strong></td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-92cb77a18d83d5216a43-r5c3" align="left">pij</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-92cb77a18d83d5216a43-r5c4" align="left">pijte</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<h3 id="reader-content-infinitive-as-a-command">Infinitive as a command</h3>
<p>The infinitive can also be used in prohibitions and in emphatic commands as in <em>neparkovat!</em> (do not park) or <em>mlčet!</em> (be quiet). It is mostly used in strong spoken language like <em>sednout</em> (sit!!!), although it can also be seen on signs marking private property like <em>nevstupovat</em> (do not enter).</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-ať-as-indirect-command-or-telling-somebody-to-do-something"><em>Ať</em> as indirect command or telling somebody to do something</h3>
<p>AŤ + verb in <em>on/a/o</em>-form or <em>oni-</em>form</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-128">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p>Řekni mu,</p></td>
<td><p><strong>ať</strong> píše.</p></td>
<td><p>Tell him</p></td>
<td><p>to write.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p>Řekni mu,</p></td>
<td><p><strong>ať</strong> dělá.</p></td>
<td><p>Tell him</p></td>
<td><p>to work.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p>Řekni mu,</p></td>
<td><p><strong>ať</strong> pracuje.</p></td>
<td><p>Tell him</p></td>
<td><p>to work.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p>Řekni mu,</p></td>
<td><p><strong>ať</strong> přijdou.</p></td>
<td><p>Tell him</p></td>
<td><p>to come.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>!</p></td>
<td colspan="4"><p>This construction is also used to express wishes.</p>
<p><strong><em>ať žije král</em></strong> long live the king</p>
<p><strong><em>ať je mír</em></strong> let there be peace</p>
<p><strong><em>ať se ti všechno daří</em></strong> may everything go well for you</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-subjunctive-as-indirect-command">Subjunctive as indirect command</h3>
<p>Another way to tell somebody to do something is to use the subjunctive. The core meaning of the subjunctive is expressing wishes and requests.</p>
<p>It uses the same basic form as <em>conditional</em> (below).</p>
<p>ABY + verb in past tense</p>

ono</td>

<td align="left">vy</td>
<td align="left">oni</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>abych</strong></td>

<td align="left"><strong>aby</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>abychom</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>abyste</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>aby</strong></td>
</tr>

<h3 id="reader-content-conditional">Conditional</h3>
<h4 id="reader-content-i-you-they-would-be">I, you, … , they would be</h4>
<ul>
<li>the conditional in Czech is formed using the past tense form of the verb and a particle <em>by</em></li>
</ul>
<p>bych, bys, … , byste byl (a,o,i)</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-132">








<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">byl(a)</td>
<td align="left">bych</td>
<td align="left">I would be</td>
<td align="left">byli</td>
<td align="left">bychom</td>
<td align="left">we would be</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">byl(a)</td>
<td align="left">bys</td>
<td align="left">you would be</td>
<td align="left">byli</td>
<td align="left">byste</td>
<td align="left">you guys would be</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">byl(a,o)</td>
<td align="left">by</td>
<td align="left">he(she, it) would be</td>
<td align="left">byli</td>
<td align="left">by</td>
<td align="left">they would be</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-133">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>!</p></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p><strong>byl (a)</strong> <strong><em>byste</em></strong> you would be ( the polite form)</p></li>
<li><p><strong><em>bychom</em></strong> belongs to literary Czech, it is almost never used in spoken language <em>bysme</em> is what people use</p></li>
<li><p>sometimes people say <em>by jsme, by jste</em> to sound literary but it is wrong</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-the-word-order-for-the-conditional-particle-by">the word order for the conditional particle <em>by</em></h4>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-134">














<tbody>
<tr>
<td>1.</td>
<td>2.</td>
<td colspan="2">…</td>
<td colspan="2">….</td>
<td colspan="2"></td>
<td>1.</td>
<td>2.</td>
<td>3.</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Jela</td>
<td>bych</td>
<td>do</td>
<td colspan="3">Brna.</td>
<td colspan="2"></td>
<td>Učil</td>
<td>bych</td>
<td>se.</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Zítra</td>
<td>bych</td>
<td colspan="2">jela</td>
<td>do</td>
<td colspan="2">Brna.</td>
<td></td>
<td>Večer</td>
<td>bych</td>
<td>se</td>
<td>učil.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<ul>
<li><p>the rules for using conditional differ slightly in Czech and in English</p></li>
<li><p>the conditional expresses</p></li>
<li><p>a) polite request Měl byste dnes čas? Zavřel bys okno? Mohl byste přijít zítra?</p></li>
<li><p>b) asking for permission Mohl bych si zavolat? Nemohla by to udělat zítra?</p></li>
<li><p>c) person´s wish Chtěli bychom jet do Londýna. Ráda bych šla na koncert.</p></li>
<li><p>d) conditional statement Jeli bychom na hory, ale museli bychom si koupit jízdenky.</p></li>
<li><p>e) advice Měl bych jít domů. Měli bychom víc studovat.</p></li>
<li><p>f) hypothetical condition Kdybych, kdybys, kdyby, kdybychom, kdybyste</p></li>
</ul>
<h4 id="reader-content-if-.-.-.">If . . .</h4>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-135">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">If ...</td>
<td align="left">... would ...</td>
<td align="left">Kdyby ...</td>
<td align="left">... by ...</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">If I had a car,</td>
<td align="left">I would go tPrague.</td>
<td align="left">Kdybych měl auto,</td>
<td align="left">jel bych do Prahy.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">If I had time</td>
<td align="left">I would go tomovies.</td>
<td align="left">Kdybych měl čas,</td>
<td align="left">šel bych do kina.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">If I had money</td>
<td align="left">I would go to Paris.</td>
<td align="left">Kdybych měl peníze,</td>
<td align="left">jel bych do Paříže.</td>
</tr>

<h4 id="reader-content-english-and-czech-conditionals-compared">English and Czech conditionals compared</h4>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-136">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td>0 conditional</td>
<td align="left">If I do it, he is sad.</td>
<td align="left">Když to udělám, je smutný</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>1<sup>st</sup> conditional</td>
<td align="left">If I do it, he will be sad.</td>
<td align="left">Jestli to udělám, bude smutný.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>2<sup>nd</sup> conditional</td>
<td align="left">If I did this, he would be sad.</td>
<td align="left">Kdybych to udělal, byl by smutný.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>3<sup>rd</sup> conditional</td>
<td align="left">If I had done this, he would have been sad.</td>
<td align="left">Kdybych to byl udělal, byl by býval smutný.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-modal-verbs-can-must-need-should">Modal verbs: can, must, need, should</h2>
<p>Unlike English, Czech doesn’t have modal verbs as such that are set apart from other verbs by many formal features. It may be useful however, to list the Czech equivalent expressions to English modal verbs. They are often followed by the infinitive.</p>
<p><strong>Note</strong> that negating certain modal verbs in Czech produces an unexpected translation in English.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-137">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">must, have to</td>
<td align="left"><strong>muset</strong> (musím, musíš, musí, …, musí)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">must not, may not</td>
<td align="left"><strong>nesmět</strong> (nesmím, nesmíš, nesmí, nesmíme, nesmíte, nesmí)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">not have to</td>
<td align="left"><strong>nemuset</strong> (nemusím, …)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">can</td>
<td align="left"><strong>moct</strong> (můžu, můžeš, může, …, můžou)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">may</td>
<td align="left"><strong>smět</strong> (smím, smíš, smí, smíme, smíte, smí)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">need</td>
<td align="left"><strong>potřebovat</strong> (-uju,-uješ,-uje,-ujeme,…,-ujou)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">should/ ought to</td>
<td align="left">měl(a, i) bych, bys, by, bysme, byste, by + infinitive</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">should</td>
<td align="left">mám, máš, …, mají + infinitive</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-must-not-and-cannot-negating-modal-verbs">Must not and cannot (Negating modal verbs)</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-138">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" align="left">ANO</td>
<td colspan="2" align="left">NE</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">muset</td>
<td align="left">must</td>
<td align="left">nesmím</td>
<td align="left">I must not</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">muset</td>
<td align="left">have to</td>
<td align="left">nemusím</td>
<td align="left">I don't have to</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">smět</td>
<td align="left">may</td>
<td align="left">nesmím</td>
<td align="left">I may not</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">moct</td>
<td align="left">can</td>
<td align="left">nemůžu</td>
<td align="left">I cannot</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">potřebovat</td>
<td align="left">I need</td>
<td align="left">nepotřebuju</td>
<td align="left">I don't need</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-reflexive-or-self-se-and-si-verbs">Reflexive or self (<em>se</em> and <em>si</em>) verbs</h2>
<p>Some verbs only (or mostly) appear with one of the reflexive pronouns (<em>se</em> or <em>si</em>). Reflexives express a direction to the speaker. As in <em>I saw myself.</em> or <em>She gave this to herself.</em> In Czech, it is used even when the ‘reflexive’ meaning is hidden and becomes a part of the verb.</p>
<p>The <em>se</em> or <em>si</em> particles take up the Koho? Co? or Komu? Čemu? case slots which means that the following noun has to take a different form.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-se-verbs"><em>se</em> verbs</h3>
<p><em>se</em> reflexive verbs are never followed by a noun in the Koho? Co? form because <em>se</em> already fills that role. We can divide them by what follows:</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-true-reflexive-my-self">True reflexive (my self)</h4>
<p>You can add <em>se</em> to most verbs to express the idea of <em>self</em>.</p>
<p><strong>uhodit se</strong> to hit onself</p>
<p><strong>topit se / utopit se</strong> to drown</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-non-reflexive-se-verbs">Non-reflexive <em>se</em> verbs</h4>
<p>These verbs no longer have a very clear reflexive meaning but are always used with <em>se</em>.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-se-verbs-with-kdo-co"><em>se</em> verbs with <em>Kdo? Co?</em></h4>
<p><strong>jmenovat se</strong> to be called/named</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-se-verbs-with-koho-čeho"><em>se</em> verbs with <em>Koho? Čeho?</em></h4>
<p><strong>bát se</strong> to be afraid of something</p>
<p><strong>ptát se / zeptat se</strong> to ask [of] someone</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-se-verbs-with-komu-čemu"><em>se</em> verbs with <em>Komu? Čemu?</em></h4>
<p><strong>líbit se</strong> to be liked (lit. to be pleasurable to someone)</p>
<p><strong>nabídnout se</strong> to offer self to someone</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-se-verbs-with-kým-čím"><em>se</em> verbs with <em>Kým? Čím?</em></h4>
<p><strong>bavit se</strong> to have a good time; lit. to be amused by something</p>
<p><strong>zabývat se</strong> to research something</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-se-verbs-with-a-preposition"><em>se</em> verbs with a preposition</h4>
<p><strong>dívat se na / podívat se na</strong> to look at something</p>
<p><strong>líbat se s</strong> to kiss someone; lit. to kiss with someone . This is similar to make out with someone. <strong>Note</strong>: this is different from to give a kiss to someone which is <em>políbit někoho</em>.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-mutual-se">Mutual <em>se</em></h4>
<p><strong>potkat se</strong> to meet (each other)</p>
<p><strong>honit se</strong> to rush, to chase (each other)</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-se-passive"><em>se</em> passive</h4>
<p><strong>Říká se.</strong> They say (it is said)</p>
<p><strong>Rozbilo se to.</strong> It broke (it was broken)</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-si-verbs"><em>si</em> verbs</h3>
<p><em>si</em> reflexive verbs are never followed by a noun in the Komu? Čemu? form because <em>si</em> already fills that role. We can divide them into several types:</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-true-si-reflexive">True <em>si</em> reflexive</h4>
<p>These really express a direction to oneself.</p>
<p><strong>dát si</strong> to have (lit. to give to oneself)</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-non-reflexive-si-verbs">Non-reflexive <em>si</em> verbs</h4>
<p>These verbs are always used with <em>si</em> but don’t have a reflexive meaning anymore.</p>
<p><strong>číst si</strong> to read (lit. to read to oneself)</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-mutual-si-to-each-other">Mutual <em>si</em> (to each other)</h4>
<p><em>Si</em> is also used to express mutual meaning when two people or things give something to each other.</p>
<p>psát si to write to each other</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-casual-narrative-si">Casual (narrative) <em>si</em></h4>
<p>This is a very interesting use of <em>si</em> (a part of what is sometimes called ethical dative). You can add <em>si</em> to almost any verb to express the meaning of casualness <em>by your leave</em>, <em>as if nothing happened</em>.</p>
<p>My utíkáme a on si jde. We’re running and he’s walking (as if nothing happened).</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-verbs-of-motion-their-aspect-and-tense">Verbs of Motion – Their Aspect and Tense</h2>
<p>Verbs of motion form a separate category in Czech with respect to both tense and aspect. Although there are only roughly 15 of them, they are very important in Czech because, through the use of different prefixes, <strong>they appear at the core of more than 500 possible dictionary entries</strong>. They differ from the other verbs in two respects. 1) <strong>Future tense is formed with a prefix <em>po-</em>,</strong> and 2) they have <strong>a special form for the repetitive aspect</strong> (where other verbs use the imperfective form).</p>
<p>Also, some of them make a <strong>distinction between movement on foot and by a vehicle</strong> (as in <em>jít</em> vs. <em>jet</em> or <em>nést</em> vs. <em>vézt</em>).</p>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> Some verbs only display some characteristics of verbs of motion.</p>
<p>The verbs most commonly listed as verbs of motion are:</p>

<p>Verbs of motion are further special in that they can each take on <strong>the full range of the 20 prefixes</strong> in Czech. Sometimes the prefix will only add its meaning to the verb, for example to indicate direction, as in <em>obejít</em> (to walk around, circumvent) but sometimes it will create a whole new word where the original meaning of the verb is barely recognizable, as in <em>najít</em> (to find).</p>
<p>By attaching a prefix to an imperfective verb of motion, the verb becomes perfective. It could then be said that <strong>each imperfective verb of motion has 20 perfective equivalents</strong> (see above for hubs of verbs) with slightly different meaning. The confusion steps in when we realize that <strong>each of these newly created perfective verbs has in turn an imperfective pair</strong> without any alteration of meaning. This pair verb is created using the repetitive verb of motion as its root. That is shown in a table below using the prefix <em>při-</em> (in the direction toward the point of reference). The same holds for all the remaining 19 prefixes.</p>

<h2 id="reader-content-models-of-verbs-for-conjugation-complete-chart">Models of Verbs for Conjugation – Complete Chart</h2>
<p>The traditional full classification of Czech verbal conjugation uses the following five classes with subcategories, giving a total of 14 models distinguished by the ending in the third person. Even that is not enough for a full classification but it covers the majority of Czech verbs. Fronek’s Czech-English/English-Czech Dictionary gives the complete breakdown. There are other ways to organize the verbs as well.</p>

<p>These are some common verbs that are <strong>irregular</strong> in one or more aspects: spát, mít, být, chtít, vědět, říct, jíst, moct, bát se, stát, jít, vést, číst, krást, třást, hnát, lhát, psát, poslat, vázat, kousat, skákat, páchat, řezat, stát se, zatnout, vzít, vyjmout, přijmout, pojmout, obout, zout, hřát, hrát, dít se, smát se, myslet (see details below for selected verbs).</p>
<p><strong>Note 1:</strong> Verbs in classes I-IV are further subdivided according to the past, the 3<sup>rd</sup> person plural forms, or palatalization of the last consonant.</p>
<p><strong>Note 2:</strong> The numbers of these categories have no correspondence to numbers of categories in the conjugation summary.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-selected-most-common-irregular-verbs">Selected (most common) Irregular Verbs</h2>
<p>Some of these 21 verbs are not strictly speaking irregular but from the point of view of the student it is best to treat them this way. Verbs can also be irregular in some of their other forms such as passive. These are not included in this chart. There are many more irregular verbs but they are rare. <em>Být</em> is not included in this list.</p>

<h2 id="reader-content-top-150-czech-verbs">Top 150 Czech Verbs</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-verb-chart">Verb chart</h3>
<p>The verbs in this chart are the most common or most commonly needed in Czech. They are not most frequent because frequency is dependent on the type of text. Written, spoken, fiction, non-fiction, journalism, all of these have different frequencies of verbs. These are judged to be most useful for the student. They are sorted alphabetically and not by frequency.</p>
