<h3 id="reader-content-how-to-use-the-verb-chart">How to use the verb chart</h3>
<h4 id="reader-content-verb">Verb</h4>
<p>The verb as it is found in the dictionary. Typically only one aspect is given for each pair. It is the one that seems to be more common (whenever possible).</p>
<p><strong>Note on <em>se</em></strong> <strong>and <em>si</em>:</strong> If <em>se</em> or <em>si</em> can be used with the verb, they are listed in parentheses. If they are not in parentheses, the verb can only be used with them. If used optionally, they typically change the meaning (at least to <em>self</em>) this is then given under English Translation also in parentheses.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-present">Present</h4>
<p>The conjugation is given in the forms of: first person singular (I), third person singular (he, she, it) and third person plural (they). When a verb has an irregular conjugation, these are the forms likely to be different.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-l-form-past">L-form (past)</h4>
<p>This is the form of the verb used in the past tense. <strong>–l</strong> means that the final <strong>–t</strong> of the infinitive is simple replaced with the <strong>–l</strong>. Otherwise the full <strong>–l</strong> form is given. Some verbs have more than one possible form. The more formal (written) is given first.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-n-form-passive">N-form (passive)</h4>
<p>Although the –<strong>n</strong> form is mostly regular, the full form is given for all verbs. Not all verbs can be used in a passive sentence but most need this form to make nouns and adjectives.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-imperative-1">Imperative</h4>
<p>Even though, the imperative is almost always regular, the full singular form is given for all verbs except for verbs ending in <strong>–ovat</strong> which only replace the infinitive ending with <strong>–uj</strong>. The plural form can always be formed according to the rules listed under Imperative.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-object">Object</h4>
<p>Sometimes called valence, this indicates what type of object is the verb likely to have. The question words indicate whether it is a noun, a noun following a preposition, or an adverbial phrase of time or place. The question word for the noun also indicates the case.</p>
<p>When two objects are separated by comma, it means, that one or the other can be chosen. When they are not, it means that both have to be present at once (although the exact word order may be different).</p>
<p>Objects listed in parentheses are optional. Those given without any marks, always need to be present in some form.</p>
<p>The slot marked by <em>koho</em> (for accusative) can be filled by <em>se</em> and <em>komu</em> (for dative) can be filled by <em>si</em> if one or both are marked as possible in the first column.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-aspect">Aspect</h4>
<p><strong>Ipf.</strong> marks an imperfective verb and <strong>Pf.</strong> marks a perfective verb.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-pair-same-meaning">Pair (same meaning)</h4>
<p>If the verb has a pair verb, with the same meaning but of the opposite aspect, it is listed here. <strong>Note:</strong> It may not be conjugated according to the same pattern. See Irregular verbs below to check whether it requires special treatment.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-prefix-derivates-diff-meaning">Prefix Derivates (diff meaning)</h4>
<p>If there are verbs derived from this one by a prefix, the most important ones are listed here. They always have a slightly different meaning than the verb in the first column (which is not marked, however).</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-english-translation">English Translation</h4>
<p>The most straightforward English translation possible is given here. See a larger dictionary for more.</p>



---

<td><p><strong>!</strong></p></td>
<td><p>Most of adverbs in Czech are formed from adjectives.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The <strong><em>-ý</em></strong> or <strong><em>-í</em></strong> ending of the adjective is dropped, and <strong><em>-e</em></strong> or <strong><em>-ě</em></strong> is added. Remember that <strong>-<em>ě</em></strong> changes the consonant before it.</p></li>
<li><p>dobrý - dobře, jarní - jarně, mladý - mladě</p></li>
</ul>
<p>k <strong>></strong> c krátký <strong>></strong> krátce</p>
<p>ch <strong>></strong> š jednoduchý <strong>></strong> jednoduše</p>
<p>r <strong>></strong> ř starý <strong>></strong> staře</p>
<p>h <strong>></strong> z drahý <strong>></strong> draze</p>
<ul>
<li>The <strong><em>-cký, -ský, zký</em></strong> endings are shortened</li>
</ul>

<ul>
<li>hezký - hezky, český - česky</li>
</ul>

<ul>
<li>The <strong><em>-ý</em></strong> ending has an equivalent <strong><em>-o</em></strong> which is used for some words. Sometimes, there is a slight difference in meaning, which has to be learned with individual words in certain contexts.</li>
</ul>

<ul>
<li>daleký - daleko; dlouhý - dlouho; jasný – jasno</li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<h2 id="reader-content-space---kde-nebo-kam">Space - <em>Kde?</em> nebo <em>Kam?</em></h2>
<p>Czech distinguishes between locality and directionality rather religiously. All prepositions of space observe this distinction. It is important whether the object is standing still or moving. For instance: <em>auto jede na hory</em> (a car is going to the mountains) vs. <em>auto jede na horách</em> ( car is in the mountains). What tell these two sentences apart is the case ending.</p>
<p>There are two prepositions for each meaning ( e.g. <em>in</em> vs. <em>into</em>). One set uses the case to distinguish while the other set uses two different prepositions.</p>

<h3 id="reader-content-conjunctives">Conjunctives</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-157">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>a</strong></p>
<p>and</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Učím se česky a francouzsky.</p>
<p>Snídám housku a čaj.</p>
<p>Půjdu domů a uvařím večeři.</p></td>

<p>I have roll and tea for breakfast.</p>
<p>I'll go home and cook dinner.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>i</strong></p>
<p>as well as</p>
<p>both… and</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Mám hlad i žízeň.</p>
<p>Zajímá mě literatura i historie.</p>
<p>Na zahradě máme ovoce i zeleninu.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>I'm hungry as well as thirsty.</p>
<p>I'm interested in literature and history.</p>
<p>We have fruits as well as vegetables...</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>jako<br>
jakoby</strong></p>
<p>as<br>
as though</p></td>

<p>Cítil se jako doma.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>It was as though in a dream.</p>
<p>He felt like he was at home.</p></td>
</tr>

<h3 id="reader-content-time-2">Time</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-161">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>když</strong></p>

<td align="left"><p>When I was in Prague I bought a book.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>až</strong></p>
<p>when, as late as</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Až budu v Praze, koupím si knihu.</p>

<p>Až skončí film, půjdeme domů.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>When I'm in Prague I'll buy a book.</p>

hned jak</strong></p>
<p>as soon as</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Hned jak přijdu, zavolám ti.</p>
<p>Jakmile se uzdraví, přijde na návštěvu.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>As soon as I get back I'll call you.</p>
<p>As soon as he recovers he will come to visit.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>zatímco</strong></p>
<p>while</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Zatímco jsi spal, já jsem pracovala.</p>
<p>Zatímco ty se máš dobře, já se mám špatně.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>While you were sleeping I was working.</p>
<p>While you're okay, I'm bad.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>kdykoli</strong></p>
<p>whenever, any time</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Kdykoli přijde, přinese dárek.</p>
<p>Můžete mi zavolat kdykoli. (<em>kdykoli serves as an adverb</em>)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Whenever he comes he brings a gift.</p>
<p>You can call me whenever.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>dokud</strong></p>
<p>as long as</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Dokud jsem naživu nedostaneš ani cent.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>As long as I live you're not gonna get a cent.</p></td>
</tr>

jestliže</strong></p>
<p>if</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Jestli bude sníh, pojedeme lyžovat.</p>
<p>Jestliže to nebude hotové včas, nedostaneš peníze.</p>

<td align="left"><p>If there is snow, we will go skiing.</p>

<p>Kdyby byl sníh, jeli bychom lyžovat.</p></td>

</tr>

<td align="left"><p>-<strong>li</strong></p>

<p>Budu-li mít čas, udělám to.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>If there is snow we will go skiing.</p>
<p>If I have time, I will do it.</p></td>
</tr>

přestože</strong></p>

<p>Even though I don't have much money I will buy it.</p></td>

anyway</p></td>

<h2 id="reader-content-selected-problematic-conjunctions">Selected Problematic Conjunctions</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-až-and-když">AŽ <em>and</em> KDYŽ</h3>
<p>Both až and když can be used in more than one context (see table below). Only when used as the equivalent of the English when (i.e. temporally) can they become a source of confusion for the learner. Až always points to the future, while když describes events in the past, or the present, most often repeatedly (for instance, <strong><em>Vždycky když ...</em></strong> . is the only way to translate <strong><em>always when</em></strong>...).</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-164">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td colspan="2"><strong>až</strong></td>
<td colspan="2"><strong>když</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td>až přijdu</td>
<td>when I come</td>
<td>když přišel</td>
<td>when he came</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>temporal</strong></td>
<td>až to bude hotové</td>
<td>when it's ready</td>
<td>když to bylo hotové</td>
<td>when it was ready</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td>až pojedeš do Brna</td>
<td>when you (will) go to Brno</td>
<td>když jedeš do Brna</td>
<td>when you go to Brno</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>local</strong></td>
<td>až do Prahy</td>
<td>all the way to Prague</td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td>až v Praze</td>
<td>not until Prague</td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>condition</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td>když přijdu</td>
<td>if I come</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td>když to udělá</td>
<td>if he does it</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Both až and když are found in a number of idioms and proverbs. E.g., <strong><em>když už tak už</em></strong> (used as when we're doing it anyway it might better be worth it), <strong><em>až na to ...</em></strong> (except that ...), <strong><em>mám toho až až</em></strong>, (I'm fed up with it), <strong><em>to je až moc</em></strong> (it's a little bit too much). There's even <strong><em>až když</em></strong> which means <strong><em>only when</em></strong>. <strong><em>Když</em></strong> + future tense is the equivalent of English 1<sup>st</sup> conditional.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-kdy-and-když">KDY <em>and</em> KDYŽ</h3>
<p>Now it’s time to put <strong><em>když</em></strong> into opposition with <strong><em>kdy</em></strong>. The basic difference is similar to that of between který and co. They both mean when. But the basic usage of <strong><em>kdy</em></strong> is related to time, e.g. question word as in <strong><em>Kdy příjdeš?</em></strong> (When will you come?), or conjunction as in <strong><em>Řekněte mi, kdy se můžeme sejít.</em></strong> (Tell me when we can meet.) while <strong><em>když</em></strong> is basically used as a conjunction in relative clauses <strong><em>Když přišel, bylo to hotové.</em></strong> (When he came, it was done.)</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-165">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Temporal</td>
<td colspan="2">Relative</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Kdy přijdeš?</td>
<td>When will you come?</td>
<td>Když přijdeš, bude to hotové.</td>
<td>When you come it will be done.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Až to bude hotové dostaneš peníze.</td>
<td>When it’s done you will get money.</td>
<td>Když to bude hotové, dostaneš peníze.</td>
<td>If it’s done you will get money.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong><em>Když</em></strong> is also used in the following way. <strong><em>Čtu jen když je světlo.</em></strong> (I read only when there is light.) <strong><em>Když to jinak nejde, přijdu.</em></strong> (If that’s the only way, I will come.) <strong><em>Když se mě to nelíbí, tak si to nekoupím.</em></strong> (If I don’t like it, I won’t buy it.) <strong><em>Když přijde, všechno bude v pořádku.</em></strong> (If/when she comes, everything will be OK.) <strong><em>Co když se jim něco stane?</em></strong> (What if something happens to them?) <strong><em>Udělám to i když nemám čas.</em></strong> (I will do it even though I don’t have time.) <strong><em>Když ono je to tak hezké.</em></strong> (But it is so nice!).</p>
<p><strong><em>Kdy</em></strong> is also used in a few rather bookish idioms. <strong><em>Nemám kdy.</em></strong> (I don’t have time.) <strong><em>Na to není kdy.</em></strong> (There’s not time for that.) <strong><em>Bylo to horší než kdy dříve.</em></strong> (It was worse than ever before.)</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-který-and-že">KTERÝ and ŽE</h3>
<p>One that sometimes causes misunderstandings is the English conjunction <strong><em>that</em></strong> which actually serves a double purpose. Consider these English sentences: 1) <em>This is the man that I saw.</em> 2) a. <em>He said that he would do it. /</em> b. <em>He doesn't know that he owes me money. <strong>That</strong></em> in 1) further describes a person (or introduces a relative clause) while in 2/ it indicates a. reported speech and b. a reported fact. These two are easily distinguished, and in Czech they require a different word. 1) in Czech, would be <em>To je ten muž, kterého jsem viděl.</em> while 2) will be a. <em>Řekl, že to udělá.</em> b. <em>Neví, že mi dluží peníze. <strong>Který</strong></em> is really an adjective, and therefore it takes on the case of the noun in the main clause (in Czech grammars you will find it classified as a <em>relative pronoun</em>). <strong><em>Že</em></strong>, on the other hand, is a true conjunction, and it doesn't change at all. <strong>Note:</strong> Unlike <strong><em>that</em></strong> in English, both <strong><em>který</em></strong> and <strong><em>že</em></strong> are always preceded by a comma.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-ať-and-aby">AŤ and ABY</h3>
<p>The dreaded <em>aby</em> and <em>ať</em> are next. The indirect imperatives, or subjunctives. They are very similar to French subjunctives. <strong><em>Ať</em></strong> is followed by the he, she, or they form of the verb, while <strong><em>aby</em></strong> requires the past tense. It takes on the function of the auxiliary, and changes its form to <em>abych, abys, aby, abysme, abyste, aby</em> (in literary Czech the correct we-form is abychom). The difference in meaning between ať and aby is very slight . The sentences in the chart below should demonstrate.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-166">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">AŤ (direct wish)</td>
<td colspan="2">ABY (indirect wish)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Řekni mu, ať přijde!</td>
<td>Tell him to come.</td>
<td>Řekni mu, aby přišel!</td>
<td>Tell him to come!</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Ať si jde.</td>
<td>Let him go.</td>
<td>Chci, aby šel.</td>
<td>I want him to go.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td>Chce, abychom tam šli.</td>
<td>He wants us to go there.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td>Nechci, aby byla válka.</td>
<td>I don't want there to be war.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td>Aby přijel, musí mít peníze.</td>
<td>He must have money in order to come.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

> Section 12 of 22 · Parts of speech: Nouns, Verbs and More > How and when Czech words change their forms · id `how-and-when-czech-words-change-their-forms` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=how-and-when-czech-words-change-their-forms)

<h1 id="reader-content-how-and-when-czech-words-change-their-forms">How and when Czech words change their forms</h1>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-167">










<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Category</strong>*</td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>Nouns</em><br>
Who, What</strong></td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>Adjectives</em><br>
What like</strong></td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>Pronouns</em><br>
who, what, those e.g. he, his, it, …</strong></td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>Numerals</em><br>
how many, what order, times</strong></td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>Verbs</em><br>
actions, states</strong></td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>Adverbs</em><br>
how, when, where…</strong></td>
<td align="center"><strong><em>Prepositions</em><br>
when, where, e.g. in, at, before</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Case</strong></td>
<td align="center">Endings change based on function in sentence and prepositions.</td>
<td align="center">Endings change following the nouns it modifies and/or the adjective’s function in the sentence.</td>
<td align="center">Forms change depending on function, preposition or position in the sentence.</td>
<td align="center">Endings change</td>
<td align="center">Doesn’t change with case but determines what case nouns, pronouns, adjectives and numerals will take.</td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center">Never change their form but require nouns, pronouns, adjectives and numeral following them to change case.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Singular/ Plural</strong></td>
<td align="center">Different ending for different numbers.</td>
<td align="center">Different ending for different numbers.</td>
<td align="center">Different form for different numbers.</td>
<td align="center">Different form for different numbers.</td>
<td align="center">Changes ending in the past, present and future tenses.</td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Gender</strong></td>
<td align="center">Can belong to one of three genders (masculine<strong>*</strong>, feminine, neuter)</td>
<td align="center">Changes ending based on the gender of the noun it follows.</td>
<td align="center">Some change ending based on the gender of the noun it relates to.</td>
<td align="center">1, 2 and all ordinal numerals change ending based on the noun they count.</td>
<td align="center">Changes ending in the past tense only.</td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Palatalization</strong></td>
<td align="center">Certain ending in certain cases soften the final consonant.</td>
<td align="center">Certain ending in certain cases soften the final consonant.</td>
<td align="center">Certain ending in certain cases soften the final consonant.</td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Person</strong></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center">1<sup>st</sup> (I), 2<sup>nd</sup> (you), 3<sup>rd</sup> (he, she, it)</td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Tense</strong></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center">Past, present, future.</td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Voice</strong></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center">Active and passive.</td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Aspect</strong></td>
<td align="center">Some nouns formed from verbs reflect aspect.</td>
<td align="center">Some adjectives formed from verbs reflect aspect.</td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center">Perfective and imperfective.</td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td align="center"></td>

most slowly</td>

</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>*</strong> Outside this system lie Conjunctions (and, if, or), Interjections (ouch!) and Particles (thus) which never change their form or influence other words to change form. <strong>Note:</strong> Some pronouns function as conjunctions (who, which, whose – so called relative pronouns) and of course change their form with all the categories relevant to pronouns.</p>
<p><strong>**</strong> Masculine nouns and adjectives can be either animate or inanimate thus in effect multiplying the number of genders to four.</p>

> Section 13 of 22 · Parts of speech: Nouns, Verbs and More > How parts of speech interact when combined in a sentence · id `how-parts-of-speech-interact-when-combined-in-a-sentence` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=how-parts-of-speech-interact-when-combined-in-a-sentence)

<h1 id="reader-content-how-parts-of-speech-interact-when-combined-in-a-sentence">How parts of speech interact when combined in a sentence</h1>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-168">










<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><em>Accross follows down</em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p><strong>Noun</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p><strong>Adjective</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p><strong>Pronoun</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p><strong>Numeral</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p><strong>Verb</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p><strong>Adverb</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p><strong>Preposition</strong></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Noun</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Typically joined by genitive</p>
<p><em>dům rodin<strong>y</strong></em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Archaic or poetic use</p>
<p><em>dům bíl<strong>ý</strong></em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Archaic or poetic use</p>
<p><em>dům náš</em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Archaic or poetic use</p>
<p><em>dům je</em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Noun either subject or object</p>
<p><em>dům stojí</em></p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Adjective</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Adjective follows noun gender and case</p>
<p><em>mal<strong>ého</strong> dom<strong>u</strong></em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Both adjectives keep the same form.</p>
<p><em>malé bílé domy</em></p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"><p>Adjective takes appropriate case form</p>
<p><em>malý stojí</em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Adverb modifies adjective</p>
<p><em>hodně malý</em></p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Pronoun</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Pronouns agree in gender and case</p>
<p><em>to<strong>ho</strong> dom<strong>u</strong></em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Both agree in gender and case</p>
<p><em>to<strong>ho</strong> malé<strong>ho</strong> dom<strong>u</strong></em></p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"><p>Both agree in gender and case</p>
<p><em>to<strong>ho</strong> jedno<strong>ho</strong> dom<strong>u</strong></em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Pronoun subject or object of verb</p>
<p><em>on ho vidí</em></p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Numeral</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Agree with noun gender and case.</p>
<p><em>jedn</em><strong>oho</strong> <em>dom<strong>u</strong></em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Work in conjunction</p>
<p><em>jedno</em><strong>ho</strong> <em>mal</em>é<strong>ho</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Agree on case and gender</p>
<p><em>jedno<strong>ho</strong> to<strong>ho</strong></em></p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"><p>Numeral object or subject</p>
<p><em>jeden přijel pozdě</em></p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Verb</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Noun either subject or object</p>
<p><em>stojí dům</em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Adjective subject or object</p>
<p><em>vidíme mal<strong>ého</strong></em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Pronoun subject or object of verb</p>
<p><em>vidím ho</em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Numeral subject or object</p>
<p><em>vidím jednu</em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>One forces the other into infinitive</p>
<p><em>musí jít</em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Adverb modifies verb</p>
<p><em>běží rychle</em></p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Adverb</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"><p>Adverb modifies adjective</p>
<p><em>moc malý</em></p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"><p>Adverb modifies verb</p>
<p><em>moc sedí</em></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>One elaborates the other</p>
<p><em>moc rychle</em></p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Preposition</strong></p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Determines case of noun</p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Determines case of adjective</p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Determines case of pronoun</p></td>
<td align="center"><p>Determines case of numeral</p></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
<td align="center"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Other parts of speech (Conjunctions, Interjections, Particles) work independently as part of the sentence as a whole.</p>
<p>Blank cells indicate a word order not found in Czech (e.g. Prepositions never follow any other part of speech. Or adverbs don’t follow prepositions in a way that modifies the adverb. But adverbs may precede an adjective in a phrase that is preceded by a preposition as in, “in a very long book”.</p>



---

> Section 14 of 22 · Word Order · id `word-order` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=word-order)

<h1 id="reader-content-word-order">Word Order</h1>
<p>Contrary to popular belief, Czech word order is not completely free. In Czech, word order serves to show what you want to stress. The general rule is <em>What you want to stress goes at the end of the sentence.</em> For instance, take the simplest possible sentence <em>Petr pije kolu.</em> (Petr is drinking coke.) The words in this sentence can be ordered in any way and it will still mean the same (unlike English where <em>Coke is drinking Peter</em> is a little awkward). BUT. Each of the orderings serves as an answer to a different question.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-169">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Question</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Answer</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Co</strong> pije Petr? (What is Petr drinking?)</td>
<td align="left">Petr pije <strong>kolu</strong>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Kdo</strong> pije kolu? (Who is drinking coke?)</td>
<td align="left">Kolu pije <strong>Petr</strong>.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Therefore, unlike English, from even a very simple answer you can guess what the question was. Notice that the answers are mirror images of their questions.</p>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> As you may see below the other possible combinations also make sense in Czech</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-170">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>Jí kolu Petr?</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Petr kolu pije.</em> (No, Petr doesn't eat coke he drinks it)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>Jí Petr kolu?</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Kolu Petr pije.</em></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>Pije Petr kolu? (not mléko, or čaj)</p>
<p>Pije kolu Petr? (not Jana, or Karel)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>(these two combinations would be most likely perceived as questions)</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Three words, thus, give us six possible combinations, and all have a slightly different meaning in Czech. The word order is always a little vague for even in English you have sentences like <em>see it he did</em>, <em>Petr he saw</em>, <em>Petr saw he</em>. To grasp the subtle differences you have to live with the language for a long time.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-typical-word-order-of-a-czech-sentence">Typical Word Order of a Czech Sentence</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-simple-sentence">Simple sentence</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-171">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">1</td>
<td align="left">2</td>
<td align="left">3</td>
<td align="left">4</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">subject</td>
<td align="left">verb</td>
<td align="left">indirect object</td>
<td align="left">object</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Petr</td>
<td align="left">píše</td>
<td align="left">Martině</td>
<td align="left">dopis.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Petr</td>
<td align="left">is writing</td>
<td align="left">to Martina</td>
<td align="left">a letter.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> <strong>3</strong> and <strong>4</strong> are interchangeable.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-sentence-with-adverbials">Sentence with adverbials</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-172">








<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>1</p></td>
<td><p>2</p></td>
<td><p>3</p></td>
<td><p>4</p></td>
<td><p>5</p></td>
<td><p>6</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>adverbial</p>
<p>of time</p></td>
<td><p>verb</p></td>
<td><p>subject</p></td>
<td><p>indirect object</p></td>
<td><p>object</p></td>
<td><p>adverbial</p>
<p>of place</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>Včera</p></td>
<td><p>psal</p></td>
<td><p>Petr</p></td>
<td><p>Martině</p></td>
<td><p>dopis</p></td>
<td><p>v parku.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>Yesterday</p></td>
<td><p>was writing</p></td>
<td><p>Petr</p></td>
<td><p>to Martina</p></td>
<td><p>a letter</p></td>
<td><p>in the park.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-173">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>!</strong></p></td>
<td><ul>
<li>Any part of the sentence could be moved at the end to be stressed:</li>
</ul>

<ul>
<li><p>Petr psal Martině dopis v parku <strong>VČERA</strong>. (not the day before)</p></li>
<li><p>Dopis psal včera v parku Martině <strong>PETR</strong>. (not Jirka)</p></li>
<li><p>Petr psal včera v parku Martině <strong>DOPIS</strong>. (not a poem)</p></li>
<li><p>Petr psal včera v parku dopis <strong>MARTINĚ</strong>. (not to Jana)</p></li>
<li><p>Petr včera dopis Martině v parku <strong>PSAL</strong>. (not read)</p>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> The finite verb tends to keep <strong>the second</strong> position. (if unstressed)</p></li>
</ul>

<ul>
<li><p>The adverbial of time usually precedes that of space.<br>
Otherwise the position of adverbials is fairly flexible</p></li>
<li><p>si, se, mě, mi, ti, se, si, ho, mu (jsem, ..., jste in byl jsem,..., byli jste)</p></li>
</ul>

<ul>
<li><p>always take the second position in the sentence</p></li>
<li><p>Měl jsem se ho zeptat. (I should have asked him.)</p></li>
</ul>

<ul>
<li>adjectives always precede nouns - <em>dobrý učitel, malý muž, žlutý banán</em><br>
(the reverse - French like - order is possible but would sound archaic)</li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-how-does-se-change-around-in-a-sentence">How does <em>se</em> change around in a sentence?</h2>
<p>It in fact <em>se</em> doesn't move at all. <em>se</em> always occupies the second position in the sentence, and other words move around it to allow for that.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-174">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td>1</td>
<td>2</td>
<td>3</td>
<td colspan="2">4</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Jak</td>
<td>se</td>
<td colspan="2">jmenuješ?</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Jmenuju</td>
<td>se</td>
<td colspan="2">Petr.</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Jak</td>
<td>se</td>
<td colspan="2">máš?</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Mám</td>
<td>se</td>
<td colspan="2">dobře.</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Jak</td>
<td>se</td>
<td colspan="2">má</td>
<td>Petr?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Petr</td>
<td>se</td>
<td colspan="2">má</td>
<td>dobře.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-where-are-jsem-and-budu-placed-in-the-past-and-future-tense">Where are <em>jsem</em> and <em>budu</em> placed in the past and future tense?</h2>
<p>The auxiallry form of the verb <em>to be</em> (jsem, jsi, jsme, jste) in the past tense is almost always in the second position.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-175">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">1</td>
<td align="left">2</td>
<td align="left">3</td>
<td align="left">4</td>
<td align="left">5</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Koupil</td>
<td align="left">jsem</td>
<td align="left">si</td>
<td align="left">to</td>
<td align="left">včera.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Včera</td>
<td align="left">jsem</td>
<td align="left">si</td>
<td align="left">to</td>
<td align="left">koupil.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Koupil</td>
<td align="left">jsi</td>
<td align="left">to</td>
<td align="left">včera.</td>
<td align="left"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Díval</td>
<td align="left">jsem</td>
<td align="left">se</td>
<td align="left">na televizi.</td>
<td align="left"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Tak</td>
<td align="left">jsem</td>
<td align="left">se</td>
<td align="left">Díval</td>
<td align="left">na televizi.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The auxiliary form of the verb <em>to be</em> (budu), can be anywhere in the sentence depending on the emphasis on. For example:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Zítra budu psát knihu. (Tomorrow, I will be writing a book).</p></li>
<li><p>Budu zítra psát knihu? (Will I be writing a book tomorrow?)</p></li>
<li><p>Zítra knihu psát budu. (I <strong>will</strong> [definitely] be writing a book tomorrow.)</p></li>
<li><p>Zítra budu knihu psát. (Writing is what I will be doing with the book tomorrow).</p></li>
</ul>
<h2 id="reader-content-questions-and-answers-in-czech">Questions and answers in Czech</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-co-kdo-kdy-kde-kam-proč">co? kdo? kdy? kde? kam? proč?</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-176">








<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>Co</strong> píše Petr?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Petr píše <strong>dopis</strong>.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>Kdo</strong> píše dopis?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Dopis píše <strong>Petr</strong>.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>Kdy</strong> čte Martina?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Martina čte <strong>večer</strong>.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>Kde</strong> se učili studenti?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Studenti se učili <strong>v parku</strong>.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>Kam</strong> šly děti?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Děti šly <strong>do školy</strong>.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>Proč</strong> chcete jíst?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Chceme jíst, <strong>protože ...</strong> .</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>!</p></td>
<td colspan="5"><ul>
<li><p>if a question starts with a <em>question word</em> the word order of the answer mirrors</p></li>
<li><p>the <strong>intonation</strong> of this kind of question is identical with that of simple statements</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-ano-nebo-ne">ano <em>nebo</em> ne</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-177">









<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>Píše</strong> <em>Petr</em> dopis?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Ano.</p></td>
<td><p><em>Petr</em> <strong>píše</strong> dopis.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>Píše</strong> <em>dopis</em> Petr?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Ano.</p></td>
<td><p><em>Dopis</em> <strong>píše</strong> Petr.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>Čte</strong> <em>Martina</em> večer?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Ano.</p></td>
<td><p><em>Martina</em> <strong>čte</strong> večer.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>Šly</strong> <em>děti</em> do školy?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Ano.</p></td>
<td><p><em>Děti</em> <strong>šly</strong> do školy.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>Učili se</strong> <em>studenti</em> v parku?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Ano.</p></td>
<td><p><em>Studenti</em> <strong>se učili</strong> v parku.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p>Chcete <em>jíst</em>?</p></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td><p>Ano.</p></td>
<td><p>Chceme <em>jíst</em>.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>!</p></td>
<td colspan="6"><ul>
<li><p>in <em>YES/NO questions</em> you typically swap the <em>verb</em> and the <em>noun</em></p></li>
<li><p>however, if your question requires a <em>YES</em> or <em>NO</em> answer, <em>ano</em> or <em>ne</em> will suffice</p></li>
<li><p>the word order of an answer to a <em>YES/NO question</em> is your typical word order for simple statements</p></li>
<li><p>the <strong>intonation</strong> of this type of question has a rising pattern - it goes up on the last syllable in the utterance</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-negation">Negation</h2>
<p>The negative particle <strong>ne</strong> behaves differently in different tenses. Most of the time it is attached directly to the main verb. Only in the <strong>future with</strong> <strong>budu</strong>, budu is negated instead.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-178">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Yes</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>No</strong></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Past</strong></td>
<td align="left">dělal jsem to</td>
<td align="left"><strong>ne</strong>dělal jsem to</td>
<td>I wasn’t doing it</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Present</strong></td>
<td align="left">dělám to</td>
<td align="left"><strong>ne</strong>dělám to</td>
<td>I’m not doing it</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Conditional</strong></td>
<td align="left">dělal bych to</td>
<td align="left"><strong>ne</strong>dělal bych to</td>
<td>I wouldn’t do it</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Future</strong></td>
<td align="left">budu to dělat</td>
<td align="left"><strong>ne</strong>budu to dělat</td>
<td>I’m not going to do it.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> Sentences like <strong>Ne</strong>jsem to dělal. <strong>Ne</strong>bych jela. or Budu to <strong>ne</strong>dělat. are wrong.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-double-negatives">Double Negatives</h2>
<p>In Czech, double negatives are a requirement! All pronouns and adverbs used to quantify have to be used in the negative form in addition to the verb in negative form.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-179">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>ANO</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>NE</strong></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Mám všechno.</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Ne</strong>mám <strong>nic</strong>.</td>
<td>I have everything / nothing.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Ví všechno.</td>
<td align="left">Neví nic.</td>
<td>She knows everything / nothing.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Mám nějaký rohlík.</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Ne</strong>mám <strong>žádný</strong> rohlík.</td>
<td>I have some bread / no bread.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Příjde vždycky ráno.</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Ne</strong>přijde <strong>nikdy</strong>.</td>
<td>He always comes… / He never comes.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-reference-pronouns-anaphors">Reference Pronouns (Anaphors)</h2>
<p>No language likes to repeat nouns over and over. Instead, pronouns are used to refer back to nouns mentioned before. For example, instead of saying <em>He bought a new car. The car was expensive.</em> you say <em>He bought a new car. <strong>It</strong> was expensive.</em> You can also refer back to verb or whole sentences. For example, <em>I ran a mile under 10 minutes. <strong>It</strong></em> <em>was very difficult.</em></p>
<p>English uses many pronouns for this purpose: <em>he, she, it, him, her, his</em></p>
<p>There are several things to remember about Czech in this respect.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The pronoun has to match the gender.</p></li>
<li><p>The pronoun may change according to case, especially after prepositions.</p></li>
<li><p>The pronoun doesn’t always have to be expressed.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-180">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Nouns</strong></td>
<td align="left">unexpressed pronoun</td>
<td align="left">Petr má nové kolo.<br>
Je červené.</td>
<td>Petr has a new bike.<br>
<strong>It</strong> is read.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">on, ona</td>
<td align="left">Jana si koupila knihu, ale<br>
<strong>ona</strong> nebyla dobrá.</td>
<td>Jana bought a book but<br>
<strong>it</strong> wasn’t good.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">mě, jemu, ji, jí</td>
<td align="left">Petra nepřinesla tužku,<br>
protože <strong>ji</strong> nemohla najít.</td>
<td>Petra didn’t bring a pen<br>
because she couldn’t find <strong>it</strong>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">s ním, bez něho</td>
<td align="left">Mám dobrý počítač,<br>
bez <strong>něho</strong> nemůžu pracovat.</td>
<td>I have a good computer,<br>
without <strong>it</strong> I cannot work.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Verbs</strong></td>
<td align="left">to</td>
<td align="left">Včera jsem ujel 100 km,<br>
bylo <strong>to</strong> těžké.</td>
<td>Yesterday, I drove 100 km, <strong>it</strong> was difficult.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">s tím</td>
<td align="left">Nemám peníze.<br>
S <strong>tím</strong> se nedá nic dělat.</td>
<td>I have no money. There’s nothing to be done about <strong>it</strong>.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></li>
</ul>
<h2 id="reader-content-relative-clauses-and-phrases">Relative Clauses and Phrases</h2>
<p>Relative clause or phrase is always begun with a relative particle. See <em>Conjunctions</em> for a list. The important thing to remember about the relative conjunctions (pronouns) <em>který</em> (which)<em>, kdo</em> (who)<em>, jaký</em> (what) is that they change with the case and gender of the noun they refer to. <em>Co</em> (what) can be used with any case without change.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-který">Který</h3>
<p><em>Který</em> can be translated into English as <em>who, what, that, which</em>. More often than not, English drops the relative pronoun, Czech never does. <em>Který</em> is always preceded by a comma.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-181">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Kdo? Co?</strong></td>
<td align="left">muž, <strong>který</strong> přišel,<br>
auto, <strong>které</strong> nejede</td>
<td align="left">man who came,<br>
car that doesn’t run</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Koho? Co?</strong></td>
<td align="left">muž, <strong>kterého</strong> jsem viděl,<br>
žena, <strong>kterou</strong> znám<br>
auto, <strong>které</strong> jsem koupil</td>
<td align="left">the man I saw<br>
the woman I know<br>
the car I bought</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Koho? Čeho?</strong></td>
<td align="left">muž, <strong>kterého</strong> jsem se zeptal<br>
město, do <strong>kterého</strong> jsem přijel</td>
<td align="left">the man I asked<br>
city in which I arrived</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Komu? Čemu?</strong></td>
<td align="left">kamarád, <strong>kterému</strong> jsem telefonoval<br>
žena, <strong>které</strong> jsem to řekl</td>
<td align="left">a friend I called<br>
woman who I told it to</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Kom? Čem?</strong></td>
<td align="left">muž, o <strong>kterém</strong> jsme mluvili<br>
země, ve <strong>které</strong> je svoboda</td>
<td align="left">man about whom we talked<br>
country in which there is freedom</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Kým? Čím?</strong></td>
<td align="left">člověk, se <strong>kterým</strong> se znám<br>
žena, se <strong>kterou</strong> přišel</td>
<td align="left">man who I know<br>
woman with who I came</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-kdo">Kdo</h3>
<p><em>Kdo</em> (who) is used differently from English. It is common only in set phrases and only for <em>TEN</em> nouns. <em>Kdo</em> is always preceded by a comma. For other genders, <em>který</em> or <em>co</em> is used.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-182">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Kdo? Co?</strong></td>
<td align="left">ten, kdo přišel</td>
<td align="left">he who came</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Koho? Co?</strong></td>
<td align="left">ten, koho znám</td>
<td align="left">he who I know</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Komu? Čemu?</strong></td>
<td align="left">ten, komu to dám</td>
<td align="left">he who I give it to</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Kým? Čím?</strong></td>
<td align="left">ten, s kým přijdeš</td>
<td align="left">he with whom you come</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-co">Co</h3>
<p>In colloquial Czech, you can use <em>co</em> (what) in any relative clause regardless of gender or case. The object must also be expressed again by a pronoun in all transitive verbs where in cases other than Kdo? Co?.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-183">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Kdo? Co?</strong></td>
<td align="left">muž, <strong>co</strong> přišel,<br>
auto, <strong>co</strong> nejede</td>
<td align="left">man who came,<br>
car that doesn’t run</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Koho? Co?</strong></td>
<td align="left">muž, <strong>co</strong> jsem <strong>ho</strong> viděl,<br>
žena, <strong>co ji</strong> znám</td>
<td align="left">the man I saw<br>
the woman I know</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Koho? Čeho?</strong></td>
<td align="left">muž, <strong>co</strong> jsem se <strong>ho</strong> zeptal<br>
město, <strong>co</strong> jsem do <strong>něj</strong> přijel</td>
<td align="left">the man I asked<br>
city in which I arrived</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Komu? Čemu?</strong></td>
<td align="left">kamarád, <strong>co</strong> jsem <strong>mu</strong> telefonoval<br>
žena, <strong>co</strong> jsem <strong>jí</strong> to řekl</td>
<td align="left">a friend I called<br>
woman who I told it to</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Kom? Čem?</strong></td>
<td align="left">muž, <strong>co</strong> jsme <strong>o něm</strong> mluvili<br>
země, <strong>co</strong> <strong>v ní</strong> je svoboda</td>
<td align="left">man about who we talked<br>
country in which there is freedom</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Kým? Čím?</strong></td>
<td align="left">člověk, <strong>co</strong> se <strong>s ním</strong> znám<br>
paní, <strong>co</strong> jsem s <strong>ní</strong> přišel</td>
<td align="left">man who I know<br>
woman who I came with</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-sentences-without-subject">Sentences without Subject</h2>
<p>See how these are used as idioms under <em>Idioms, Phraseology and Vocabulary.</em></p>
<h3 id="reader-content-weather---impersonal-states">Weather - impersonal states</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-184">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Prší.</td>
<td align="left">It's raining.</td>
<td align="left">Bude pršet. Pršelo.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Sněží.</td>
<td align="left">It's snowing.</td>
<td align="left">Bude sněžit. Sněžilo.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Mrzne.</td>
<td align="left">It's freezing.</td>
<td align="left">Bude mrznout. Mrzlo.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Hřmí.</td>
<td align="left">There's thunder.</td>
<td align="left">Bude hřmít. Hřmělo.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Blýská se.</td>
<td align="left">There's lightning.</td>
<td align="left">Bude se blýskat. Blýskalo se.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Taje.</td>
<td align="left">There's a thaw.</td>
<td align="left">Bude tát. Tálo.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-je-adverb"><em>je</em> + adverb</h3>
<p>This construction is used most often for describing states of weather, time, <em>etc.</em></p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-185">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Jak je venku?</td>
<td align="left">How's the weather outside?</td>
<td align="left">Jak bude venku? Jak bylo venku?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je teplo.</td>
<td align="left">It is warm.</td>
<td align="left">Bude teplo. Bylo teplo.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je horko.</td>
<td align="left">It is hot.</td>
<td align="left">Bude horko. Bylo horko.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je hezky.</td>
<td align="left">It is nice.</td>
<td align="left">Bude hezky. Bylo hezky.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je chladno.</td>
<td align="left">It is cold.</td>
<td align="left">Bude chladno. Bylo chladno.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je zima.</td>
<td align="left">It is cold.</td>
<td align="left">Bude zima. Byla zima.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je pět hodin.</td>
<td align="left">It's five o'clock.</td>
<td align="left">Bude pět hodin. Bylo pět hodin.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-feelings---je-komu-čemu-adverb">Feelings - <em>je</em> + <em>Komu? Čemu?</em> + adverb</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-186">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Jak je ti?</td>
<td align="left">How are you feeling?</td>
<td align="left">Jak ti bude? Jak ti bylo?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je mi dobře.</td>
<td align="left">I'm feeling well.</td>
<td align="left">Bude mi dobře.Bylo mi dobře.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je mi zima.</td>
<td align="left">I'm cold.</td>
<td align="left">Bude mi zima. Bylo mi zima.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je mi špatně.</td>
<td align="left">I'm feeling sick.</td>
<td align="left">Bude mi špatně. Bylo mi špatně.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je mi teplo.</td>
<td align="left">I'm hot.</td>
<td align="left">Bude mi teplo. Bylo mi teplo.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Je mi smutno.</td>
<td align="left">I'm feeling sad.</td>
<td align="left">Bude mi smutno. Bylo mi smutno.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>



---

> Section 15 of 22 · Pronunciation · id `pronunciation` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=pronunciation)

<h1 id="reader-content-pronunciation">Pronunciation</h1>
<h2 id="reader-content-pronunciation-of-individual-sounds">Pronunciation of individual sounds</h2>
<p>Czech prounciation is not particularly difficult. It is about as hard as it is to pronounce any language, if it does not happen to be the one you grew up with. This is because your muscles just find it hard to adjust to new things. It is true, however, that pronunciation is often the most difficult part for adult learners and adults very rarely achieve native-like accent.</p>
<p>It is still important to practice pronunciation when learning Czech because Czech native speakers tend to value correct pronunciation over grammar when interacting with non-native speakers. A student of Czech will have an easier time trying to converse with natives speakers when getting the basics of pronunciation right.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-vowels">Vowels</h3>
<p>There are a few peculiarities in Czech that tend to cause problems for English-speaking mouths. The most troublesome is the pronunciation of <strong>vowels</strong>.</p>
<p>1) no matter where they are in the word, Czech vowels never loose their full quality, and are always <strong>pronounced fully</strong> and unflaggingly.</p>
<p>2) each Czech vowel can be either <strong>short or long</strong>. Long vowels are exactly two times longer than their short brothers. It is very frequent that vowel length changes meaning of words significantly. For instance, <em>děla</em> (cannons) is differentiated from <em>dělá</em> (s/he is working) only by the long <em>á</em> in the latter, and there are even more embarrassing examples. The length of vowels is completely independent on the <strong>stress</strong>. The stress is always on the first syllable in all Czech words. It is a very frequent that a long vowel occurs in an unstressed position as in <em><strong>dě</strong>lá</em>. There are two diphthongs <em>ou, au</em> and <em>eu</em> that are pronounced fully, as well.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-consonants">Consonants</h3>
<p>Czech <strong>consonants</strong> are much less awkward. There are many that are the same as or very similar to those in English. There are five, however, that are not in English at all: <em>ch, ď, ť, ň, <strong>ř</strong></em></p>
<ul>
<li><p>of the above <strong>ř</strong> is the most feared one; it is famous for its quirky nature and soft gurgling sound; it is the sound that Czech children learn last (around 5 – 6 years), and a certain portion of the population, including former President Havel, never learn to do it quite right; except for its fame and difficulty (it has been claimed that Czechs invented it deliberately to make Czech harder to learn) <strong>ř</strong> has little importance for learning the language, and many Czechs will be disappointed if you learn it well, which you very likely will</p></li>
<li><p><strong>ch</strong> is the same as in Scottish loch but mispronouncing it will cause no disasterous misunderstandings;</p></li>
<li><p><strong>ď</strong>, <strong>ť</strong>, <strong>ň</strong>, on the other hand have no claims to fame but play a much more important role in the language because they often distinguish between different meanings of similar words</p></li>
</ul>
<p>There are even more issues with sounds which are in both English and Czech but are pronounced just a little differently in Czech. For instance, <em>k, p, t</em> don't have aspiration, so it will be difficult for you to distinguish them from <em>g, b, d</em> when they are the first sound in a word. Czech <em>r</em>'s are rolled very much like the Scottish <em>r</em>'s. Finally, unlike English, Czech <em>h</em> is voiced, which probably makes it the most difficult of Czech sounds for English speakers. Other than that, there's nothing in Czech that will surprise you.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-contrasts">Contrasts</h3>
<p>There are several groups of sounds which have something in common one way or another. <em>Vowels</em> and <em>consonants</em> are one example. Vowels are <em>long</em> and <em>short</em>. Consonants are <em>soft</em> and <em>hard</em>, or <em>voiced</em> and <em>unvoiced</em>. It is important to be aware of this opposition since very often when the form of a word changes the ending consonant changes from hard to soft. See the <em>how to</em> spell it charts below for examples.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-short-and-long-vowels">Short and long vowels</h4>
<p>There are many pairs of Czech words which are distinguished only by the length of the vowels.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-soft-hard-and-neutral-consonants">Soft, hard and neutral consonants</h4>
<p>This distinction is important for the choice of <strong>i</strong> or <strong>y</strong> which are pronounced identically but spelled depending on the preceding consonant.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-187">













<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>hard</strong></td>
<td align="left">d</td>
<td align="left">t</td>
<td align="left">n</td>
<td align="left">ch</td>
<td align="left">k</td>
<td align="left">h</td>
<td align="left">g</td>
<td align="left">r</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">[-y]</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>soft</strong></td>
<td align="left">ď</td>
<td align="left">ť</td>
<td align="left">ň</td>
<td align="left">š</td>
<td align="left">c, č</td>
<td align="left">z</td>
<td align="left">ž</td>
<td align="left">ř</td>
<td>j</td>
<td align="left">[- i]</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The distinction between soft and hard consonants is very important for Czech.</p>
<p>One of the less important implications is the spelling of <em>i</em> and <em>y</em> which are pronounced the same. The <em>i</em> only follows soft consonants, and <em>y</em> hard.</p>
<p>But models of nouns and adjectives are also divided into hard and soft depending on the final consontant. Soft and hard models have different endings.</p>
<p>There is also a group of sounds that are neutral. Neither soft nor hard.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-188">











<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>neutral</strong></td>
<td align="left">b</td>
<td align="left">f</td>
<td align="left">l</td>
<td align="left">m</td>
<td align="left">p</td>
<td align="left">s</td>
<td align="left">v</td>
<td align="left">[- i ]/[-y]</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Czechs have to learn by heart which of the neutral consonants are followed by <strong>i</strong> or <strong>y</strong>. The default is <strong>i</strong> with a list of exception that has to be memorised by every Czech school child.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-voiced-vs-unvoiced-voiceless">Voiced vs unvoiced (voiceless)</h4>
<p>Some consonants are <em>voiced</em> and some are <em>unvoiced</em> (or <em>voiceless</em>). In Czech, it is very important to be aware of the difference because all <em>voiced</em> consonants become <em>unvoiced</em> at the end of words. For instance, <em>muž</em> (man) is pronounced muš. The other rule is that there can only be two consonants of the same quality next to each other, and the second governs the first. For instance, <em>kdy</em> (when) is pronounced gdy, etc.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-189">














<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>voiced</strong></td>
<td align="left">b</td>
<td align="left">v</td>
<td align="left">d</td>
<td align="left">ď</td>
<td align="left">g</td>
<td align="left">h</td>
<td align="left">z</td>
<td align="left">ž</td>
<td align="left">dž</td>
<td align="left">dz</td>
<td align="left">ř</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>unvoiced</strong></td>
<td align="left">p</td>
<td align="left">f</td>
<td align="left">t</td>
<td align="left">ť</td>
<td align="left">k</td>
<td align="left">ch</td>
<td align="left">s</td>
<td align="left">š</td>
<td align="left">č</td>
<td align="left">c</td>
<td align="left"><em>ř</em></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The following consonants do not have unvoiced counterparts, and therefore are not influenced by nor do they do anything to other consonants: <em>r, l, m, n, j</em>. So e.g. <em>z</em> in <em>znát</em> (to know) does not change to <em>s</em> as it would for instance in <em>zpátky</em> (back). Nor does <em>s</em> followed by <em>n</em> change to <em>z</em> as in <em>sníst</em> (to eat up) as it would in <em>les dole</em> (forest below) pronounced as <em>lezdole</em>. See section on Assimilation below for much more detail.</p>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> The last three contrasts only appear in cases of assimilation. The exception is the the loan word <em>džus</em> which forms a minimal pair with another loan word <em>čus</em>. There are other rare loan words where <em>dž</em> appears unassimilated such as <em>maharádža</em> but which do not form minimal pairs with other words.</p>
<p><strong>Note 2:</strong> <em>Ř</em> becomes voiceless when next to another voiceless consonant, e.g. it is pronounced voiceless in <em>tři</em> but voiced in <em>čtyři</em>.</p>

<h2 id="reader-content-pronouncing-syllables-words-and-sentences">Pronouncing syllables, words and sentences</h2>
<p>This section describes what happens when we go beyond the pronunciation of individual sounds.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-end-of-word-devoicing">End of word devoicing</h3>
<p>All consonants at the end of a word are pronounced unvoiced. This is often only in one or two of their declined or conjugated forms. When a word is spelled with a voiced consonant at the end, there are other forms (for instance including a vowel) where it is pronouced voiced. Here are some common patterns for nouns and verbs.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-devoiced-nominative-masculine-singular">Devoiced nominative masculine singular</h4>
<p>Many masculine nouns (of all models) end in a voiced consonant which is devoiced in the nominative singular and voiced in all other forms where an ending is added.</p>

<h4 id="reader-content-devoicing-homophones">Devoicing homophones</h4>
<p>Final consonant devoicing results in a number of homofones. However, they rarely result in true ambiguity because they appear across parts of speech or case. While devoiced homophones do not cause misunderstanding, they are a frequent source of spelling erorrs for native speakers. They are also often used for word play.</p>

<h3 id="reader-content-voicing-assimilation">Voicing assimilation</h3>
<p>There are a few things that happen to individual sounds when put together. Especially consonants are vulnerable. If two consonants meet, the second always makes the first change its voicedness.<a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/complete-table-of-contents.md#reader-content-fn1" class="footnote-ref" id="reader-content-fnref1"><sup>1</sup></a> For instance, in <em>kde</em> the voiced d changes the unvoiced k so that it is pronounced as voiced g. The pause is unvoiced so ending consonants are always pronounced unvoiced. Additionally, the following word always forces the change on the final consonant of the preceding word. Voiced consonants without an unvoiced pair (i.e. <em>m, n, l, r, j</em>) and <em>v </em>do not usually trigger assimilation.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-feminine-genitive-plural-feminine-and-neuter">Feminine genitive plural feminine and neuter</h4>
<p>When the final vowel in a feminine or neuter model is preceded by two consonants, the first consonant is revoiced in Genitive Plural.</p>

<thead>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-8174201bea8cf0fbd35b-r1c1" scope="col" align="left"><strong>Sg</strong></th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-8174201bea8cf0fbd35b-r1c2" scope="col" align="left"><strong>Gen Pl</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>

</tr>

<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-8174201bea8cf0fbd35b-r7c2" align="left">lů<strong>ž</strong>ek</td>

<h4 id="reader-content-k-g-voicing"><em>k</em> > <em>g</em> voicing</h4>
<p>When <em>k </em>is followed by a voiced consonant, it assimilates to the voiced <em>g</em>. Because non-assimilated <em>g</em> is almost always associated with loan words, Czechs do not realize that this is happening and will claim that they in fact pronounce <em>k.</em></p>
<p>This voicing assimilation happens within as well as between words. It is most prominent in the very frequent wh- question words: <em>kde, kdo, kdy</em> which are pronounced <em>gde, gdo, gdy.</em></p>
<p>The final <em>k </em>in <em>jak</em> (how) is often voiced as <em>g</em> when followed by a voiced consonant. For instance, <em>jak do</em> (how to) is pronounced <em>jagdo</em>.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-ř-devoicing"><em>ř</em> devoicing</h3>
<p><em>ř</em> can also be devoiced. Voiceless <em>ř</em> is not an independent sound in Czech and most Czechs have no awareness of its existence. The voiced <em>ř</em> is unique to Czech but the voiceless <em>ř</em> can be found in Polish and other languages.</p>
<p>Devoiced <em>ř</em> appears in two contexts:</p>
<ol>
<li><p>In a consonant cluster where <em>ř</em> is the final consontant preceded by a voiceless consonant.</p></li>
<li><p>When <em>ř</em> is the final consonant (usually in imperatives):</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Most common patterns with devoiced <em>ř</em>:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-198">




<thead>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-da2931984a6604e09c68-r1c1" scope="col" align="left">Pattern</th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-da2931984a6604e09c68-r1c2" scope="col" align="left"><strong>Examples</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-da2931984a6604e09c68-r2c1" scope="row" align="left">kř</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-da2931984a6604e09c68-r2c2" align="left"><em>křída, křest, křen, křesťan, skřítek</em></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-da2931984a6604e09c68-r3c1" scope="row" align="left">př</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-da2931984a6604e09c68-r3c2" align="left"><em>před, přes, příklad, přítel, přát (si), spříst</em></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-da2931984a6604e09c68-r4c1" scope="row" align="left">tř</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-da2931984a6604e09c68-r4c2" align="left"><em>tři, třída, třeba, třít, třást (se), střed, střelit, střep</em></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-da2931984a6604e09c68-r5c1" scope="row" align="left">-ř</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-da2931984a6604e09c68-r5c2" align="left"><em>měř, miř, věř, zvěř</em></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-consonant-clusters">Consonant Clusters</h3>
<p>Another prominent feature of Czech pronunciation is its tendency to cluster consonants. As many as 4 consonants can be in one syllable without any vowels. Thus, <em>zmrzlina</em> (icecream) is in fact a t-syllable word. Examples of monosyllabic words without any vowels are <em>smrt</em>, <em>krk, vlk, smrk, pštros</em>, <em>strom, stvol</em>.</p>
<p>6 consonants in a row can be found in verbs in the past tense such as <em>scvrkl</em> (shrank) forming two syllables and even a 8 consonant rarity could be contemplated in the verb <em>scvrnkls</em> (you flicked [sth] off a surface). There is also the famous Czech sentence <em>Strč prst skrz krk</em> which seemingly lacks vowels completely because <em>r</em> plays the role of the vowel.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-syllables">Syllables</h3>
<p>When you speak, it is not really the sounds you pronounce, it is <em>syllables, words and sentences</em>. Thus, when you practice pronunciation start putting the sounds into syllables and words. You will also find that then they are easier to pronounce.</p>
<p>All syllables have to have at least a vowel or a sound that plays the role of a vowel. In Czech, <em>r</em> and <em>l</em> can play the role of a vowel which is why it is possible to have Czech syllables with just consonants such as <em>krk</em> or <em>vlk</em>. It is possible to have have a syllable with just a vowel. In Czech, there are even two words <em>a</em> and <em>i</em> that are just a vowel. But it is not possible to have a syllable with just one consonant.</p>
<p>Each language has a typical syllable patterns, or combinations of vowels and consonants (VC). In Czech, these patterns are most typical.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-199">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">CV</td>
<td align="left">50%</td>
<td align="left">na, se, já, máma, Lída, kolo, jede, koruna</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">CVC</td>
<td align="left">15%</td>
<td align="left">rok, sůl, nad, sám, Radka, krk, plk</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">CCV</td>
<td align="left">10%</td>
<td align="left">sto, pro,</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">CCVC</td>
<td align="left">8%</td>
<td align="left">proč, krok, strk, smrk, zmrzlina</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">CCCVC</td>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">strom</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">CCCCVC</td>
<td align="left"></td>
<td align="left">pštros</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Most words you find in text, will have three or four syllables. This is partly because most words have an ending added to them which often adds a syllable. Two syllable words are common but single syllable word are relatively rare. Mostly pronouns and prepositions.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-stress">Stress</h3>
<p>Syllables are what carries stress. In Czech, it is always the <strong>first syllable</strong> that is stressed. Each sentence is divided into stress segments.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>| <strong>Mám</strong> | <strong>rád</strong> | <strong>o</strong>vocné | <strong>kned</strong>líky. |</p>
<p>| <strong>Do</strong>brý | <strong>u</strong>čitel | <strong>má</strong> | <strong>rád</strong> | <strong>své</strong> | <strong>stu</strong>denty |</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Notice, that the <strong>first syllable</strong> (in bold) is always stressed even when the vowel is short and the vowel in the following syllable is long. The <strong>unstressed vowels</strong> maintain their quality and are not reduced. This is very different from English. In English, only the stresed vowel is pronounced fully, the others are reduced.</p>
<p>Also, the stress segments are of <strong>different lengths</strong>. In English, the space between two stressed syllables is usually the same. English is a stress-timed language which means that vowels in unstressed syllables are reduced to keep the space between stresses roughly the same. For example, “give me a call” becomes “gimmeuh call” or “I’m going to” becomes “amana”. That’s how knock knock jokes work. Czech is syllable timed and the space between stressed syllables is more variable. This gives it a different rhythm.</p>
<p>These two features make it harder (but not impossible) to rap in Czech. But most importantly, they make it very challenging for English speakers to sound natural. This is the <strong>most important feature of Czech</strong> <strong>pronunciation</strong> to learn well.</p>
<p>Usually, one word occupies one stress segment but sometimes words combine in a way that the stress shifts between them. Usually, prepositions and some particles like <em>mi, mu, si</em> or <em>se</em> are combined with another word to be in a stress segment.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>| <strong>Uči</strong>li se | <strong>čes</strong>ky| <strong>od</strong> rána | <strong>do</strong> noci. |</p>
<p>| <strong>Mě</strong>la jsem mu | <strong>rá</strong>no | <strong>za</strong>volat. |</p>
</blockquote>
<h3 id="reader-content-intonation">Intonation</h3>
<h4 id="reader-content-intonation-rules">Intonation Rules</h4>
<p>There are basically two types of intonation in Czech:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>simply descending</strong> which is used for simple statements and questions with question word</p></li>
<li><p><strong>flat, rising on the last syllable</strong> which is used for YES/NO questions</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-200">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>!</strong></td>
<td>No matter what the intonation pattern, the stress is always on the first syllable of every word which gives the intonation a slight rising pattern on each word.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></li>
</ul>

> Section 16 of 22 · Spelling · id `spelling` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=spelling)

<h1 id="reader-content-spelling">Spelling</h1>
<p>Czech spelling is reputed to be very regular. It is true in that it is learnable. But it is not completely straightforward. The are basically three types of rules that are at work.</p>
<ol>
<li><p><strong>Letter = sound</strong>: a certain combination is always pronounced in a certain way. For instance, if you see <em>á</em> you pronounce it as aaaargh.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Letter combinations</strong> are pronounced in a certain way. For instance, ě in mě is pronounced differently than when it follows vě, or dě.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Assimilation of consonants</strong> described in the chapter on Pronunciation</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Exceptions:</strong> Some words or patterns of words are known to be spelled differently from the rules 1-3. Most common with some uses of <em>i</em> vs <em>y</em>.</p></li>
</ol>

<h2 id="reader-content-czech-accent-marks---diacritics">Czech accent marks - Diacritics</h2>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-210">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>´</strong></p></td>
<td><p>čárka</p></td>
<td><p>used to lengthen vowels (the vowel is the same only twice as long)</p>
<p>can be used with all vowel with the same effect</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>ˇ</strong></p></td>
<td><p>háček</p></td>
<td><p>used to soften consonants <em>c, d, n, r, s, t, z</em></p>
<p>if <em>d, t, n</em> are followed by <em>e</em> the ˇ is placed over the <em>e</em>, when followed by <em>i/í</em> the ˇ is dropped (see below)</p>
<p>(<strong>Note:</strong> In print, háček ˇ over <em>d</em> and <em>t</em> is displayed as an apostrophe ’: <em>ť, ď</em>.)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>°</strong></p></td>
<td><p>kroužek</p></td>
<td><p>only used with <em>u</em> if it is not the first letter in the word; otherwise ´ is used. For instance, <strong>ú</strong>kol vs dom<strong>ů</strong> or s<strong>ů</strong>l</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-spelling-rules-for-i-and-ě">Spelling rules for <em>i</em> and <em>ě</em></h2>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-211">








<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>letter</strong></td>
<td><strong>+</strong></td>
<td colspan="2"><strong>letter</strong></td>
<td><strong>is pronounced</strong></td>
<td><strong>as in</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>n</td>
<td>+</td>
<td>i</td>
<td>í</td>
<td>ňi / ňí</td>
<td>nyní</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>t</td>
<td>+</td>
<td>i</td>
<td>í</td>
<td>ťi / ťí</td>
<td>titíž</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>d</td>
<td>+</td>
<td>i</td>
<td>í</td>
<td>ďi / ďí</td>
<td>jdi</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>n</td>
<td>+</td>
<td>ě</td>
<td></td>

</tr>

<td>d</td>

<td>b/p/v</td>

</tr>
</tbody>

<p>The above chart describes how <em>n, t, d, m, b, p, v</em> are pronounced when followed by <em>ě</em> or <em>i/í</em>. Notice that the <em>ě</em> is pronounced as a regular <em>e</em>.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-use-of-y-vs.-i">Use of <em>y</em> vs. <em>i</em></h2>
<p>The distinction between <strong><em>Y</em></strong> and. <strong><em>I</em></strong> is the most irregular phenomenon in the Czech spelling system. Both <strong><em>y</em></strong> and <strong><em>i</em></strong> denote identical sounds. <strong><em>Y</em></strong> is used regularly after all the hard consonants and never after soft consonants. Its use after ambiguous consonants is completely arbitrary and the words which use <strong><em>y</em></strong> have to be memorized by rote (even by native speakers.)</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-basic-rules-of-use-of-i-and-y-after-consonants">Basic rules of use of <em>i</em> and <em>y</em> after consonants</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-212">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="3"><p><strong>i<br>
í</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>after <strong>ž, š, c, č, ř, j, ď, ť, ň</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>děti, život, žít, jít</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>after <strong>b, f, m , l , p, s, v, z</strong> in all words where <strong>y</strong> is not specifically required</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>bít, lid, dopis, vila</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>after <strong>l</strong> in past plural form of verbs if the subject is <em>masculine animate</em></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>muži šli, Petr a Jana šli (masc. takes precendence)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td rowspan="3"><p><strong>y</strong></p>
<p><strong>ý</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>after <strong>h, ch, k, r, d, t, n</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>nohy, roky, ryba, rány</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>after <strong>b, f, m , l , p, s, v, z</strong> for prescribed words (can be found in Czech spelling manuals)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>být, byt, mýt, lyže, sypat, vyjít, vykat, brzy, jazyk, …</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>after <strong><em>l</em></strong> in past plural form of verbs if the subject is not <em>masculine animate</em></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>ženy a děti šly</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-213">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">!</td>
<td align="left">There are a few instances where <em>i</em> and <em>y</em> distinguish the meaning of two words. Most notably <em>bít</em> (to beat) vs <em>být</em> (to be) and <em>mít</em> (to have) vs <em>mýt</em> (to wash). In spoken language, however, these words are homonymous.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-ú-vs.-ů"><em>ú</em> vs. <em>ů</em></h2>
<p>The only difference is the position in the word.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-214">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="2"><strong>ú</strong></td>
<td>beginning of words</td>
<td>úkol, úspěch, účet,</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>words where a prefix is added to the beginning of a word whose root begins with <strong>ú</strong></td>
<td>účet - zúčtovat, úhel - pravoúhlý</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>ů</strong></td>
<td>middle and end of word</td>
<td>různý, způsob, Petrů</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-using-capital-letters">Using Capital Letters</h2>
<p>Czech rules for the use of capital letters are different from English. Titles of books and articles, and names of states and institutions all capitalize only the first word. Only cities with two names capitalize both. Also not capitalized are names of days of the week and months. Even words like names of nations are not capitalized when used as adjectives. Names of languages are also not capitalized.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-punctuation">Punctuation</h2>
<p>Czech punctuation rules are much stricter then those of English.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-215">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="3"><p><strong>,</strong></p></td>
<td rowspan="3"><p>čárka</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>After all conjunctions except <strong><em>a, nebo, i</em></strong>.</p></td>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p>Neví to, protože je hloupý. Není tu, ale poslal peníze.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>Always to separate clauses.</p></td>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p>Protože neměl peníze, nepřišel.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>Always to separate inserts longer than one word.</p></td>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p>Slíbil to, ale, protože neměl peníze, neudělal to.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>;</strong></p></td>
<td><p>středník</p></td>
<td colspan="3" align="left"><p>Rarely used in Czech texts, Czech typewriter keyboard doesn't even include it.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>:</strong></p></td>
<td><p>dvojtečka</p></td>
<td colspan="3" align="left"><p>Similar to English.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>?</strong></p></td>
<td><p>otazník</p></td>
<td colspan="3" align="left"><p>Similar to English.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>!</strong></p></td>
<td><p>vykřičník</p></td>
<td colspan="2" align="left"><p>Used always at the end of imperative sentences.</p>
<p>Not usually used for exclamations.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Jdi domů!</em> (Go home!)</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
