> Section 17 of 22 · Idioms, Phraseology and Vocabulary · id `idioms-phraseology-and-vocabulary` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=idioms-phraseology-and-vocabulary)

<h1 id="reader-content-idioms-phraseology-and-vocabulary">Idioms, Phraseology and Vocabulary</h1>
<p>This section describes the very important parts of language that usually fall somewhere between the grammar, dictionary and the phrasebook. You rarely find them in one place. But without them, you can never consider yourself to be proficient in a language.</p>
<p>The list here is not exhaustive and is presented from the point of view of an English speaking learner. We focused on things that are different from English and a common point of confusion. However, they are real patterns and learners coming from any language can benefit from them.</p>
<p>This section is forever incomplete. There is a long list of subsections in progress that may appear in future editions.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-words-with-a-difference">Words with a difference</h2>
<p>This section is particularly English centric. It describes words the usage of which is in some special way different from English. Speakers of other languages may find them unremarkable. These differences go both ways, often speakers of Czech will make mistakes in English because of confusions arising from these words.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-ask-zeptat-se-vs.-požádat">ask: zeptat se vs. požádat</h3>
<p>There are two very distinct ways of asking. One is asking for information and the other is asking for a favor. This translates into two Czech words:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-216">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>zeptat se</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>požádat</strong></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>Zeptal se kolik je hodin.<br>
(<em>He asked what time it was.</em>)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Požádal ho, aby odešel.</p>
<p>(<em>He asked him to leave.</em>)</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> Often, <em>říct</em> [<em>tell to</em>] will be used instead of <em>požádat</em>. <em>They asked him to leave.</em> will be translated as <em>Řekli mu aby odešel</em>. [They told him to leave.]</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-how-to-say-czech-in-czech">How to say Czech in Czech</h3>
<p>Where there is one word in English there are five in Czech.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-217">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">čeština</td>
<td align="left">Czech language</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Češka</td>
<td align="left">Czech woman</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Čech</td>
<td align="left">Czech man</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">český, česká, české</td>
<td align="left">Czech (adj.) as in Czech Republic</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">česky</td>
<td align="left">Czech as in <em>I speak Czech</em> - an adverb</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Compare this with the words for English!</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-218">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">angličtina</td>
<td>English language</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Angličanka</td>
<td>English woman</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">Angličan</td>
<td>English man</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">anglický, anglická, anglické</td>
<td>English (tea)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2">anglicky</td>
<td>English as in <em>I speak English</em> - an adverb</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>!</td>
<td colspan="2">Note that only the names of nationalities are spelled with a capital letter.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-to-cut-in-czech">to cut in Czech</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-219">





<thead>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r1c1" scope="col"></th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r1c2" scope="col" align="left">to (verb) (ipf, pf)</th>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r1c3" scope="col" align="left">a (noun)</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r2c1" scope="row">cut (with scissors)</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r2c2" align="left">stříhat, střihnout</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r2c3" align="left">střih</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r3c1" scope="row">cut (with an ax)</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r3c2" align="left">sekat, seknout</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r3c3" align="left">sek</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<th id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r4c1" scope="row">cut (with a saw, knife)</th>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r4c2" align="left">řezat, říznout</td>
<td id="reader-content-czech-semantic-table-61c24c69a77df9c1a98c-r4c3" align="left">řez</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-first-in-czech"><em>First</em> in Czech</h3>
<p>The word <em>first</em> has two equivalents in Czech in different contexts.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-220">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">First from a group.</td>
<td align="left">Přišel <strong>první<em>.</em></strong></td>
<td align="left">He arrived <strong><em>first</em></strong>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">First in a sequence of activities.</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Nejdříve</strong> snídám.</td>
<td align="left"><em><strong>First</strong>,</em> I have breakfast.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-functionwork">function/work</h3>
<p>To describe something as functioning, Czech uses the metaphor of motion and not of work. Therefore, the word <em>pracovat</em> can never be used in this context. Here are some examples:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-221">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>fungovat</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>jít/chodit</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>běžet</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Motor funguje.<br>
(The engine works.)</td>
<td align="left">Nejde mi počítač.<br>
(My computer doesn’t work.)</td>
<td align="left">Naše televize už zase běží. (Out TV works again.)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-going-on-foot-and-by-vehicle"><em>Going</em> on foot and by vehicle</h3>
<p>Where English has one word meaning <em>to go,</em> Czech has two. One for <em>going on foot,</em> and second for <em>going by a vehicle</em> (car, bike, train, bus).</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-222">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="4" align="center"><img src="media/image89.svg" alt="Walk">{width=“0.7903226159230097in” | <img src="media/image91.svg" alt="Car">{width=“1.0in” | height=“0.7903226159230097in”} | height=“1.0in”}</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" align="center"><strong>jít</strong></td>
<td colspan="2" align="center"><strong>jet</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center">jdu</td>
<td align="center">jdeme</td>
<td align="center">jedu</td>
<td align="center">jedeme</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center">jdeš</td>
<td align="center">jdete</td>
<td align="center">jedeš</td>
<td align="center">jedete</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center">jde</td>
<td align="center">jdou</td>
<td align="center">jede</td>
<td align="center">jedou</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-223">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>jít</strong></td>
<td align="left">pěšky</td>
<td align="left">on foot</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>jet</strong></td>
<td align="left">vlakem, autem, autobusem, na kole, na koni</td>
<td align="left">by train, car, bus; on a bike, horse</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The same difference shows up in other verbs of motion as well. For example: <em>nést</em> (carry in hand) vs. <em>vézt</em> (carry by vehicle).</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-funny-happy-lucky-vs.-veselý-šťastný-směšný">funny, happy, lucky vs. veselý, šťastný, směšný</h3>
<p>There are many words in Czech to match the English funny, happy and lucky, but none of them are a perfect match.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-224">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td>funny (laughable)</td>
<td align="left">směšný</td>
<td align="left">happy (optimistic)</td>
<td align="left">šťastný</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>funny (amusing)</td>
<td align="left">zábavný</td>
<td align="left">happy (cheerful)</td>
<td align="left">veselý</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>funny (strange)</td>
<td align="left">podivný</td>
<td align="left">to be lucky</td>
<td align="left">mít štěstí</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-fun-in-czech">fun in Czech</h3>
<p>There is no easy way to express the English word ‘fun’ in Czech, in all its contexts. Here are some example phrases. They often use words like <em>zábava</em> (entertainment), <em>legrace</em> (humour), <em>prča</em> (informal)</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-225">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">We’ll have fun.</td>
<td align="left">Bude zábava. Budeme se bavit.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">They’re having fun.</td>
<td align="left">Dobře se baví.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">She is fun. She is a fun person.</td>
<td align="left">Je s ní zábava. Je s ní legrace.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-to-know-in-czech">to know in Czech</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-226">








<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>vědět</strong></p></td>
<td><p>+</p></td>
<td><p>že ..., co ..., kdo ..., kdy …, etc.</p></td>
<td><p>Vím, kdo jsi.</p>
<p>Víš, kde je Praha?</p>
<p>Víš, co to je?</p></td>
<td><p>I know who you are.</p>
<p>Do you know where Prague is?</p>
<p>Do you know what it is?</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>znát</strong></p></td>
<td><p>+</p></td>
<td><p>Koho? Co?</p></td>
<td><p>Znám pana Pavla.</p>
<p>Znám Prahu.</p>
<p>Znáš Janu? Znám ji.</p></td>
<td><p>I know Mr. Pavel.</p>
<p>I know Prague.</p>
<p>Do you know Jana? I know her.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2"><p><strong>umět</strong></p></td>
<td><p>+</p></td>
<td><p>infinitive</p>
<p>(noun, adverb)</p></td>
<td><p>Umím zpívat.</p>
<p>Umíš hrát košíkovou?</p>
<p>Umíš česky?</p></td>
<td><p>I can sing.</p>
<p>Can you play basketball?</p>
<p>Do you speak Czech?</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>!</p></td>
<td colspan="5"><p>Sometimes, the infinitive after <strong><em>umět</em></strong> is only assumed, and <strong><em>umět</em></strong> is followed directly by a noun or an adverb. E.g. <em>Umím (vařit) polévku.</em> or <em>Umím (mluvit) česky.</em></p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-illnesses-and-treatments-that-are-and-that-are-not">Illnesses and treatments that are and that are not</h3>
<p>Despite the advances in international medicine, there are still interesting differences in the popular terminology of different languages.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-227">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>rýma</strong></td>
<td align="left"><em>cold</em></td>
<td align="left">Czech cure for a stuffy nose is to blow your nose. Even in public. Americans prefer to sniff. Mutually socially unacceptable.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>angína</strong></td>
<td align="left"><em>tonsillitis</em></td>
<td align="left">Very common complaint for Czechs, often used for conditions not technically a tonsillitis but more of a soar throat.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>angína pectoris</strong></td>
<td align="left"><em>angina</em></td>
<td align="left">Commonly confused in Czech-English translations.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>nachlazení</strong></td>
<td align="left"><em>catch a chill</em></td>
<td align="left">Not as important a complaint in English (hence no noun). Czech cure is no ice-cream, keep warm and prevent drafts (<em>průvan</em>).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>obklad</strong></td>
<td align="left"><em>compress</em></td>
<td align="left">Common Czech cure for soar throat, headache, sprains, etc.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>pálení žáhy</strong></td>
<td align="left"><em>heartburn</em></td>
<td align="left">Seems slightly more common in English.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>sádra</strong></td>
<td align="left"><em>plaster cast</em></td>
<td align="left">Seems to be a slightly more popular cure for broken and sprained limbs.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>anestetikum</strong></td>
<td align="left"><em>novocaine</em></td>
<td align="left">Used only sparingly (for most serious operations) by Czech dentists. Brand as such not known to Czechs.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-jaký-vs.-který-what-vs.-which">Jaký vs. Který / What <em>vs.</em> Which</h3>
<p>This is a very easy distinction which is also infinitely tricky. The two Czech words correspond almost exactly to two English words. But in English <em>what</em> can often be used instead of which, which is very confusing when translated directly into Czech.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-228">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>jaký</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Jaký chceš rohlík?</p>
<p>Chci dobrý rohlík.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>What roll do you want.</p>
<p>I want a good roll.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>expresses quality independent of surroundings</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>který</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Který rohlík chceš?</p>
<p>Chci ten nalevo.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Which roll do you want?</p>
<p>I want the one on the left.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>chooses one out of a group of many</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-jak-vs.-jaký-adverbs-vs.-adjectives">Jak vs. Jaký / Adverbs vs. Adjectives</h3>
<p>This is an important distinction, which is also expressed in English, although not so strictly.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-229">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Jak se máš?</td>
<td align="left">Mám se dobře.</td>
<td align="left">Jaký je to banán?</td>
<td>To je český banán.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">How are you?</td>
<td align="left">I'm well.</td>
<td align="left">What kind of banana is it?</td>
<td>It is a Czech banana.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Jak pracuješ?</td>
<td align="left">Pracuju pomalu.</td>
<td align="left">Jaká je tvoje práce.</td>
<td>Moje práce je zajímavá.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">How do you work.</td>
<td align="left">I work slowly.</td>
<td align="left">What is your work like?</td>
<td>My work is interesting.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-ještě-ne-not-yet-vs.-už-ne-not-anymore"><em>Ještě ne</em> – Not yet vs. <em>Už ne</em> – Not anymore</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-230">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Ještě ne – not yet</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Už ne – not anymore</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Ještě to nevím. – <em>I don’t know it yet.</em></td>
<td align="left">Už to nevím. – <em>I don’t know it anymore.</em></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Ještě jsem tam nebyl. – <em>I haven’t been there yet.</em></td>
<td align="left">Už jsem tam nebyl. – <em>I was not there anymore.</em></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Ještě tam nebudu. – <em>I won’t be there yet.</em></td>
<td align="left">Už tam nebudu. – <em>I won’t be there anymore.</em></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-use-of-jejsou-is-vs.-there-is">Use of je/jsou – is vs. there is</h3>
<p>The Czech <em>je</em> has several uses that require slightly different translations into English. The translation may depend on word order or on the general meaning.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-231">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>je = is, jsou = are</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>je = there is, jsou = there are</strong></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>*Herec je ve filmu.* = The actor is in a film.</p>
<p>*Děti jsou doma.* = The children are at home.</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>*Ve filmu je herec.* = There is an actor in the film.</p>
<p>*Doma jsou děti.* = There are children at home.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> The same principle applies in the future (<em>there will be/bude</em>) and past (<em>there was/byl</em>).</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-ještě-still-vs.-už-already"><em>Ještě</em> – Still vs. <em>Už</em> – Already</h3>
<p>Although relatively simple, these two words can cause quite a few problems. Compare their usage in negative and positive sentences. Notice how the meaning of <em>už</em> changes from past to present. With the future tense <em>ještě</em> and <em>už</em> can substitute for the future perfect in English, as in <em>When you come, it will have been finished.</em> – <em>Až přijdete, už to bude hotové.</em> Unlike their English equivalents, both <em>ještě</em> and <em>už</em> always appear in the beginning of the sentence.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-232">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Ještě – still</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Už – already, finally</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Ještě jsem tady. – <em>I’m still here.</em></td>
<td align="left">Už jsem tady. – <em>I’m finally here.</em></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Ještě jsem tam byl. – <em>I was still there.</em></td>
<td align="left">Už jsem tam byl. – <em>I was already there.</em></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Ještě tam budu. – <em>I will still be there.</em></td>
<td align="left">Už tam budu. – <em>I will be there already.</em></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-last-in-czech---minulý-vs-poslední">Last in Czech - minulý vs poslední</h3>
<p>Czech has two words to express the equivalent of the English last.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-233">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="2"><p><strong>poslední</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>last</em> in a series</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Byl poslední.</em> - He finished last.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><em>latest</em> event</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>poslední móda</em> - latest fashion</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>minulý</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>past</em> event</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>minulý rok</em> - last year</p>
<p><em>minulý</em> týden - last week</p>
<p><em>minulý čas</em> - past tense</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-make-vs.-do-vs.-work">make vs. do vs. work</h3>
<p>Most of the uses of the English make are translated by <em>dělat/udělat</em> into Czech. Only the sense of actually manufacturing something will use the verb <em>vyrábět/vyrobit</em>.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-234">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>**dělat/udělat** (do, make, work)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>pracovat</strong><br>
(work)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>**vyrábět/vyrobit** (make)</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p>Udělám dnes večer jídlo.<br>
(I will make dinner tonight.)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Pracuju v kanceláři.</p>
<p>(I work in an office.)</p></td>
<td align="left"><p>Petr musel tu součástku vyrobit.</p>
<p>(Petr had to make that part.)</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-meet-potkat-vs.-setkat-se">meet: potkat vs. setkat se</h3>
<p>You can either meet someone by appointment or by accident. Czech has two separate words for this. <em>Potkat</em> means to meet by accident and <em>setkat se</em> means to meet by prior arrangement. <em>Setkat</em> <em>se</em> can also be expressed as <em>mít schůzku</em> (to have a meeting, appointment).</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-235">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>potkat</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>setkat se</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Petr potkal Janu na ulici.<br>
(<em>Petr met/bumped into Jana in the street.</em>)</td>
<td align="left">Setkali se s prezidentem v deset hodin.<br>
(<em>They met the president at 10 o’clock.</em>)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-na-vs.-v">na vs. v</h3>
<p>The prototypical meaning of <em>na</em> is <em>on</em> while the prototypical meaning of <em>v</em> is <em>in</em>. For a variety of places, though, <em>na</em> is used instead of <em>v</em> with the same meaning. For example, <em>na Moravě.</em></p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-236">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>countries that are islands or peninsulas</strong></td>
<td align="left">Gibraltar, Hawaii, Island, Nový Zéland (but <strong>not</strong> Austrálie, Grónsko, Británie, Irsko, Itálie or Španělsko)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>selected other countries</strong></td>
<td align="left">Morava, Slovensko, Ukrajina, Podkarpatská Rus</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>local places on top of hills</strong></td>
<td align="left">Vinohrady in Prague, or Kladno near Prague (people from outside the region may use <em>v</em>)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>some institutions</strong></td>
<td align="left">pošta, policie, univerzita, fakulta, gymnázium</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>selected places</strong></td>
<td align="left">náměstí, diskotéka, výstava, nádraží</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>activities (as destinations)</strong></td>
<td align="left">výlet, procházka, opera, hra, film</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-translating-no-into-czech">Translating <em>no</em> into Czech</h3>
<p>The English word <em>no</em> has two different meanings which require two different words in Czech.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-237">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong><em>no</em> as <em>ne</em></strong></p></td>
<td><p><strong>no as žádný</strong></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>Máte chleba? Ne, nemáme.</p>
<p>(Do you have bread? No, we don’t.)</p></td>
<td><p>Žádný student nenapsal test.</p>
<p>(No student wrote the test.)</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-use-of-to-this-vs.-that">Use of <em>to</em> (this vs. that)</h3>
<p>The Czech <em>to</em> can be confusing because it can be translated as two different words:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-238">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><strong>to = this, that</strong></p></td>
<td align="left"><p><strong>to = it</strong></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><p><em>To je problém.</em> = This/that is a problem.</p>
<p><em>To je Karel.</em> = This/that is Karel .</p></td>
<td align="left"><p><em>Bylo to dobré.</em> = It was good.</p>
<p><em>Kdo je to?</em> = Who is it?.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Sometimes, it is difficult to decide on a good translation.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-there-isare-in-czech">there is/are in Czech</h3>
<p>Czech doesn't have the construction <em>there is/there are</em>. Czech speakers have many difficulties mastering this construction in English.</p>
<p>You simply use '<em>je</em>' or '<em>jsou</em>'. Sometimes it is possible to use '<em>má</em>' or '<em>mají</em>' or even ‘<em>existuje</em>’.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-239">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>there is a castle in Prague</p></td>
<td><p>v Praze <strong>je</strong> hrad</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>there are problems in the USA</p></td>
<td><p>v USA <strong>jsou</strong> problémy<br>
v USA <strong>mají</strong> problémy<br>
USA <strong>mají</strong> problémy</p>
<p>v USA <strong>existují</strong> problémy</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Note</strong> that sometimes you must add 'tam' or 'tady', as in:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-240">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">there's a car over there</td>
<td align="left">tam <strong>je</strong> auto<br>
tam <strong>máme/mají</strong> auto</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">there's a car here</td>
<td align="left">tady <strong>je</strong> auto</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-idioms-set-expressions-and-useful-patterns">Idioms, set expressions and useful patterns</h2>
<p>Idioms are expressions that are usually used as whole chunks in which just a small part can be substituted. They also</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-animal-metaphors">Animal metaphors</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-241">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">lev</td>
<td align="left">lion</td>
<td align="left">hard working, strong</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">rys</td>
<td align="left">lynx</td>
<td align="left">great eyes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">liška</td>
<td align="left">fox</td>
<td align="left">clever, crafty</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">sova</td>
<td align="left">owl</td>
<td align="left">wise</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">vlk</td>
<td align="left">wolf</td>
<td align="left">hungry</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">myš</td>
<td align="left">mouse</td>
<td align="left">quiet</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-agreeing-and-disagreeing">Agreeing and disagreeing</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-242">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">I (don’t) agree.</td>
<td align="left">(Ne)Souhlasím</td>
<td align="left">Agreement</td>
<td align="left">Shoda/Dohoda</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-mental-and-physical-healthfeelings-i-am-vs.-it-is-vs.-i-have-a">Mental and Physical Health/Feelings <em>I am</em> vs. <em>it is</em> vs. <em>I have a</em></h3>
<p>There are many phrases that cannot be translated directly from English to Czech. Often, groups of phrases have a similar translation. You need to memorize which phrases translate in what way. Most commonly, the literal translations oscillate between <em>to have/to be</em> or some other verb.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-i-am-mámnemám">I am = mám/nemám</h4>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-243">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">I’m thirsty.</td>
<td align="left">Mám žízeň.</td>
<td align="left">I’m hungry.</td>
<td align="left">Mám hlad.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">I’m busy.</td>
<td align="left">Nemám čas.</td>
<td align="left">I’m free/available.</td>
<td align="left">Mám čas/volno.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">I’m lucky.</td>
<td align="left">Mám štěstí.</td>
<td align="left">I’m not lucky.</td>
<td align="left">Mám smůlu.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">I’m happy/glad.</td>
<td align="left">Mám radost.</td>
<td align="left">I’m depressed.</td>
<td align="left">Mám depresi.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-i-am-je-mi">I am = je mi</h4>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-244">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><em>I’m cold.</em></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Je mi zima.</strong></td>
<td align="left">I’m warm.</td>
<td align="left">Je mi teplo.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">I'm sick.</td>
<td align="left">Je mi špatně.</td>
<td align="left">I’m well.</td>
<td align="left">Je mi dobře.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">I’m sorry.</td>
<td align="left">Je mi líto.*</td>
<td align="left">I’m sad.</td>
<td align="left">Je mi smutno.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>* <em>I’m sorry</em> only translates as <em>je mi líto</em> in the context of expressing regret and not in apologizing, where <em>promiňte</em>, or <em>pardon</em> is used. Another equivalent is <em>mrzí mě</em>.</p>
<h4 id="reader-content-i-have-a-mám">I have (a) = mám</h4>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-245">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><em>I have a cold</em></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Mám rýmu.</strong></td>
<td align="left">I have a cough.</td>
<td align="left">Mám kašel.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><em>I have flu.</em></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Mám chřipku.</strong></td>
<td align="left">I have tonsillitis.</td>
<td align="left">Mám angínu.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-i-have-a-ache-bolí-mě">I have a …ache = bolí mě …</h4>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-246">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><em>I have a headache.</em></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Bolí mě hlava.</strong></td>
<td align="left">I have a toothache.</td>
<td align="left">Bolí mě zuby.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-it-is-je-adverb-weather">it is = je + adverb (weather)</h4>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-247">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><em>It is cold.</em></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Je zima.</strong></td>
<td align="left">It’s nice.</td>
<td align="left">Je hezky.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-i-am-jsem">I am = jsem</h4>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-248">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>I’m glad.</p></td>
<td><p>Jsem rád.</p></td>
<td><p>I’m very happy.</p></td>
<td><p>Jsem šťastný.</p>
<p>Jsem veselý.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-i-am-a-verb">I am = a verb</h4>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-249">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">I'm bored.</td>
<td align="left">Nudím se.</td>
<td align="left">I’m in a hurry.</td>
<td align="left">Spěchám.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-expressing-possibility-and-necessity">Expressing Possibility and Necessity</h3>
<p>Similarly to English, both of these can be expressed through modal verbs. For example,</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-250">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Possibility/Impossibility</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>Necessity/Lack of necessity</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">lze, nelze</td>
<td align="left">třeba, potřeba, zapotřebí</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">možno, možné</td>
<td align="left">nutno, nutné</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">asi</td>
<td align="left">nezbytné, nevyhnutné, neodvratitelné</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> <em>možná, třeba</em> are also used as <em>maybe</em>.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-incorrect-idioms-resulting-from-confusion-with-english">Incorrect idioms resulting from confusion with English</h3>
<p>Very often, what you say in English does not sound very Czech when translated literally. Here are a few examples of the more common mistranslations of English phrases.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-251">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">No problem.</td>
<td align="left">Není problém.</td>
<td align="left">To je v pořádku.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Is it OK?</td>
<td align="left">Je to dobré?</td>
<td align="left">Nevadí to?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">How are you? So-so.</td>
<td align="left">Jak se máte? Tak-tak.</td>
<td align="left">Jde to. Docela dobře.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">I am happy.</td>
<td align="left">Jsem šťastný.</td>
<td align="left">Mám radost. Je mi dobře. Jsem veselý.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">I have a question.</td>
<td align="left">Mám otázku.</td>
<td align="left">Můžu se zeptat?</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-social-interactions">Social interactions</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-arranging-appointments">Arranging appointments</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-252">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">We’ll meet at…</td>
<td>Sejdeme se v/u…</td>
<td align="left">I will drop in later.</td>
<td>Stavím se později.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-asking-questions-with-negatives">Asking questions with negatives</h3>
<p>Czechs often ask negative questions to show politeness where English would use <em>would</em> or just ask a direct question. For instance, <strong><em>Nevíte kde je tady nádraží?</em></strong>, <strong><em>Není tady pan Novák?</em></strong>, <strong><em>Nemáte housky?</em></strong> = Do you know where the train station is? Is Mr. Novák here? Do you have housky?</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-253">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Nevíte, kde je nádraží?</td>
<td>Where is the train station, please?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Nevíte, kolik je hodin?</td>
<td>Would you know what time it is?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Nemáte rohlíky?</td>
<td>Do you have rohlíky?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Nechceš jít dnes do kina?</td>
<td>Would you like to go to movies?</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-assurances">Assurances</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-254">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">That’s OK.</td>
<td align="left">To je v pořádku.</td>
<td align="left">There's no hurry.</td>
<td align="left">To nespěchá.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-female-forms-of-occupation-names">Female forms of occupation names</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-255">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">učitel, řidič, fotograf, ředitel,</td>
<td align="left"><strong>-ka</strong></td>
<td>učitelka, řidička, fotografka, ředitelka</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">dělník, úředník</td>
<td align="left"><strong>-ice</strong></td>
<td>dělnice, úřednice</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">soudce, důchodce</td>
<td align="left"><strong>-kyně</strong></td>
<td>soudkyně, důchodkyně</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">vrátný</td>
<td align="left"><strong>-ý > -á</strong></td>
<td>vrátná</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">průvodčí, vrchní</td>
<td align="left"><strong>-í > -í</strong></td>
<td>průvodčí, vrchní</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>In Czech, most nouns for occupations have both a male and a female form. The female form is typically formed from the male form.</p>
<p>The <em>vrátný/vrátná</em> and <em>vrchní</em> models are conjugated as adjectives.</p>
<p>Two professions in particular are so commonly associated with females that they have no easy male equivalent. They are <em>zdravotní sestra</em> (nurse) and <em>sekretářka</em> (secretary). A male secretary could be <em>asistent</em> (although there is <em>asistentka</em>) or <em>tajemník</em> (which would be used for the Secretary of the UN). <em>Sekretář</em> would be used for a piece of furniture. There is no easy way of saying male nurse since <em>sestra</em> means sister.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-formation-of-czech-last-names">Formation of Czech last names</h3>
<p>All Czech women's surnames end in <em>–<strong>ová</strong></em> and are conjugated as adjectives (with the exception of those ending in <strong><em>-ů</em></strong>).</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-256">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">pan</td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">paní</td>
<td align="left">manželé</td>
<td align="left"><img src="media/image92-wmf.png"> rodina</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Novák</td>
<td>+ová</td>
<td align="left">Novák<strong>ová</strong></td>
<td align="left">Novák<strong>ovi+</strong></td>
<td align="left">Novák<strong>ova++</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Čapek</td>
<td>+ová</td>
<td align="left">Čapk<strong>ová</strong>*</td>
<td align="left">Čapk<strong>ovi</strong></td>
<td align="left">Čapk<strong>ova</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Frýba</td>
<td>+ová</td>
<td align="left">Frýb<strong>ová</strong></td>
<td align="left">Frýb<strong>ovi</strong></td>
<td align="left">Frýb<strong>ova</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Novotn<strong>ý</strong>**</td>
<td>ý <strong>></strong> á</td>
<td align="left">Novotn<strong>á</strong></td>
<td align="left">Novotn<strong>í</strong></td>
<td align="left">Novotn<strong>ých</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Jank<strong>ů</strong></td>
<td>ů</td>
<td align="left">Jank<strong>ů</strong></td>
<td align="left">Jank<strong>ů</strong></td>
<td align="left">Jank<strong>ů</strong>***</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>* C<strong>e</strong>C + ová <strong>></strong> CCová</p>
<p>** These surnames have the form of an adjective and they are conjugated using the hard adjective model.</p>
<p>*** Surnames ending in –ů are rare and are mostly derived from first names like Petrů, Karlů, Janů. They do not change their form. Equivalent surnames without the –ů also exist: Petr-Petrová-Petrovi.</p>
<p>+ These forms are conjugated according to the hard adjective plural model.</p>
<p>++ These forms are conjugated according to the possessive adjective model.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-liking-in-czech-mám-rád-...-a-líbí-se-mi-...">Liking in Czech (<em>Mám rád</em> ... a <em>Líbí se mi ...)</em></h3>
<h4 id="reader-content-like-things-and-people">Like things and people</h4>
<p>In Czech, there are two ways of expressing your likes. You can either like the way something looks <em>líbit se</em> or like it for its properties <em>mít rád</em>. The difference is also grammatical. With <em>mít rád</em> the person who likes is the subject and the liked person or thing is the object (<em>Petr má rád Martinu</em> - Petr likes Martina). While with <em>líbit se</em> the liked person or thing is the subject and the person who likes is an indirect object (<em>Komu? Čemu?</em> [dative] case-see below). E.g. <em>Líbí se mi Praha -</em> I like Praha [<em>Praha is pleasing to me</em>-I like the way it looks].</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-257">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>mít rád<br>
+ Koho? Co?</strong></td>
<td><strong>líbit se komu<br>
+ Kdo? Co?</strong></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">mám rád Janu</td>
<td>líbí se mi Jana</td>
<td>I love (like) Jana vs. I like the way Jana looks</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">mám rád rohlíky</td>
<td>líbí se mi rohlíky</td>
<td>because they taste good <em>vs</em>. because they look funny</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">mám rád knihy</td>
<td>líbí se mi knihy</td>
<td>because they are interesting <em>vs.</em> because they look nice</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><em>mít rád</em> is more general, and is never used with specific objects</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-258">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">mám rád ten rohlík</td>
<td align="left">líbí se mi ten rohlík</td>
<td align="left">I'm in love with this <em>rohlík</em> vs. I like the way it looks</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-like-food-chutnat">Like food (chutnat)</h4>
<p>If you want to say you like the food in front of you, you have to use <em>chutnat</em>; it has the same structure as <em>líbit se</em></p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-259">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">ten rohlík mi chutná</td>
<td>ten rohlík se mi líbí</td>
<td>I like this roll (tastes good) vs. I like it (it looks good)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-like-to-do">Like to do</h4>
<p>Here, you only use <em>rád</em> and the form of the verb.</p>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> Rád has to reflect the gender and number of the subject: <strong>rád</strong> (man), <strong>ráda</strong> (woman), <strong>rádo</strong> (child), <strong>rádi</strong> (plural)<strong>, rády</strong> (plural women or children)</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-260">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>rád</strong> + verb (NOT infinitive)</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">rád čtu</td>
<td>I like to read</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">rád hraju tenis</td>
<td>I like to play tennis</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">rád dělám úkoly</td>
<td>I like to do homework</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="reader-content-negating-rád">Negating <em>rád</em></h4>
<p>The word rád is negated differently if it is used about activities vs when it is used about people or objects. With <em>mám rád, ne</em> goes on the the verb <em>mít</em>. With rád + Verb, <em>ne</em> goes on <em>rád</em>.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-261">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">mám rád knihy</td>
<td align="left">nemám rád knihy</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">rád čtu</td>
<td align="left">nerad čtu</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-prosím-in-czech-the-multipurpose-conversation-glue"><em>Prosím</em> in Czech – the multipurpose conversation glue</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-262">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Zavři okno, <em>prosím.</em></td>
<td align="left">Close the window, <strong>please.</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Kávu, <em>prosím</em>. / <em>Prosím</em> jednu kávu</td>
<td align="left">Coffee, <strong>please</strong>. / One coffee <strong>please</strong>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">(Děkuju.) > <em>Prosím.</em></td>
<td align="left">(Thank you.) <strong>></strong> <strong>You're welcome.</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><em>Prosím.</em> (Tady je ta kniha.)</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Here you are.</strong> (Here's the book.)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Prosím Tě.</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Please.</strong> (Informal)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Prosím Vás.</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Please.</strong> (Formal)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><em>Prosím</em> is either used as a particle or a verb. If used as a verb it is followed by a noun in the Koho? Co? case, e.g. <em>Kávu prosím</em>. It is the <em>I</em> form of the verb <em>prosit</em> (to ask, to beg, to plead).</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-prý-prej---i-hear-they-say">prý, prej - I hear, they say</h3>
<p>There is this little word <em>prý</em> (with its colloquial partner <em>prej</em>) which is used to report on things that you have heard from an unspecified source and may not be sure whether they’re true or not. It may be translated as <em>I hear</em> or <em>They say</em> into English. For example:</p>
<p><strong>Prý</strong> bude letos drahý benzín. (I hear that gas will be expensive this year.)</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-how-do-you-address-people">How do you address people?</h3>
<p>This is an issue of politeness. It is very important to know how to address people.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Rule 1: The ending of a name is changed when addressing the person (Michal - Michale).</p></li>
<li><p>Rule 2: In polite conversations, it is important to use the title if the person has one. Very much like in German. The name is not used after the title when addressing.</p></li>
</ul>
<h3 id="reader-content-going-to-see-someone">Going to see someone</h3>
<p>Czech has two prepositions (similar to the French <em>chez</em>) that are usually translated into English as <em>going to see someone</em> or <em>being at someone’s place.</em></p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-263">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>u (being at someone’s)</strong></p></td>
<td><p><strong>k (going to see someone)</strong></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>Dnes večer budeme u Katky.</p>
<p>(Tonight, we’ll be at Katka’s.)</p>
<p>Kde jsi? U zubaře.</p>
<p>(Where are you? At the dentists.)</p></td>
<td><p>Zítra půjdeme k babičce.</p>
<p>(Tomorrow, we’ll go to see granny.)</p>
<p>Musím jít k doktorovi.</p>
<p>(I must go see a doctor.)</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-small-talk">Small talk</h3>
<p>There is no expression in Czech to describe the English ‘small talk’. There is a word for ‘chitchat’ (pokec) or you can say ‘Přijďte si popovídat’ (Come over for a chat). But there is no word for ‘small talk’. Sentences like ‘they engaged in meaningless small talk’ or ‘she is no good at small talk’ are almost impossible translate into Czech.</p>
<p>Czechs have many fewer tools to engaged in small talk. There is no equivalent of the ‘how are you’ although that may be changing under the influence of American TV. Some phrases you can use on meeting people casually without necessarily wanting to know how they are:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Jak se vede? (How is it going)</p></li>
<li><p>(Tak) jak to jde? (How is it going)</p></li>
<li><p>Jak se daří? (How is it going?)</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The response to these is something like:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Ale jde to. (Not bad)</p></li>
<li><p>No jo, to víte. Nestěžuju si. (You know. I don’t complaint.)</p></li>
</ul>
<p>However, these phrases are not quite as formalized as they are in English.</p>
<p>Asking about other people is also common:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>A co máma? (How about your mom?)</p></li>
<li><p>Jak se daří mámě?</p></li>
<li><p>Jak se vede bratrovi? (How is it going with your brother)</p></li>
</ul>
<p>A common noncommital answer is:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Ale jo, jde to. (Not bad.)</p></li>
<li><p>No to víte, jak by to šlo. (You know, how could it be)</p></li>
</ul>
<p>However, these are all rather informal and quite loose. A conversation can be built up from these blocks for a while.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-wishing-good-luck-trip-nice-time-etc.">Wishing good luck, trip, nice time, etc.</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-264">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Have a nice weekend.</td>
<td align="left">Hezký víkend.</td>
<td>Have a nice trip.</td>
<td align="left">Šťastnou cestu.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Have a good time.</td>
<td align="left">Dobře se bavte.</td>
<td>Good luck.</td>
<td align="left">Hodně štěstí.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Enjoy yourself.</td>
<td align="left">Užijte si to.</td>
<td>Fingers crossed.</td>
<td align="left">Držím ti palce.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-what-are-the-acronyms-of-academic-titles-used-with-peoples-names">What are the acronyms of academic titles used with people’s names?</h3>
<p>Czechs use their academic titles with their names even outside strictly professional contexts. Czech uses a variety of acronyms that can precede or be appended to a person’s name. These are the most common titles ranked more or less by importance.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-265">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Mgr.</strong></td>
<td align="left">magistr – MA</td>
<td align="left"><strong>RNDr.</strong></td>
<td align="left">doktor – sciences specialization</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>Ing.</strong></td>
<td align="left">inženýr – MSc</td>
<td align="left"><strong>CSc.</strong></td>
<td align="left">kandidát věd –after name, not awarded any more</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>PhDr.</strong></td>
<td align="left">doktor – PhD, general</td>
<td align="left"><strong>DrSc.</strong></td>
<td align="left">doktor věd –after name, not awarded any more</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>JUDr.</strong></td>
<td align="left">doktor – legal specialization</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Doc.</strong></td>
<td align="left">docent – associate professor independent of a school</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>MUDr.</strong></td>
<td align="left">doktor – medical doctor</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Prof.</strong></td>
<td align="left">profesor - professor independent of a school</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-text-and-conversation-structure">Text and conversation structure</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-a-vs.-i-and-vs.-as-well">a vs. i / and vs. as well</h3>
<p>There is a significant differrence between <em>i</em> and <em>a</em>. 'i' means basically 'as well as' while 'a' means 'and'. Sometimes their meanings overlap slightly but there's always a subtle change in perspective.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-266">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>a</strong></td>
<td align="left"><strong>i</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Byl tam Petr a Pavel</td>
<td align="left">Byl tam Petr i Pavel</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Petr and Pavel were there.</td>
<td align="left">Petr and Pavel were both there.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>When <em>i</em> occurs before a noun, it means <em>even</em> or <em>as well</em>: 'I Petr tam byl' - 'Even Petr showed up'/'Petr was there, as well'</p>
<p>You can even have <em>a</em> and <em>i</em> as part of the same clause in spoken language: 'A i Petr tam byl' - 'And Petr was there, too.'</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-conditional-with-the-infinitive">Conditional with the infinitive</h3>
<p>Sometimes in colloquial speech, the first part of a conditional sentence can be expressed through the infinitive.</p>
<p><strong>Vědět to</strong>, nešel bych tam. If I had known it, I wouldn’t have gone there.</p>
<p><strong>Být vámi</strong>, nedělal bych to. If I were you, I wouldn’t do it.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-telling-stories-about-sequences-of-events">Telling stories about sequences of events</h3>
<p>To tell a story, a lot of connecting words are needed:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-267">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>teď</strong></td>
<td align="left">Teď sedím doma a čtu si.</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Now</strong> I’m sitting at home and I’m reading.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>předtím</strong></td>
<td align="left">Předtím jsem se díval na televizi.</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Before that</strong> I was watching TV.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>potom</strong></td>
<td align="left">Potom si uvařím večeři.</td>
<td align="left"><strong>After that</strong> I will cook dinner.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>později</strong></td>
<td align="left">Později se půjdu na procházku.</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Later</strong>, I will go for a walk.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>nakonec</strong></td>
<td align="left">Nakonec půjdu spát.</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Finally</strong> (at the end), I will go to bed.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-strong-and-taboo-language">Strong and taboo language</h2>
<h3 id="reader-content-animal-insults">Animal insults</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-268">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">prase</td>

</tr>

<h3 id="reader-content-how-do-you-swear-in-czech">How do you swear in Czech?</h3>
<p>The short answer is YOU don’t. In English, the acceptability of the most common swear words has increased greatly in the last few decades. While still considered inappropriate for children and mainstream broadcasting, they are more and more acceptable in many contexts. There are no such swearwords in Czech.</p>
<p>The most common Czech expletives have little to do with sex. While there are many sex-related taboo words, the common expletives have more to do with excreta. The following list gives the most common expletives and related euphemisms. Learners are strongly advised not to use them in any context.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-269">





<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>Expletive</p></th>
<th><p>Euphemisms</p></th>
<th><p>English explanation</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>do prdele</p></td>
<td><p>do Prčic</p>
<p>do prkýnka</p></td>
<td><p>Expression of frustration. Literally: “to ass”</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>kurva</p></td>
<td><p>kurnik</p>
<p>kurňa</p></td>
<td><p>Stronger expression of frustration. Probably the closest to exclaiming Fuuck! in furstration. Literally: “whore”. When used as an insult, can be directed at men.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>jdi do prdele</p></td>
<td><p>jdi do Prčic</p>
<p>jdi se bodnout</p></td>
<td><p>Expression of frustration directed at another person. Literally: “go to ass”</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>je to na hovno</p>
<p>stojí to za hovno</p></td>
<td><p>je to na houby</p></td>
<td><p>Dismissive expletive indicating low value of an object or idea. Literally: “shit”. <strong>Note:</strong> this is never used as an expresion of frustration.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>já se na to vyseru</p></td>
<td><p>já se na to vykašlu</p></td>
<td><p>Expressing dissatisfaction with something or giving up on something. Roughly equivalent to <em>fuck it</em>, <em>I give up</em> but can also be used to describe someone not finishing something. Literally <em>shit on something</em> or in the euphemism <em>cough on something</em>.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>posraný</p></td>
<td><p>podělaný</p></td>
<td><p>Adjective describing something negatively. Roughly equivalent to <em>fucking</em> or <em>fucked up</em>. Lit. <em>shat on</em>.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-dude-ty-vole">Dude! – ty vole</h3>
<p>Even though <em>vůl</em> (ox) is an insult, in its Vocative form, <em>vole</em>, it can also be used to mean something equivalent to <em>dude</em>. You can often hear people sprinkle their speech (when addressing a very good friend) with <em>ty vole</em>. This is not an insult but is also completely inappropriate for any context outside extremely informal interactions with friends. For example, it would not be used within the family context.</p>
<p>Some people use <em>vole</em> or <em>ty vole</em> as a filler in their informal speech similarly to the way people overuse <em>you know</em> or <em>you see</em> in English.</p>
<h3 id="reader-content-religious-expletives-and-euphemisms">Religious expletives and euphemisms</h3>
<p>Expletives based on religion are no longer perceived as problematic. But there are still many old euphemisms that are used even if nobody perceives the full religious expletives to be blasphemous. They all have roughly the same meaning of expressing frustration and dismay.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-270">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Ježíši Kriste</td>
<td align="left">Jesus</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Ježišmarjá</td>
<td align="left">Jesus Mary</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">pane Bože</td>
<td align="left">Lord</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">panenko Marie</td>
<td align="left">virgin Mary</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">hergot, herdek, hernajz</td>
<td align="left">from German Herr Gott</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">kruci, krucinál, kruci písek</td>
<td align="left">from Latin for cross</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">sakra, sakryš, sakra práce, sakramente</td>
<td align="left">from sacral</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">pro pána Jána, pro pána Krále</td>
<td align="left">for Lord John’s/King’s sake</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">mankote, šmankote</td>
<td align="left">from German mein Gott</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="reader-content-sex-and-genitalia-taboo-words-and-insults">Sex and genitalia taboo words and insults</h3>
<p>Strong language related to sex or human genitalia is entirely inappropriate for learners. There are no neutral terms that would be acceptable in conversation and we strongly advise against attempting to use them even in jest if you look them up in a dictionary.</p>



---

> Section 18 of 22 · Differences between standard and non-standard Czech · id `differences-between-standard-and-non-standard-czech` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=differences-between-standard-and-non-standard-czech)

<h1 id="reader-content-differences-between-standard-and-non-standard-czech">Differences between standard and non-standard Czech</h1>
<p>As is usual in most languages, the Czech colloquial language has different pronunciation, and specific words and expressions. Unlike most languages, in Czech, the colloquial language also uses consistently different endings. For instance, you learn <em>mladý</em> (young) and hear <em>mladej</em> on the street, or you see <em>Šumavský chléb</em> (Šumava bread) written on a shelf in the bakery but people ask for <em>Šumavskej chleba</em>. As you see the difference is mostly in vowels.</p>
<p>It is also noteworthy that the Moravian 'dialect' uses many of the endings of the written standard, although not consistently, leading many Moravians to claim that they speak “better Czech” than Bohemians.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-which-czech-is-taught-to-foreigners">Which ‘Czech’ is taught to foreigners?</h2>
<p>As a learner you don't have to worry too much about it, but it is important to keep in mind that you may hear some endings pronounced differently from how you learned them.</p>
<p>Choosing the right form can be a hard decision for a teacher to make. The standard variant is more regular but also more complex. If you started speaking colloquial Czech with beginners' mistakes it would sound ridiculous. The solution usually is, to use a simplified standard Czech and make it more communicative by introducing elements from the colloquial as appropriate.</p>
<p>The trick is that the two 'languages' are not always mutually exclusive. For two words in one sentence uttered by a Czech in normal everyday communication, you may find one ending colloquial and one literary, and there are no rules for this. Therefore, teachers cannot simply resolve to teach one or the other because except for in writing they are rarely separated. On top of that, Czechs themselves sometimes quarrel which is which.</p>
<p>The hard thing is to tell when to use which ending. The terms colloquial and literary or spoken and written are not very accurate. The colloquial variant is never used in writing (except, paradoxically enough, in literature), or for official purposes on TV, radio announcements, speeches, advertisements etc. You would use it with your friends but it is also used in less formal public situations like lectures, speeches to small audiences, etc.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-some-differences-between-standard-and-colloquial-czech-spisovná-a-nespisovná-čeština">Some differences between “standard” and “colloquial” Czech <em>spisovná a nespisovná čeština</em></h2>
<p>This is a very important feature of the Czech language. In reality, there are two language codes that are used for different occasions.</p>
<p>'Literary' and 'colloquial' are not the best terms to distinguish between the two varieties of present-day Czech, but they are the most commonly used ones. Better terms might be Standard Czech and Common Czech (used by H. Kučera in his The Phonology of Czech of 1961). <strong>Standard</strong> Czech is taught in schools, used in mass media, and most written documents, both official and unofficial. <strong><em>Common</em></strong> Czech is almost exclusively spoken. It is used in all personal communication, and also for semi-official purposes, including public speeches.</p>
<p>Although there are many differences in vocabulary, pronunciation, and syntax, the most pronounced disparity is in morphology. For some grammatical models there are two very different sets of endings. A short example is given below.</p>
<p>The main problem for a foreigner learning the two different codes is that the difference is often a matter of scale. Also, often in the spoken language standard Czech forms are mixed with common Czech ones.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-271">









<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="4">Morphology</td>
<td colspan="3">Vocabulary</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Trend</td>
<td>Standard</td>
<td>Common</td>
<td>English</td>
<td>Standard</td>
<td>Common</td>
<td>English</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>-ý/í > -ej</strong></td>
<td>mladý</td>
<td>mladej</td>
<td>young</td>
<td>být</td>
<td>bejt</td>
<td>to be</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>-ý/í > -ej</strong></td>
<td>nosí</td>
<td>nosej</td>
<td>they wear</td>
<td>chléb</td>
<td>chleba</td>
<td>bread</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>-é > -ý</strong></td>
<td>mladého</td>
<td>mladýho</td>
<td>of young</td>
<td>mléko</td>
<td>mlíko</td>
<td>milk</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>-ý(í)m > -y(i)m</strong></td>
<td>vidím</td>
<td>vidim</td>
<td>I see</td>
<td>méně</td>
<td>míň</td>
<td>less</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>o- > vo-</strong></td>
<td>okno</td>
<td>vokno</td>
<td>window</td>
<td>mnoho</td>
<td>moc</td>
<td>many / much</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>-ami > -ama</strong></td>
<td>s ženami</td>
<td>s ženama</td>
<td>with women</td>
<td>bysme</td>
<td>bychom</td>
<td>we would</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The difference originated at the time of the National Revival, when the, then 200 years old, language of Bible Kralická was taken as the basis for standard Czech by the revivalists like Dobrovský. But in fact, even in the time of Blahoslav, many of these morphological forms had been out of common use. Czech was also developing parallel to this, mostly among the common people, and mostly out of the cities. This language was used for writing only by Catholic priests who were striving to be understood by their audience.</p>

<h3 id="reader-content-sometimes-e-is-pronounced-as-í">Sometimes <em>e</em> is pronounced as <em>í</em></h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-276">

<h2 id="reader-content-what-you-hear-and-what-you-read">What you hear and what you read</h2>
<p>What you hear people in the streets and shops say and what you read on signs around you can be quite different. Most textbooks teach you language on the more formal end of the scale (and for good reason because speaking highly colloquially with a thick accent and while making rudimentary errors would be ridiculous) but at the same time, they will tend to include words that do not sound out of place in the spoken language. Here are some very common examples of functiona words:</p>

<p>Sometimes there is a similar difference in English e.g. the difference between <em>boty/obuv</em> is similar to that of <em>shoes/footwear</em> (i.e. footwear is more general) but you are still more likely to see <em>obuv</em> in shop windows than <em>boty</em>.</p>
