> Section 19 of 22 · Czech Trivia · id `czech-trivia` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=czech-trivia)

<h1 id="reader-content-czech-trivia">Czech Trivia</h1>
<h2 id="reader-content-what-is-the-relationship-of-czech-to-english-or-other-languages">What is the relationship of Czech to English or other languages?</h2>
<p>Czech (along with English, German, French, Spanish, Greek, as well as Persian or Hindi, and dozens of other languages) belongs to the Indo-European family of languages. Languages from this family split out in about 4,000 BC, so by now they only have a few words in common (<em>bratr</em> - brother - frére, <em>matka</em> - mother - mére, and such). The only languages in Europe that don't belong to this family are Basque (which is on its own), and Hungarian, Estonian and Finnish (Finno-Ugric languages).</p>
<p>This large family has many branches, for instance, <em>Germanic</em> (English, German) or <em>Romance</em> (French, Spanish, Italian, Romanian). Czech belongs to the <em>Slavic</em> branch (together with Slovak, Polish, Russian, Bulgarian, Serbochrovatian, Bielorussian, Slovenian, and Ukrainian). Along with Polish and Slovak, Czech is a West-Slavic language.</p>
<p>This chart provides a summary of the relationship of Czech to other languages. Circles indicate genealogical closeness of a particular language to Czech. Typefaces differentiate between different types of languages. The typology here is very simplified.</p>
<p><img src="media/image93-wmf.png"></p>
<p>The typology of languages as presented here is mostly based on morphology with some implications for syntax. Also, no language is strictly limited to one type, usually, the type indicates a general tendency. The four basic types are:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-281">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Inflecting</td>
<td align="left">Agglutinating</td>
<td align="left">Isolating</td>
<td align="left">Polysynthetic</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Grammatical relationships expressed by:</td>
<td align="left">endings</td>
<td align="left">endings</td>
<td align="left">word order</td>
<td align="left">combinations of lexical items</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Endings express:</td>
<td align="left">combination of many functions</td>
<td align="left">each ending a single function</td>
<td align="left">N/A</td>
<td align="left">N/A</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-what-is-the-history-of-czech">What is the history of Czech?</h2>
<p>The history of Czech, as it is known now, starts somewhere in the 15th and 16th century when the first Czech grammar and a new translation of the Bible were published. The beginnings of Czech spelling lie in the work of Master Jan Hus in the early 15th century. Of course, the history of Czech goes much further back in the past to the 9th century when the Slavic dialects that later gave birth to Czech were spoken in the area. The first written records come from the turn of the 13th and 14th centuries. The first translation of the Bible into Czech dates somewhere to the 1380s thus being the third in Europe after Italian and French. The use of Czech as a literary and official language receded slowly with the Thirty-Year War (in the 17th century). Czech was then replaced by German as the language used for official purposes in the Czech lands as the Hapsburgs took over. In the following centuries, Czech was mostly spoken by lower classes (especially in the country) although some Baroque literature was also written in Czech. However, compared to German and Latin, there are relatively few literary works in Czech from the Baroque period. That is why by the end of the 18th century Czech was believed to be a dying language by many scholars of Czech origin.</p>
<p>The turn of the century, however, witnessed the birth of a cultural and political movement referred to as the National Revival or Awakening (Národní Obrození). A new Czech grammar book and a large dictionary were published. Speaking Czech became fashionable, and many works of literature were published in the language including some high profile faked historical texts. As the century progressed, Czech found its place along with German as the official language, and was beginning to be taught in schools of higher education again. Since 1918 (founding of Czechoslovakia), Czech has been the sole official language in the Czech Republic. You can see some of the most important dates of the development of the language in the chart below.</p>
<hr>
<hr>
<h3 id="reader-content-some-important-dates-in-the-history-of-czech-language">Some important dates in the history of Czech language</h3>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-282">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>863</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Christian Mission of Cyril and Methodius to Great Moravia - liturgy in Old Church Slavonic - New Testament translated. Local Slavic tribes spoke a variety of dialects which were similar to Old Church Slavonic.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1000s</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Beginnings of Czech State. No written Czech documents. Beginnings of western influence.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1100s</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Latin replaced Old Church Slavonic as liturgical, administrative, and literary language.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1200s</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">First written Czech materials, at first, mostly annotations of Latin works which, towards the end of the century, developed into more extensive works such as legends and chronicles.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1313</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left"><em>Dalimilova Kronika</em> - the first big chronicle written in Czech, based, to a large extent on earlier Latin works of similar content.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1348</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Founding of Prague University (today's Charles University) began a period of Czech translations of official and other documents culminating with a Czech translation of the Bible from Latin Vulgata. First Latin/Czech dictionaries/word lists were also compiled at that time.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1412</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Master Jan Hus' <em>De Ortographia Bohemica</em> - an influential work codifying Czech writing system which became the basis of the writing system used today.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1499</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Viktorín Kornel ze Všehrd wrote first work of scientific rigor in Czech concerned with the contemporary legal system. This work inspired translations of classical works from Greek and Latin.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1533</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">First Czech grammar by Beneš Optát, Petr Gzel and Václav Filomates.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1571</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Czech Grammar by Jan Blahoslav started work which ultimately led to a new translation of the Bible.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1593</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left"><em>Bible Kralická</em> - new translation of the Bible directly from the Greek and Hebrew original. Later during the national revival, it became one of the sources for codification of Czech literary language.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1627</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">After the lost battle of White Mountain (1620) German was pronounced equal to Czech as the official language of Czech state administration and courts, which, in effect, caused the actual disappearance of Czech from official documents by the end of the century. This was due, mostly, to lack of Czech officials and scribes at the courts of prevailing foreign nobility. Ultimately, this led to the ousting of Czech as the official language from schools and state administration offices.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1600s<br>
-1700s</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Baroque period in the Czech lands - formative period for today's Czech colloquial language. Although this period is usually connected with the degeneration of the Czech language, it witnessed the creation of such works as Czech grammars written in Latin (1672), and the Czech printing manual (1668), and Defenses of the Czech Language which were the overture to the Czech national revival.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1790s -1860s</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Czech National Revival - a cultural and political movement which set out to revive Czech language and culture. Among the names that are usually associated with the movement at its different stages are: J. K. Tyl, J. Dobrovský, Lad. Čelakovský, J. Jungman, Fr. Palacký, and many others. This period saw founding of the first Czech newspaper, translations of famous literary works as well as active development of original Czech literature, and introduction of Czech to schools (1850).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1809</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Dobrovský's Czech grammar <em>Ausführliches Lehrgebäude der böhmischen Sprache</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1835</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">First volume of Czech dictionary (with German equivalents) by Josef Jungman was published (the last volume came out in 1839). A major lexicographical work, unsurpassed for over a century, which had a great formative influence on the Czech language. Jungman used vocabulary of the Kralická Bible period and of the language used by his contemporaries. He borrowed words not present in Czech from other Slavic languages (e.g., Polish, Russian, Serbian). He also inspired development of Czech scientific terminology, thus, making it possible for original Czech research to develop.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1836</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Publication of <em>Máj</em> by Karel Hynek Mácha. This poem not only marked the beginning of Romanticism in Czech literature, but was also the first original poetic work of such magnitude in the Czech language. To this date, it is considered one of the gems of Czech literature.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1918</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Founding of Czechoslovakia with Czech and Slovak as the official languages. Especially, the 1930s were a time of very intensive literary and publishing efforts.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1948</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Beginning of state intervention into language politics. Only one, state-approved, curriculum, was taught at schools. Although Russian was compulsory at all levels of the education system, there is very little Russian influence on Czech from this period.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>1989</strong></td>
<td></td>
<td></td>
<td align="left">Decentralization of language education. Beginning of English influence on Czech with the influx of new technology and business practices. Later in the 1990s, new reference works are published. In 1994, the project of Czech National Corpus is launched (<a href="http://ucnk.ff.cuni.cz/">http://ucnk.ff.cuni.cz</a>).</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-what-type-of-language-is-czech">What type of language is Czech?</h2>
<p>Inflecting, would be a linguist's answer. This linguistic term means that Czech words have different forms depending on their function. The one consequence of this for learning Czech is that if you learn a new word, you are not necessarily able to use it 'correctly' (i.e. in its right form) in all possible contexts. You also have to learn what its other forms are in order to you use it so that it says what you want it to say.</p>
<p>Conversely, working with the dictionary you may not be able to look up words as you see or hear them. You have to know how they changed to their present form. So, for instance, <em>oči</em> (eyes) you won't find in the dictionary under <em>oč</em> (as you would English eyes) but as <em>oko</em> which is the basic dictionary form.</p>
<p>In English (an analytic language but none the easier), words are either put in the right order (<em>John loves Mary</em> vs. <em>Mary loves John</em> kind of thing), or helping words such as <em>have, do, of,</em> etc<em>.</em> are used. Hence, instead of different forms and endings, you have to learn all the subtleties of word order to be able to use new words so that they mean what you want them to mean. It is generally easy to look up words in the dictionary as even irregular verbs (<em>go-went-gone</em>) are listed.</p>
<p>As an example of how different forms of a word change the meaning of a sentence, let us compare a few sentences in Czech and in English. For instance, in the two following English sentences</p>
<p>A. A patient teacher teaches a clever pupil.</p>
<p>B. A clever pupil teaches a patient teacher.</p>
<p>to get the pupil to teach the teacher, all you have to do is swap their positions in the sentence. The words remain the same, their form doesn't change.</p>
<p>In Czech, it does not matter who goes first. What is important, though, is the form of the two parties. So in Czech A. and B. would mean the same thing if you didn't change the form of the words, as below.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-283">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">A1.</td>
<td align="left">Trpěliv<strong>ý</strong> učitel učí chytr<strong>ého</strong> žák<strong>a</strong>.</td>
<td>A patient teacher teaches a clever pupil.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">A2.</td>
<td align="left">Chytr<strong>ého</strong> žák<strong>a</strong> učí trpěliv<strong>ý</strong> učitel.</td>
<td>A patient teacher teaches a clever pupil.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>They are not the same if you do change their forms, and don't change the word order.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-284">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">A1.</td>
<td align="left">Trpěliv<strong>ý</strong> učitel učí chytr<strong>ého</strong> žák<strong>a</strong>.</td>
<td>A patient teacher teaches a clever pupil.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">B.</td>
<td align="left">Trpěliv<strong>ého</strong> učitel<strong>e</strong> učí chytr<strong>ý</strong> žák</td>
<td>A clever pupil teaches a patient teacher.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>In principle, it is pretty easy to learn how to go back and forth between different forms and their meanings, although, just as for a learner of English it takes time to learn to always use the right word order, it will take time for you to learn to always use the right form, but by then you will be able to communicate freely, and have time to focus on correctness.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-which-words-have-different-forms-and-when">Which words have different forms and when?</h2>
<p>Most words in Czech do most of the time. All <strong>nouns, adjectives, pronouns</strong>, and <strong>numerals</strong> change their forms according to <em>case</em>, <em>gender</em>, and <em>number</em>. <strong>Verbs</strong> change their forms according to person and tense (like in English). So, for instance, in <em>mluvím</em> (I speak) <em>-ím</em> has the same role as I.</p>
<p>The words that <strong>don't</strong> change their forms are <strong>adverbs, prepositions,</strong> and <strong>conjunctions</strong>.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-are-there-polite-forms-of-verbs-like-in-french-or-german">Are there polite forms of verbs like in French or German?</h2>
<p>Yes, there are. They are used similarly to French. We also use the second person plural<a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/complete-table-of-contents.md#reader-content-fn2" class="footnote-ref" id="reader-content-fnref2"><sup>2</sup></a> when speaking with someone we respect or don't know very well. <em>Qu'est que tu as?</em> vs <em>Qu'est que Vous avais?</em> (in French) = <em>Co máš?</em> vs <em>Co máte?</em> (in Czech).</p>
<p>You can always tell the informal form by the ending <em>-š</em> and the formal by <em>-te</em>. The pronouns used for pointing at people are <strong><em>Ty</em></strong> (you – informal) and <strong><em>Vy</em></strong> (you – formal).</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-what-do-the-days-of-the-week-mean">What do the days of the week mean?</h2>
<p>Unlike in English, where the names of the days of the week are mostly derived from the names of Norse gods, the Czech names have mostly easily identifiable meaning which can be useful a useful mnemonic device for the student.</p>
<p><strong>Note</strong> that the Czech week begins with Monday. It is important to keep in mind when comparing calendars.</p>
<p><strong>Note</strong> that just like names of the months, names of the day always begin with a small letter.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-285">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>pondělí</strong></td>
<td align="left">The day after Sunday (do nothing day), i.e. <em>po neděli</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>úterý</strong></td>
<td align="left">The only obscure one: from the old Slavic <em>vtoroj</em> (second). Compare with Russian for Tuesday <em>vtornik</em>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>středa</strong></td>
<td align="left">The middle day, from <em>střed</em> (middle).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>čtvrtek</strong></td>
<td align="left">The fourth day, from <em>čtvrtý</em> (the fourth)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>pátek</strong></td>
<td align="left">The fifth day, from <em>pátý</em> (the fifth)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>sobota</strong></td>
<td align="left">Quite obviously derived from the word Sabbath.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>neděle</strong></td>
<td align="left">Do-nothing day, from <em>nedělat</em> (do nothing)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-what-do-the-names-of-months-mean">What do the names of months mean?</h2>
<p>Now, they mean very little and, for some of them, most Czechs would not know the origin, or would mistake it for something else. Others, however, are very clear.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-286">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>leden</strong></td>
<td align="left">Month of <em>led</em> (ice). Derives clearly from the Czech word <em>led</em> (ice) since it describes a period when everything is covered with ice.</td>
<td align="left">January</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>únor</strong></td>
<td align="left">Month of ice breaking and sinking in the water. It comes from a word <em>nořit</em> (to submerge, sink). For most Czechs, this is rather opaque.</td>
<td align="left">February</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>březen</strong></td>
<td align="left">Month of birch trees (<em>bříza</em>)</td>
<td align="left">March</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>duben</strong></td>
<td align="left">Month of budding oaks (<em>dub</em>)</td>
<td align="left">April</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>květen</strong></td>
<td align="left">Month of flowers (<em>květy</em>)</td>
<td align="left">May</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>červen</strong></td>
<td align="left">Month of worms (<em>červy</em>). Worms were used for making red (č<em>ervený</em>) dye.</td>
<td align="left">June</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>červenec</strong></td>
<td align="left">The same etymology.</td>
<td align="left">July</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>srpen</strong></td>
<td align="left">Month of harvest with sickle (<em>srp</em>)</td>
<td align="left">August</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>září</strong></td>
<td align="left">Month of rutting (<em>za říje</em> = at the time of rutting)</td>
<td align="left">September</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>říjen</strong></td>
<td align="left">Month of rutting (<em>říje</em> = rut)</td>
<td align="left">October</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>listopad</strong></td>
<td align="left">Month of falling leaves (<em>list</em> = leaf, <em>pad</em>at = to fall)</td>
<td align="left">November</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><strong>prosinec</strong></td>
<td align="left">Month of gray color (from Old Czech – has no present-day equivalent)</td>
<td align="left">December</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<p><strong>Note</strong> that the names of the months never begin with a capital letter.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-spelling-out">Spelling out</h2>
<p>Because Czech spelling works by and large on the what you see is what you say principle, Czechs almost always know how a name or a word is spelled. Therefore, if you want to spell out your name to someone, pronounce it as if it were written in Czech. For example, <em>Joan</em> would be yo-ah-n and <em>John</em> something like yo-eh-n both pronounced as single words with three syllables.</p>
<p>Because they have no training in it (and no need for it), Czechs often have trouble visualizing a word quickly based simply on hearing a sequence of letters. That’s why using the English approach of spelling by naming all the letters, even using the Czech names, can cause problems. Of course, sometimes it is necessary to specify the letter by name but then only that one letter is singled out after the whole word was pronounced.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-how-many-endings-does-czech-have">How many endings does Czech have?</h2>
<p>Theoretically, for nouns there could be 196 different endings (14 different functions - 6 + 1 cases in the singular and 6 +1 in the plural - times 14 different models). In fact, there are only 24 different endings: <em>-a, -e/ě, -i, -o, -u, -y; -ou; -é, -í, -ů; -ech, -ách, -ích; -em, -ám, -ím, -ům; -mi; -ami, -emi, -ími; -ovi, -ové</em>, and <em>0 -</em> nothing is also considered an ending. But you will see that they have much in common, for instance, <em>m, ch, v</em> are always used for similar functions.</p>
<p>There are some more endings for adjectives but no more than ten. Overall, there are fewer than 40 endings for all nouns, adjectives, pronouns and numerals which is not much at all.</p>
<p>There are 4 basic verbal models which gives us 32 options (with sub-models 78) with about 18 ending forms that are actually distinct from one another.</p>
<p>Then, there are about a dozen endings used for other purposes, and that's about it. Apparently, Czech, the language of endings, makes do with about 60 different endings altogether. If we consider that there are several hundred irregular words (both verbs and nouns) in English, this is not bad at all. This is not to say that endings is all that there is to learning Czech, just as irregular verbs are not all that there is to learning English.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-are-there-many-dialects-of-czech">Are there many dialects of Czech?</h2>
<p>The question of what is a dialect and what is an accent is a very complicated one. As for many modern languages, differences between dialects are growing less and less pronounced. For Czech, historically prominent dialects are confined to small regions, and are slowly dying out, mostly because of the influence of the media. Traces of historical dialects can be found in the standard language of different regions, but very few people speak something that could be identified as a pure dialect.</p>
<p>In the popular perception, there are two widely recognized dialects: Moravian (which is in fact three quite distinct dialects with quite a few different words and expressions), and Bohemian. Most people can identify the difference primarily by accent and some prominent vocabulary differences.</p>
<p>Among accents that are readily recognized is the fairly strong Prague accent and the northern Morvian accent with influences from Polish. The Czech Roma minority also speaks Czech with a recognizable accent.</p>
<p>The difference between the written standard and colloquial language is often more pronounced than that between different dialects. Some of the Moravian dialects also use some ending that are notably identical to the written standard Czech.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-is-there-anything-about-czech-that-is-easy">Is there anything about Czech that is easy?</h2>
<p>Yes, a number of things. The tenses, for instance. There is <strong>no sequence of tenses</strong> (a common threat to a learner of English of which you even may not be aware). Also, Czech does <strong>not have articles</strong>. It is very <strong>easy to learn Czech spelling</strong>, too. In some respects Czech is <strong>more regular</strong> than English. It has very few irregular verbs. On the other hand it does abound with exceptions in nouns. The <strong>stress is always on the first syllable</strong> but there are short and long vowels that are independent on it.</p>
<p>All in all, it is almost impossible to say what is easy and what is hard. There are certainly many things in any language which are cursed by its learners but still, countless brave men and women are undertaking the dangerous venture, lured by the promise of acquiring the key which will fling the gates to another culture wide open. Such a prize always makes this adventurous enterprise well worth all the effort.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-common-learner-errors">Common Learner Errors</h2>
<p>The most visible errors are those of <strong>morphology</strong>. Czech <strong>cases</strong> and conjugation of verbs are often the source of mistakes made even by more advanced learners. Inflectional rules, however, are easy to memorize because for the purpose of learning the language they are largely arbitrary. (Only, palatalization of roots with some endings makes this a little more difficult.) Also, native speakers take these errors for what they are, and don't consider them disruptive to communication.</p>
<p>On the other hand, learners of non-Slavic languages have grave problems with the Czech <strong>verbal aspect</strong>. Aspect, like articles and tenses in English, is hard to describe in terms of 'if-then' rules. To learn it properly, one needs to acquire a 'Czech perspective' on time and activity. Moreover, erroneous use of aspect is often detrimental to communication, as native speakers don't recognize them as errors but rather substitute other meanings.</p>
<p><strong>Pronunciation</strong> is also important to successful communication. Good pronunciation is regarded as an indicator of good knowledge of the language by the native speakers. Well pronounced, even errors of aspect don't seem so bad.</p>
<p>Several aspects of pronunciation commonly pose difficulties for learners of Czech as a foreign language. <strong>Long vowels</strong> distinguish meaning in Czech (for instance, <em><strong>b</strong>ý<strong>t</strong></em> vs. <em><strong>b</strong>y<strong>t</strong></em> – to be vs. apartment). The length of the long vowel is approximately double of the short vowel. Stress in Czech is always on the first syllable. Only some prepositions take on the stress. Long vowels are pronounced long even in <strong>unstressed</strong> position (e.g. <strong><em>léta</em></strong> vs. <strong><em>létá –</em></strong> summers vs. he flies).</p>
<p>Another difficult feature are <strong>consonant clusters</strong>. R and l can function as sonant units in Czech syllable. An example can be a popular Czech tongue twister with no vowels <strong><em>Strč prst skrz krk</em></strong> (Put your finger down your throat). Of unique and difficult sounds, Czech boasts <em>ř</em>, basically trilled <strong><em>r</em></strong> which is not in any other language (except for Polish and some dialects of French).</p>
<p>Another class of errors altogether are those of <strong>idioms and vocabulary</strong>. Most of the <em>Top 10 Mistakes</em> are of this origin. Short of listing the most common ones, however, it is impossible to provide an informative classification.</p>
<p>Czech <strong>syntax</strong> is traditionally described as free. It is seen as some compensation for the complex morphology. This apart from being far from the truth, leads to neglecting syntax in Czech textbooks. Among other things, word order in Czech is used to differentiate between given and new information. For instance, <em>Petr viděl Janu</em> and <em>Janu viděl Petr</em>, superficially both meaning <em>Petr saw Jana</em>, both imply a different context. They might be paraphrased as It was Petr who saw Jana and It was Jana whom Petr saw. Which, of course, in English would be considered two very different sentences.</p>
<p>Part of Czech's reputation as a difficult language also comes from the fact that Czech <strong>native speakers</strong> don't have as much experience in dealing with people who are learning the language. While we always appreciate the effort, we may not always be ready to overlook errors and focus on communication. This also had to do with how foreign languages are taught in Czech schools where correctness is often put above communication.</p>
<p>In his book <em>Chrám i tvrz</em> (A Cathedral and a Fortress), Pavel Eisner a famous Czech writer, translator and an excellent philologist wrote that <strong>Czechs are differently sensitive to different kinds of errors</strong>. In speaking about the sensitive and vulnerable points of Czech he wrote:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>“For us, mistakes in <strong>cases and collocations</strong> are very conspicuous but we accept them easily, often laughing, when we realize that the speaker is a foreigner. Mistakes in the complex laws of <strong>aspect</strong>, however, we feel are very awkward although we can admit that it is extremely hard for a non-Czech to master this devilish grammatical complexity of the Czech language. We react most vividly, also, at any deviation from the <strong>vowel length</strong> and the <strong>correct placement of stress</strong>.”</p>
</blockquote>



---

> Section 20 of 22 · Czech Grammar as Czechs Learn It · id `czech-grammar-as-czechs-learn-it` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=czech-grammar-as-czechs-learn-it)

<h1 id="reader-content-czech-grammar-as-czechs-learn-it">Czech Grammar as Czechs Learn It</h1>
<p>This section lists the key terms used to talk about Czech grammar by Czechs. All Czech children will have been taught these terms (although some will remember them less well than others). Knowing these terms will be useful to you when you communicate with your teachers or read Czech textbooks. The Czech terms don’t always match English grammatical terminology perfectly but where equivalents exist, it should be fairly easy to understand how they relate to one another. This section is based on the work of teachers working for US Peace Corps Pre-Service Training in Summer 1996.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-word-classes-parts-of-speech---slovní-druhy">Word classes (parts of speech) - slovní druhy</h2>
<p>There are 10 word classes recognized in the grammar of the Czech language:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-288">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">Nouns</td>
<td align="left">podstatná jména</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Adjectives</td>
<td align="left">přídavná jména</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Pronouns</td>
<td align="left">zájmena</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Numerals</td>
<td align="left">číslovky</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Verbs</td>
<td align="left">slovesa</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Adverbs</td>
<td align="left">příslovce</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Prepositions</td>
<td align="left">předložky</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Conjunctions</td>
<td align="left">spojky</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Particles</td>
<td align="left">částice</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Interjections</td>
<td align="left">citoslovce</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The first 5 classes change their form in different contexts. The changes in 1 – 4 are called <em>declension</em>. The changes in verbs (5) are called conjugation.</p>
<p>Classes 6 – 10 do not change their form (with one minor exception).</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-nouns-podstatná-jména">Nouns – podstatná jména</h2>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-289">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="4" align="left">When we talk about nouns, we indicate their:</td>
<td>CASE</td>
<td>1st - 7th</td>
<td>(pád)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NUMBER</td>
<td>singular or plural</td>
<td>(číslo)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>GENDER</td>
<td>masculine, feminine or neuter</td>
<td>(rod)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>MODEL</td>
<td>pán, muž, čaj, žena, kuře, ...</td>
<td>(vzor)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-290">







<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="7" align="left">These are the names of cases:</td>
<td>nominative</td>
<td><strong>1. pád</strong></td>
<td>1st case</td>
<td>Kdo? Co?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>genitive</td>
<td><strong>2. pád</strong></td>
<td>2nd case</td>
<td>Koho? Čeho?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>dative</td>
<td><strong>3. pád</strong></td>
<td>3rd case</td>
<td>Komu? Čemu?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>accusative</td>
<td><strong>4. pád</strong></td>
<td>4th case</td>
<td>Koho? Co?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>vocative</td>
<td><strong>5. pád</strong></td>
<td>5th case</td>
<td>Kdo!Co!</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>locative</td>
<td><strong>6. pád</strong></td>
<td>6th case</td>
<td>Kom? Čem?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>instrumental</td>
<td><strong>7. pád</strong></td>
<td>7th case</td>
<td>Kým? Čím?</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> In Czech school grammar, cases are not given names but simply numbers and are always presented with the appropriate What/Who question for clarity. Academic grammars use the internationally recognized Latinate names.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-adjectives-přídavná-jména">Adjectives – přídavná jména</h2>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-291">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="4" align="left">When talking about adjectives, we can identify these features:</td>
<td>CASE</td>
<td>as nouns</td>
<td>(pád)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NUMBER</td>
<td>as nouns</td>
<td>(číslo)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>GENDER</td>
<td>as nouns</td>
<td>(rod)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>KIND</td>
<td>hard, soft and possessive</td>
<td>(druh)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-pronouns-zájmena">Pronouns – zájmena</h2>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-292">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="3" align="left">For pronouns, we can identify these features:</td>
<td align="left">CASE</td>
<td align="left">as above</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">NUMBER</td>
<td align="left">as above</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">KIND</td>
<td align="left">personal, possessive, demonstrative, reflexive, relative, interrogative, indefinite and negative</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-293">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="3"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td rowspan="8">Examples of different kinds of pronounce:</td>
<td>PERSONAL</td>
<td>já, ty, on, ona, (ono), my, vy, oni</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>POSSESSIVE</td>
<td>můj, tvůj, jeho, její, ...</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>DEMONSTRATIVE</td>
<td>ten, ta, to, tento, tato, tamta, ...</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>REFLEXIVE</td>
<td>se, si, sám, ...</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RELATIVE</td>
<td>který, jaký, čí, kdo, co (they start a clause)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>INTERROGATIVE</td>
<td>který, jaký, čí, kdo, co (they start a question)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>INDEFINITE</td>
<td>někdo, nějaký, něco, málokdo, ...</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NEGATIVE</td>
<td>nikdo, nic, nijaký, žádný, ničí, ...</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-numerals---číslovky">Numerals - číslovky</h2>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-294">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="2" align="left">When we talk about numerals, we can identify:</td>
<td>CASE</td>
<td>as above</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>KIND</td>
<td>cardinal, ordinal, multiplication and generic</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-295">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="4" align="left">Examples of numerals of different kinds:</td>
<td>CARDINAL</td>
<td>jedna, dva, tři, čtyři, ...</td>
<td>(one, two, …)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>ORDINAL</td>
<td>první, druhy, třetí, ...</td>
<td>(first, second, …)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>MULTIPLICATION</td>
<td>jednou, dvakrát, třikrát, ...</td>
<td>(once, twice, …)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>GENERIC</td>
<td>jedny, dvoje, troje, ...</td>
<td>(a pair of, two pairs of, ...)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-verbs---slovesa">Verbs - slovesa</h2>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-296">






<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="7" align="left">For verbs we can identify these features:</td>
<td>PERSON</td>
<td>1st, 2nd or 3rd</td>
<td>(osoba)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NUMBER</td>
<td>as above</td>
<td>(číslo)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>TENSE</td>
<td>past, present or future</td>
<td>(čas)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>VOICE</td>
<td>active or passive</td>
<td>(rod)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>MOOD</td>
<td>indicative, imperative, conditional</td>
<td>(způsob)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>ASPECT</td>
<td>perfective or imperfective</td>
<td>(vid)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>CATEGORY</td>
<td>1st, 2nd, 3rd or 4th (5th)</td>
<td>(druh)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Here are some examples to give you an idea of what to imagine under verb categories:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-297">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">*<strong>dělám:*</strong> I'm doing</td>
<td align="left">1st person, singular, present, active, imperfective, 2nd category, and indicative mood in the following sentence: <em>Dělám domácí úkol.</em></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">*<strong>nespi (!):*</strong> don't sleep</td>
<td align="left">2nd person, singular, present, active, imperative, imperfective, 4th category</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">**byl postaven:** was built</td>
<td align="left">3rd person, singular, past, passive, perfective, 3rd category, and indicative mood in the following sentence: <em>Tento hrad byl postaven v 11. století.</em> (This castle was built in the 11th century.)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-adverbs---příslovce">Adverbs - příslovce</h2>
<p>Adverbs express various circumstances of events (place = místo, time = čas, reason = důvod, manner = způsob) or grade of quality. They join verbs, adjectives or other adverbs.</p>
<p>Here are some examples of types of adverbs:</p>
<p><em>tady</em> (here - place) <em>brzy</em> (early/soon - time) <em>proto</em> (therefore - reason)</p>
<p><em>rychle</em> (quickly - manner) <em>velmi</em> (very - grade of quality)</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-prepositions---předložky">Prepositions - předložky</h2>
<p>There are many prepositions in the Czech language. After certain prepositions we use certain cases of nouns. Sometimes the case you use changes the meaning of the preposition.</p>
<p>These prepositions take only one case:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-298">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">od, do, z(ze), u, blízko, vedle, kromě, bez</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Koho?Čeho?</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">naproti, k(ke), proti</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Komu?Čemu?</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">pro, přes, skrz, mimo</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Koho?Co?</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">po, o, v (ve)</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Kom?Čem?</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">s (se)</td>
<td align="left"><strong>Kým?Čím?</strong></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>These prepositions take two different cases to express different meanings:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-299">





<thead>
<tr>
<th></th>
<th align="left">Kde?</th>
<th align="left">Kam?</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Na</td>
<td align="left">Kom?Čem?</td>
<td align="left">Koho?Co?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>nad, mezi, pod, před, za</td>
<td align="left">Kým?Čím?</td>
<td align="left">Koho?Co?</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Czech prepositions, unlike English, are always followed by other words - they never occur at the end of a sentence.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-300">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Dům, o kterém jsem mluvil, ...</td>
<td align="left">The house I talked about ...</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Na koho čekáš?</td>
<td align="left">Who are you waiting for?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Je těžké s ním pracovat.</td>
<td align="left">He is difficult to work with.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Moreover, unlike English, there are no “prepositional adverbs” in Czech.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-301">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Od té doby jsem tam nebyl.</td>
<td align="left">I haven't been there since.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Nikdo v domě není.</td>
<td align="left">There is nobody in.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-conjunctions---spojky">Conjunctions - spojky</h2>
<p>They connect clauses, or words in sentences. Here are some common conjunctions:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-302">



<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="center">a, i, nebo, ale, když, protože, že, kdyby</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Note</strong> that Czech prepositions (with the notable exception of <strong><em>a</em></strong>) are always preceded by a comma.</p>
<p>In Czech, you always have to use the conjunction <strong><em>že</em></strong> for <strong><em>that</em></strong> in the following sentences:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-303">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><em>Myslím si, že to vím.</em></td>
<td align="left">I think (that) I know it.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><em>Vím, že mám pravdu.</em></td>
<td align="left">I know (that) I am right.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-particles---částice">Particles - částice</h2>
<p>They usually start a sentence and they indicate the kind of the sentence question, command, exclamation. They often express wishes.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-304">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left"><em>Ať žije královna!</em></td>
<td align="left">Long live the Queen!</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left"><em>Kéž by pršelo!</em></td>
<td align="left">I wish it rained!</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2 id="reader-content-interjections---citoslovce">Interjections - citoslovce</h2>
<p>They express moods, emotions and will. They point to someone or something, or mark voices or noise.</p>
<p>ach (oh), au (ouch), br (sound make to indicate a feeling of cold), fuj (yuck), bác (bam), bim – bam (sound indicating bell tolling), haf (woof), ...</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-word-order---slovosled-syntax">Word order - Slovosled (Syntax)</h2>
<p>The Czech sentence (or clause) is described as having two basic parts:</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-305">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">subject</td>
<td align="left">predicate (verb)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">podmět</td>
<td align="left">Přísudek</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Petr</td>
<td align="left">čte.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Petr</td>
<td align="left">reads.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>This core can be extended by an object (předmět):</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-306">





<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">subject</td>
<td align="left">predicate (verb)</td>
<td align="left">object</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">podmět</td>
<td align="left">Přísudek</td>
<td align="left">předmět</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Petr</td>
<td align="left">Čte</td>
<td align="left">knihu.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Petr</td>
<td align="left">Reads</td>
<td align="left">a book.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Each of these core parts of the sentence (<em>větné členy</em>) can be further elaborated by adding adjectives and/or possessive pronouns to the nouns, and adverbs and/or prepositional phrases to the verbs.</p>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-307">









<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">attribute</td>
<td align="left">subject</td>
<td align="left">predicate</td>
<td align="left">adverbial</td>
<td align="left">attribute</td>
<td align="left">object</td>
<td align="left">adverbial</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">přívlastek</td>
<td align="left">podmět</td>
<td align="left">přísudek</td>
<td align="left">příslovečné určení</td>
<td align="left">přívlastek</td>
<td align="left">předmět</td>
<td align="left">příslovečné určení</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Náš</td>
<td align="left">Petr</td>
<td align="left">čte</td>
<td align="left">rychle</td>
<td align="left">hezkou</td>
<td align="left">knihu</td>
<td align="left">v parku.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left">Our</td>
<td align="left">Petr</td>
<td align="left">reads</td>
<td align="left">quickly</td>
<td align="left">a nice</td>
<td align="left">book</td>
<td align="left">in the park.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<table id="reader-content-czech-table-308">




<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left">!</td>
<td align="left">The actual order of the parts of the sentence is comparatively free. The following order would also be possible: <em>Náš Petr čte v parku rychle hezkou knihu.</em> (stressing the <em>hezkou knihu</em>). The order of words in a sentence is used to emphasize certain words. The words that are placed towards the end of the sentence are emphasized.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>



---

> Section 21 of 22 · Acknowledgements and licensing details · id `acknowledgements-and-licensing-details` · [read in the reader](https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/?book=czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate&section=acknowledgements-and-licensing-details)

<h1 id="reader-content-acknowledgements-and-licensing-details">Acknowledgements and licensing details</h1>
<h2 id="reader-content-origins-of-the-navigator">Origins of the Navigator</h2>
<p>The Czech Language Navigator as a whole is an original creation based on materials developed for the US Peace Corps language program in the mid 1990s (referred to in previous editions as the Yellow Pages of the Czech Language). The Navigator adds to the cumulative grammatical knowledge of many generations.</p>
<p>It is impossible to compile an entire grammar book completely from scratch. The information in this Navigator is based on the reframing of information from reference materials too numerous to list. Some portions of this text contain original analysis (most significantly aspect) and all explanations are original. But the main contribution is in the compilation. Many people contributed to the original drafts over a period of several years.</p>
<p>Some of the sections were composed as separate handouts over many years of teaching and therefore, there is a certain inconsistency of presentation and tone of language. It is possible that this also resulted in some small portions of the text being copied from elsewhere without attribution as is common in the practice of busy teachers putting together handouts. We tried to replace all of these with original work but some may have been overlooked. We will be happy to remove these when pointed out or provide attribution.</p>
<h2 id="reader-content-licensing-and-reuse">Licensing and reuse</h2>
<p>This work is licensed under Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-ShareAlike 4.0 International. Under Commercial use we understand print for profit but not print distribution that charges for the cost of print and delivery. Neither do we consider commercial use using this work as a textbook in commercial courses, provided students are not charged for the text beyond the cost of reproduction.</p>
<p>We welcome and encourage the following reuse:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Corrections of errors and misprints (because of the frequent switching between Czech and English</p></li>
<li><p>Alternative formatting of charts and tables</p></li>
<li><p>Replacing or adding examples</p></li>
<li><p>Clarifying exisitng or adding alternative explanations</p></li>
<li><p>Adding new sections</p></li>
<li><p>Translation of the English into other languages</p></li>
<li><p>Transforming the content into other formats, including interactive apps</p></li>
</ul>
<p>We have learned over many years that there is no one way to present grammar that will suit all learners and teachers alike. This reference is intended to be as complete a starting point as possible but does not claim to be the ultimate book.</p>



---

<p>Nouns <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-nouns">6</a></p>
<p>Gender <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-gender">6</a></p>
<p>What is gender? <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-what-is-gender">6</a></p>
<p>Summary of agreement rules! <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-summary-of-agreement-rules">7</a></p>
<p>How to tell the gender of a noun <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-how-to-tell-the-gender-of-a-noun">7</a></p>
<p>There are some which are almost exception proof: <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-there-are-some-which-are-almost-exception-proof">7</a></p>
<p>The rest are shared by two genders about 50/50: <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-the-rest-are-shared-by-two-genders-about-5050">7</a></p>
<p>Case <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-case">8</a></p>
<p>What is case? <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-what-is-case">8</a></p>
<p>What are the functions of Czech cases? <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-what-are-the-functions-of-czech-cases">8</a></p>
<p>Summary of Czech cases <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-summary-of-czech-cases">9</a></p>
<p>Models <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-xx-models">10</a></p>
<p>Kdo? Co? - Nominative - 1. pád - 1<sup>st</sup> case <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-kdo-co---nominative---1.-pád---1st-case">10</a></p>
<p>Function <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-function">10</a></p>
<p>Form <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-form">11</a></p>
<p>Exceptions in the plural <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-exceptions-in-the-plural">11</a></p>
<p>Koho? Co? - Accusative - 4. pád - 4<sup>th</sup> case <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-koho-co---accusative---4.-pád---4th-case">11</a></p>
<p>Function <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-function-1">11</a></p>
<p>Form <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-form-1">11</a></p>
<p>Most common verbs <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-most-common-verbs">12</a></p>
<p>Most common prepositions <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-most-common-prepositions">12</a></p>
<p>Koho? Čeho? - Genitive - 2. pád - 2<sup>nd</sup> case <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-koho-čeho---genitive---2.-pád---2nd-case">12</a></p>
<p>Function <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-function-2">12</a></p>
<p>Form <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-form-2">12</a></p>
<p>Singular <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-singular">13</a></p>
<p>Plural <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-plural">13</a></p>
<p>Most common verbs <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-most-common-verbs-1">13</a></p>
<p>Most common prepositions <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-most-common-prepositions-1">13</a></p>
<p>Typical categories of words <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-typical-categories-of-words">13</a></p>
<p>Komu? Čemu? - Dative - 3. pád - 3<sup>rd</sup> case <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-komu-čemu---dative---3.-pád---3rd-case">13</a></p>
<p>Function <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-function-3">13</a></p>
<p>Form <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-form-3">14</a></p>
<p>Most common verbs <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-most-common-verbs-2">14</a></p>
<p>Most common prepositions <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-most-common-prepositions-2">14</a></p>
<p>(o) Kom? Čem? - Locative - 6. pád - 6<sup>th</sup> case <a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/nouns.md#reader-content-o-kom-čem---locative---6.-pád---6th-case">14</a></p>

<section id="reader-content-footnotes" class="footnotes footnotes-end-of-document">
<hr>
<ol>
<li id="reader-content-fn1"><p>Voicedness is a feature of consonants which is present in next to all languages. A consonant is <em>voiced</em> if you produce a vibration in your larynx while pronouncing it e.g. <em>v</em>, and <em>unvoiced</em> if you don't e.g. <em>f</em>. Voiced and unvoiced consonants are pared together. See the <em>How to pronounce</em> section for recorded examples.<a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/pronunciation.md#reader-content-fnref1" class="footnote-back">↩︎</a></p></li>
<li id="reader-content-fn2"><p>For which English only has <em>you all</em>, or <em>you guys</em>.<a href="https://czechnavigator.dominiklukes.net/agent/czech-language-navigator-reference-candidate/chapters/czech-trivia.md#reader-content-fnref2" class="footnote-back">↩︎</a></p></li>
</ol>
</section>
